Congressional Research Service analyses have documented that the number of independent hog farms in the United States declined from more than 660,000 in 1980 to fewer than 67,000 by 2012, while total hog production increased over the same period.14 The consolidation was not the elimination of pork production. It was the elimination of the people who had been doing it, replaced by a smaller number of larger-scale contract operations producing more animals at lower margins under tighter corporate control. The social cost of that consolidation has been documented by rural sociologists for decades, and the industry itself does not seriously contest it. It shows up in rural depopulation, in the loss of small-town economic activity, and in the civic infrastructure that contracts when the people who supported it leave.

The social axis scores net negative: the system's benefits flow primarily to consumers in the form of cheap protein and to the corporate entities that control the processing and distribution chain; its costs are concentrated in workers, in adjacent communities, and in the rural regions whose agricultural economies the system has restructured.

Environmental Integrity

The environmental axis is where the industrial feedlot system's costs are most extensively documented and least disputed.

Waste management is the entry point. The EPA's regulatory definition of a large CAFO, an animal feeding operation confining more than 1,000 animal units at 1,000 pounds each, means that a single large cattle feedlot can produce waste volumes equivalent to a small city. The USDA estimated in 2009 that confined animal operations in the United States produce approximately 500 million tons of manure annually, roughly three times the volume of human sanitary waste produced by the entire US population.15 That waste is typically stored in anaerobic lagoons or applied to surrounding cropland at rates that frequently exceed the land's absorptive capacity for nitrogen and phosphorus. When the application exceeds the capacity, the surplus runs off into surface water.

The water quality consequences are measurable at continental scale. Nutrient runoff from agricultural operations, of which confined animal waste is a leading component, is the primary driver of the hypoxic zone in the Gulf of Mexico, a seasonal dead zone that covered approximately 6,334 square miles in 2024 according to NOAA survey data, making it one of the largest areas of oxygen-depleted water in the world.16 The Chesapeake Bay, closer to the concentrated poultry production of the Delmarva Peninsula, has experienced a parallel pattern of nutrient-driven hypoxia that has degraded its fisheries for decades.

The numbers are real. What they describe is also visible. Louisiana shrimpers working the waters off the Atchafalaya Delta have watched the dead zone's expansion reshape their industry over three decades. In peak hypoxia years — midsummer, after the spring flush of nitrogen from the Mississippi watershed reaches the Gulf — trawlers that once worked nearshore waters are pushed miles further out, burning more diesel to reach the oxygenated water where the shrimp still congregate. The catch does not disappear. It migrates, and the boats follow it, and the economics tighten. In 2023, the dock-side price for Gulf brown shrimp hit its lowest point in over a decade, driven in part by import competition but compounded by the operational costs of fishing around a dead zone the size of Connecticut. The hypoxic zone is not an abstraction measured only by NOAA research vessels. It is a feature of the working landscape that the people who fish the Gulf navigate around every season, and its growth tracks the nutrient load from the agricultural system upstream with a fidelity that the science has been documenting since the early 1990s.

Greenhouse gas emissions from confined animal production are significant in the global accounting. The FAO's widely cited 2013 assessment estimated that global livestock supply chains contribute approximately 14.5 percent of all anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions, with the confined production segment contributing disproportionately through enteric methane from cattle, nitrous oxide from manure management, and carbon dioxide from feed crop production and transportation.17 The feedlot system, specifically, contributes on the production side through methane from enteric fermentation and manure storage, and on the input side through the full emissions profile of the commodity grain system that feeds it: the diesel for planting, harvesting, and transporting the corn, the natural gas for synthesizing the nitrogen fertilizer the corn requires, and the emissions from the land-use practices under which the corn is grown.

The system sequesters no carbon. Feedlot pens are bare dirt or concrete. The land on which the animals stand contributes nothing to soil carbon accumulation. The land from which the feed is grown, under annual row-crop cultivation with conventional tillage, is losing soil carbon rather than gaining it, as the next section documents.

The environmental axis scores net negative across every metric the framework measures: water pollution, greenhouse gas emissions, soil degradation via feed production, and zero contribution to carbon sequestration.

Survivability

The survivability axis asks what the system requires to keep functioning and what happens when those requirements are not met. For industrial feedlot production, the dependency profile is broad and deep.

The system requires continuous commodity grain inputs. A single large cattle feedlot finishing 30,000 head per year requires, at roughly six to eight pounds of grain per pound of weight gain over the finishing period, millions of pounds of corn annually.18 That corn must be available, at a price the feedlot can absorb, delivered on a schedule that matches the metabolic demands of the animals currently on feed. A disruption to the corn supply — drought, export-market competition, ethanol-mandate pressure on available supply — translates directly and immediately into financial stress on the feedlot.

The system requires continuous federal subsidies for its feed inputs. As the economic section documented, below-cost commodity grain has been the structural condition of the feedlot's profitability for decades. Without the commodity-support system, feed prices would reflect the full cost of production, and the feedlot's margins, already thin in most years, would narrow further or invert. The policy structure is the floor the system stands on.

The system requires continuous diesel for transportation. Commodity grain moves from the Corn Belt to feedlots across the country by truck, rail, and barge. Finished animals move from feedlots to a small number of large processing facilities. Processed meat moves from those facilities to distribution centers and retail outlets nationwide. Each link in this chain requires petroleum-derived fuel, and the chain is long. A sustained increase in diesel prices propagates through every link.

The system requires continuous antibiotics for animal health at the stocking densities the system operates at. Confining thousands of animals in close quarters at the stress levels inherent to the feedlot environment creates disease pressure that sub-therapeutic antibiotic administration manages. Remove the antibiotics and the disease pressure reasserts itself at densities that make clinical treatment of individual animals economically unfeasible.

Remove any one of these requirements — grain supply, federal subsidies, diesel, antibiotics — and the system cannot continue to function at its current scale and cost structure. This is not a prediction of imminent failure. It is a description of the system's dependency profile. The system operates successfully because all four of these conditions are currently being met. Its survivability score reflects what happens when any one of them is not.

The survivability axis scores very low, not because the system is poorly managed, but because the system's design requires continuous external inputs that are historically contingent, geopolitically exposed, and, in the case of climate-dependent grain supply, increasingly volatile.

Industrial Grain Monoculture

The second system is, in a structural sense, the first system's substrate. Industrial grain monoculture — the large-scale, chemically intensive production of corn, soybeans, and wheat that dominates the American agricultural landscape — supplies the feed for the feedlot system, the caloric base for most processed food, and the raw material for the ethanol industry. It covers roughly 250 million acres of US cropland, and the three crops together account for the majority of planted acreage in the country.19

Economic Viability

The economic picture for industrial grain monoculture mirrors the feedlot's in structure: positive at the point of sale, substantially different when externalities are accounted.

For centralized producers — the large-scale operations with enough acreage to capture economies of scale in equipment, input purchasing, and commodity marketing — the system is profitable in most years, though the margins are sensitive to commodity prices, input costs, and the federal support structure. The farm bill's commodity support programs — direct payments, counter-cyclical payments, crop insurance subsidies, and their successive reformulations — have provided a floor under commodity grain profitability for decades, smoothing the volatility that would otherwise make large-scale monoculture economically unsustainable in low-price years.20

For family-scale operators and local economies, the picture is different. The consolidation of US grain production has followed the same trajectory as livestock production: fewer, larger operations producing more output. The number of farms in the United States has declined from approximately 6.8 million in 1935 to roughly 2 million today, while average farm size has more than tripled, and the majority of production value is now concentrated in a small fraction of operations.21 The economic benefits of the system flow disproportionately to the largest operators and to the input suppliers, processors, and commodity traders that the system depends on. Rural communities in the Corn Belt and the wheat-growing regions of the Plains have experienced the same pattern of depopulation, retail contraction, and civic thinning that the livestock consolidation produced in poultry- and pork-producing regions.

The system delivers cheap calories to urban consumers. The calories come from corn syrup, soybean oil, and refined wheat flour, and their prices have kept food expenditure as a share of household income at historically low levels. This is a real benefit to household budgets. It is also a benefit whose costs appear elsewhere: in the healthcare system, where diet-related chronic disease driven in part by the overconsumption of cheap refined carbohydrates and seed oils imposes costs that the National Institutes of Health has estimated at hundreds of billions of dollars annually; in the environmental costs documented below; and in the rural economic hollowing the consolidation has produced.22

Social Equity

The social axis for grain monoculture is structurally similar to the feedlot case: the system's benefits are distributed broadly to consumers in the form of cheap food, and its costs are concentrated in the communities where the production occurs.

Rural communities in intensive monoculture regions bear the environmental health burden: pesticide exposure, nitrate contamination of drinking water, respiratory effects from dust and chemical drift. They bear it without capturing a proportional share of the economic value the production generates. The value is captured at the processing and distribution stages, which are physically and economically distant from the fields. Farm labor in large-scale grain operations has shifted from a human workforce to a mechanical one; the tractor, the combine, and the chemical application system have replaced the hands, and the people those hands belonged to have, for the most part, moved elsewhere.

The displacement narrative for grain monoculture is quieter than for livestock. There are no CAFO siting controversies, no concentrated processing facilities with high-visibility labor conditions. The underlying structural change is the same. Fewer people are needed to produce more grain, and the communities that were organized around the people are contracting as the people leave.

Environmental Integrity

The environmental costs of industrial grain monoculture are extensive, well documented, and, in some dimensions, cumulative in ways that compound across decades.

Topsoil loss is the foundational concern. David Pimentel, whose career at Cornell established much of the empirical basis for energy-input analysis in agriculture, estimated that US cropland loses topsoil at an average rate of roughly ten times the natural soil-formation rate, with annual erosion rates on conventionally tilled cropland averaging approximately 1.8 billion tons across the country.23 The Natural Resources Conservation Service's more recent modeling using the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation places the figure at roughly four to five tons per acre per year on average for conventionally managed cropland, against a natural formation rate of roughly half a ton per acre per year.24 The loss is quiet. It does not produce dramatic events in most years. It produces a slow, cumulative thinning of the soil horizon that reduces the land's productive capacity over generational timescales. The soil that took centuries to build is being removed in decades.

Biodiversity reduction is a direct consequence of the monoculture model. A cornfield is, by design, one species of plant over a very large area. The organisms that would normally occupy the ecological niches of a diverse plant community — the insects, the birds, the soil microbiota, the mycorrhizal fungi — are either absent or suppressed by the chemical regime required to maintain the monoculture. Tilman and colleagues' landmark 2002 paper in Nature documented that intensification of agricultural production practices was the single largest driver of biodiversity loss in terrestrial ecosystems globally, operating through habitat conversion, chemical inputs, and the simplification of plant communities from diverse assemblages to single-species stands.25

Groundwater contamination from nitrogen fertilizer and pesticide leaching is a chronic and geographically extensive problem. Nitrate contamination of drinking water from agricultural sources affects millions of Americans, particularly in rural areas overlying the shallow aquifers of the Corn Belt and the Great Plains. The USGS National Water-Quality Assessment has documented nitrate concentrations exceeding the EPA's maximum contaminant level in a significant fraction of shallow groundwater wells in agricultural areas, with the highest concentrations correlated with the most intensive corn and soybean production.26

Mycorrhizal damage from fungicide application is a less visible but ecologically significant consequence. Mycorrhizal fungi, the symbiotic networks that connect plant root systems to soil nutrient pools and to each other, are suppressed by the fungicide applications that modern grain production relies on for disease management. The disruption of mycorrhizal networks degrades the soil's capacity to cycle nutrients, retain water, and support the biological activity that builds soil organic matter over time. The consequence is a system that becomes progressively more dependent on synthetic inputs to replace the biological functions it has disabled, a dependency spiral that the environmental literature has documented across multiple cropping systems.27

Nutrient runoff from grain monoculture is the leading cause of non-point source water pollution in the United States. Synthetic fertilizer is applied at rates calibrated to maximize yield in an average year, which means applied in excess of what the crop uses in any below-average year. The nitrogen and phosphorus that the crops do not absorb moves through the soil profile into groundwater and across the surface into streams, rivers, and eventually into the coastal waters where it drives the eutrophication and hypoxia described in the feedlot section. Foley and colleagues, in their 2011 Nature paper surveying the environmental footprint of global agriculture, identified nutrient management in intensive grain systems as one of the highest-priority leverage points for reducing agriculture's environmental impact.28

The environmental axis scores net negative: topsoil loss, biodiversity reduction, groundwater contamination, mycorrhizal disruption, and nutrient runoff as the nation's leading non-point source water pollutant.

Survivability

The survivability axis for grain monoculture reveals a dependency profile that is structurally parallel to the feedlot's.

The system requires continuous chemical inputs. Synthetic nitrogen fertilizer, produced from natural gas through the Haber-Bosch process, is the single largest energy input in grain production. Pimentel and Pimentel's analysis in Food, Energy, and Society documented that roughly forty percent of the total energy input in corn production is accounted for by nitrogen fertilizer alone.29 Without it, corn yields fall to a fraction of their current levels, because the monoculture regime has depleted the biological nitrogen-fixation capacity that diverse systems maintain through legume rotation and mycorrhizal function. The system has substituted a chemical input for a biological process, and the substitution is not reversible on a short timeline.

The system requires continuous fossil fuel for field operations and irrigation. Every pass of the tractor — planting, cultivating, applying chemicals, harvesting — requires diesel. In the western Corn Belt and the Great Plains, where rainfall is insufficient for reliable yields, irrigation from the Ogallala Aquifer and other groundwater sources requires energy for pumping, and the Ogallala itself is being drawn down at rates that exceed recharge in much of its extent, making the water supply a diminishing resource rather than a renewable one.30

The Ogallala drawdown is worth pausing on, because it is the survivability axis made physically legible. The aquifer underlies approximately 174,000 square miles across eight states from South Dakota to the Texas Panhandle, and it accumulated its water over millions of years, recharged slowly by precipitation filtering through the High Plains soils above it. In southwestern Kansas — Grant, Stanton, and Haskell counties, the heart of the irrigated corn and feedlot corridor — the saturated thickness of the aquifer has declined by more than sixty percent since large-scale pumping began in the 1950s. The Kansas Geological Survey has documented wells that produced reliably for decades going dry or dropping below the threshold at which irrigation is economically viable, and the projection for much of the southwestern Kansas portion of the aquifer is functional depletion within a generation.[^30a] This is not a speculative scenario. It is a measurement. Farmers in the region can tell you the rate their water table is dropping; many of them track it year to year. The arithmetic is not ambiguous. The system that depends on this water is drawing down its supply faster than geology can replace it, and when the water is gone, the irrigated agriculture it supports goes with it. The dependency the survivability axis measures is, in this case, a physical quantity with a known depletion rate and a calculable endpoint.

The system requires continuous federal subsidies. Commodity grain production in the United States has operated under federal price support, crop insurance subsidy, and conservation-compliance payment structures for decades. These programs smooth the volatility of commodity markets and keep production profitable in years when market prices fall below the cost of production. Without the subsidy structure, the marginal operations, those farming the least productive land at the highest per-acre cost, would exit production, and the system's total output would contract.

The system requires continuous favorable climate for predictable yields. The cultivars planted across the Corn Belt are selected for performance under a specific range of temperature, moisture, and growing-season conditions. Deviations from the expected range produce yield losses that propagate through the entire commodity system. The 2012 drought reduced the national corn yield by roughly twenty-seven percent in a single season.31 Climate projections for the central United States indicate increasing frequency and severity of growing-season heat stress and drought stress over the coming decades, which means the climate condition the system was optimized for is itself becoming less reliable.32

Remove any one of these — chemical inputs, fossil fuel, federal subsidies, favorable climate — and the system cannot continue to produce at its current scale and cost structure. As with the feedlot system, this is not a prediction of when the disruption occurs. It is a mapping of the dependency profile. The system's survivability score reflects the breadth and depth of its dependencies, each of which is contingent on conditions that are volatile, contested, or trending in directions unfavorable to the system's continuation in its current form.

The Improvement Acknowledgment

I want to register something that the axis-by-axis scoring, taken alone, might obscure.

Industrial grain monoculture is not a fixed technology. It is a set of practices, and the practices are changing. Cover cropping means planting a secondary crop between cash-crop seasons to hold soil, fix nitrogen, and maintain mycorrhizal networks. The practice has expanded substantially in the United States over the last decade, and the evidence for its benefits across multiple dimensions is strong and growing. The USDA's Census of Agriculture reported that cover crop acreage in the US increased from roughly 10 million acres in 2012 to over 18 million acres by 2022, a pace of adoption that suggests the practice is moving from the margins of the system toward something closer to standard practice in progressive operations.33 Reduced tillage and no-till systems, which leave crop residue on the soil surface rather than turning it under, reduce erosion rates dramatically and improve soil biological activity. Integration of perennial vegetation into row-crop systems — buffer strips, alley cropping, silvopasture adjacent to grain fields — can restore some of the habitat complexity and biological function that pure monoculture eliminates. Precision agriculture, variable-rate application of inputs guided by GPS mapping and soil sensing, can reduce fertilizer and pesticide use per unit of output by applying inputs where the crop needs them rather than uniformly across the field.

These improvements are real, and they are meaningful. Gabe Brown's operation near Bismarck, North Dakota, is the case that cover-crop advocates cite most often, and the case is worth citing because the numbers are specific. Brown transitioned a 5,000-acre grain and livestock operation from conventional tillage and continuous monoculture to a no-till, diverse cover-crop, integrated-grazing system over roughly two decades. His soil organic matter measurements, documented in NRCS technical visits and in Brown's own published accounting,34 rose from less than two percent to over six percent on fields that had been under the system longest. His input costs dropped substantially: no synthetic fertilizer purchases, no fungicide, no insecticide, herbicide use reduced to a fraction of the county average. His water infiltration rates, a direct measure of soil biological health, increased severalfold, meaning his fields absorb rainfall that would run off a conventionally managed field carrying topsoil and nitrogen with it. The operation is profitable, and the profitability does not depend on commodity support payments in the way his neighbors' operations do, because his input costs are low enough that the margin holds without the subsidy floor.35

Brown's case is not typical. It is a best-case demonstration of what the improving trajectory produces when a skilled operator commits to the transition fully. But it establishes that a grain monoculture operated with winter cover cropping, no-till practices, precision nutrient application, and integrated perennial buffer strips would score materially better on the environmental axis than the same acreage operated under conventional practices. It would build soil rather than lose it, support biological activity rather than suppress it, and reduce nutrient runoff rather than contribute to it. The practices exist, they work, and their adoption is expanding.

The book does not present monocultures as categorically destructive. It presents the current dominant industrial implementation, the version of monoculture that operates across most of the 250 million acres of US cropland without these improvements, as scoring poorly on the four-axis framework for specific, structural reasons. The distinction matters because the structural analysis and the technological trajectory are both true at the same time. The dominant implementation has the vulnerabilities the framework identifies. The improving implementation has the potential the evidence supports. Both findings are accurate. They describe different populations of practice on the same landscape.

What the improvement trajectory does not resolve, however, is the survivability question. A cover-cropped, no-till, precision-managed corn operation is still a corn operation. It still requires seed, fertilizer, and diesel — in reduced quantities, but still required — and a climate regime compatible with corn production. It still sells into a commodity market whose price is shaped by global supply and demand and by federal policy. The improvements narrow the dependency profile. They reduce the system's dependence on any single input by making the system more biologically self-sustaining. But they do not eliminate the profile. A system that depends on fewer external inputs is more survivable than one that depends on more, and the improving monoculture is more survivable than the conventional one. It is not, by the framework's measure, as survivable as a system that depends on very few external inputs and can tolerate the loss of any one of them without collapsing. The improvement is real. The structural constraint remains.

What the Scoring Shows

Two systems, eight axis scores, one pattern.

Industrial feedlot animal production and industrial grain monoculture both score positively on the economic axis at the point of sale, and both score negatively when externalized costs are accounted. Both score net negative on the social axis, with benefits flowing to consumers and corporate entities and costs concentrated in workers, adjacent communities, and the rural regions whose economies the systems have restructured. Both score net negative on the environmental axis across essentially every metric. Both score very low on the survivability axis, with dependency profiles that are broad, deep, and contingent on conditions that are volatile.

The pattern is structural rather than incidental. The two systems were not designed by malevolent architects. They evolved through seventy years of economic incentive, agricultural policy, technological development, and consumer demand, each reinforcing the others, producing a food system that is extraordinarily efficient at converting fossil energy and federal dollars into cheap calories under stable conditions. That efficiency is real. It feeds people. The framework does not deny this. What the framework adds is the observation that the efficiency is built on a specific set of conditions, and that the conditions are not guaranteed.

The question the scoring raises is whether an alternative exists that escapes these structural constraints: a food system that scores well on the first three axes without depending on the specific set of external conditions that make the industrial system's performance possible. The next chapter takes up that question, and the answer it finds is instructive in a way I did not expect when I began this work. The leading alternative — regenerative agriculture, holistic management, the pasture-based model — scores substantially better than industrial production on every axis. It also scores better on the survivability axis, which is the axis where the industrial system is most vulnerable. But it does not score as well on survivability as its advocates might hope, and the reason it does not is a structural one that the framework makes visible in a way that three-axis assessment cannot.

The industrial system's survivability deficit is the floor. The question is what can be built above it.

CHAPTER SIX

Pasture, Honestly

◆ ◆ ◆

Joel Salatin's Polyface Farm sits in the Shenandoah Valley of Virginia, on about five hundred and fifty acres of rolling pasture, woodlot, and pond that have been in his family since the early 1960s. The operation is the most publicly described small farm in the United States. A steady stream of agricultural interns, journalists, documentary crews, and Polyface's own mail-order customers have made the farm into the de facto reference site for what has come to be called, interchangeably, regenerative agriculture, holistic management, or pasture-based farming. Salatin has earned that attention. By his own published accounting, the soil organic matter on Polyface rose from roughly one and a half percent when his parents bought the land in the early 1960s to around eight percent by the time he was writing You Can Farm at the end of that century, against a continental cropland average closer to one to three percent.1 That is not a trivial gain. An inch of topsoil takes a century or more to form under conventional agriculture. Polyface has built measurable inches within a human career.

Nothing in this chapter should be read as denying that it is working. The previous chapter applied the four-axis framework, economic, social, environmental, survivability, to the two dominant industrial systems and watched them score poorly on each axis, for reasons that were structural rather than incidental. The obvious next question is whether the leading alternative scores better. It does. By a substantial margin, on the axes where it is easiest to measure, it does. The harder question, which is what this chapter is actually about, is whether scoring better is the same thing as escaping the structure. I have come to think it is not, and I have come to think this in a way that takes Salatin seriously rather than the other way.

What the Work Actually Achieves

The case for regenerative agriculture, as a practice rather than a brand, is strongest when it is presented specifically. I want to take the time to present it specifically before anything else.

Start with soil. The conventional row-crop regime in the United States has been losing topsoil for a century. USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service modeling using the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation places average annual erosion rates across conventionally managed cropland at roughly four to five tons per acre, against natural soil-formation rates of a half ton or so. The result, on aggregate, is the loss of something on the order of ninety centimeters of topsoil across the major US cropping regions since the start of European farming on the continent.2 Rotationally grazed pasture, by contrast, does not erode at anything like that rate. Machner and colleagues, reviewing long-term grazing trials across the southern and central United States, reported that well-managed perennial pasture under adaptive multi-paddock grazing can build soil organic carbon at rates on the order of 0.3 to 1 ton of carbon per hectare per year in the upper soil horizon over multi-decadal time frames, in a system from which the ruminants are simultaneously producing meat.3 The soil carbon and the meat are not trading against each other. They are being produced together.

The animal-welfare comparison is not close. A broiler chicken in a conventional confined house lives thirty-five to forty-two days in a structure whose stocking density is regulated, in the United States, by the industry's own standards rather than by federal law.4 The bird typically cannot turn around without stepping on another bird and cannot exhibit most of its species-typical behaviors at all. A broiler on pasture lives in daylight on grass, in a covered mobile structure moved once a day, with access to insects, seeds, and whatever small animals it can catch. The life is shorter than wild chickens' lives and is still ended by slaughter, but within the window of the bird's life the quality of that life is, by any reasonable behavioral measure, incomparable. The same comparison holds for laying hens, for pastured pigs, and for cattle, though with different gradients. Confined animal feeding operations have imposed a kind of welfare compression that has no real historical precedent in Western agriculture, and pasture-based systems do not operate at that compression. On the welfare axis, the difference is not a matter of degree but of kind.

The operation's internal design deserves credit on its own terms as well. Polyface's signature move, and the move most imitated across the pastured-livestock literature, is the integration of species in rotation across the same ground. Cattle graze first. The pasture is then rested for a set interval, during which the cow manure ages and fly larvae develop in the pats. The broilers follow in mobile field shelters, eating the larvae and other insects mobilized by the cattle, adding their own manure to the surface, and continuing to be moved forward. Laying hens, in a separate mobile structure Salatin calls the "eggmobile," circulate through the same ground in a slightly different phase of the rotation, completing the fly-cycle disruption and contributing a different manure-deposition pattern. Pigs are used elsewhere on the operation for targeted tillage and forest-floor disturbance. The design moves biological work: pest control, manure distribution, vegetation management, nutrient cycling, from purchased inputs and mechanical operations into the animals themselves. It is a serious piece of agroecological engineering — and it has earned the attention it has received. Any fair assessment of the operation starts by acknowledging that the integration works and works well within its own perimeter.

The rural economic picture is similar in structure. A typical industrial broiler contract puts the grower in capital debt to a vertically integrated processor, with most of the margin on the bird captured at processing and distribution.5 The grower absorbs the capital cost, the environmental externalities, and the labor; the processor absorbs most of the profit. A pasture-based operation at Polyface's scale, by contrast, captures significantly more of the value of the bird on the farm itself. Salatin has argued for decades that rural economic renewal runs through the direct-marketed farm rather than through the contract-grower arrangement, and he is right about this as a matter of basic accounting. Where pasture-based operations exist at scale in a region, they support local processing infrastructure, farm-to-consumer distribution networks, agritourism, and a retained agricultural workforce that the contract system has hollowed out. That hollowing has been measurable for decades. The rural sociology literature, some of the longest-running sociological work on American agriculture, has documented across fifty years of comparative studies beginning with Walter Goldschmidt's original comparisons of Arvin and Dinuba in the 1940s that the consolidation of US livestock and commodity production has coincided with measurable declines in rural population, retail employment, small-town median income, and civic participation across regions where the transition from diversified to contract production has been most complete.6 The pasture-based operation is working against that curve. The people Polyface employs live in the Shenandoah Valley. The money Polyface earns is spent in the Shenandoah Valley. The operation is not extracting value from its region toward a distant headquarters — which is what the contract model does structurally.

The carbon case for well-managed grazing, finally, is genuine. This is not the same thing as saying it is sufficient. Richard Teague and colleagues argued in a 2016 paper in the Journal of Soil and Water Conservation that adaptive multi-paddock grazing, if scaled across existing North American grasslands, could plausibly offset a meaningful fraction of North American agriculture's greenhouse gas footprint through soil carbon accumulation and reduced reliance on synthetic nitrogen fertilizer.7 The Teague paper is the most forceful peer-reviewed articulation of the climate case for regenerative grazing, and I take it seriously. I will come back to its limits later in this chapter — because the limits are real — but the positive case is not fabricated. Well-managed grazing genuinely pulls carbon into soil — and it does so while producing food — and the effect is not a rounding error.

Put all of that together and you have a system that is, by any fair accounting, substantially better across several important axes than what it is trying to replace. It builds soil. It produces food. It restores something close to normal animal lives. It supports rural economies. It sequesters carbon. None of those achievements is imaginary — and none of them should be held at arm's length in what follows.

Applying the Framework

Run the four-axis framework across this version of the operation — and the scoring changes noticeably from the industrial comparison in the previous chapter.

On the environmental axis, pasture-based operations score well. Soil carbon accumulates rather than erodes. Chemical inputs — synthetic fertilizer, herbicide, insecticide, fungicide, antibiotic — are absent or dramatically reduced, meaning the downstream externalities of runoff, resistance, and non-target toxicity are absent or dramatically reduced. Biodiversity on and around the operation is higher: perennial pastures host arthropod, avian, and small-mammal assemblages that row-crop fields cannot support.8 Water infiltration rates into rotationally grazed soil are higher than into tilled soil by substantial multiples, with consequent reductions in flood peaks and downstream sedimentation.9 The environmental axis, in a pasture-based system run competently, moves from the deep negative register of the industrial score into a positive register that is not marginal.

On the social axis, the picture is similarly improved. Worker conditions on a pasture-based operation are not the worker conditions of an industrial slaughterhouse. The operation sits inside the community it feeds rather than at the end of a commodity chain that terminates at a processor two states away. Rural community indicators — employment, retention, median income for agricultural families — track with the presence of diversified direct-marketed agriculture in ways that have been documented for a generation now.6 The social axis improves.

On the economic axis, the scoring is conditional and depends on scale. At the scale of a Polyface or a comparable operation, several hundred acres, owner-operated, direct-marketed, the economics work: the farm is profitable, the family is supported, the next generation has a ladder onto the operation if one of them wants it. The work is not easy and the margins are not wide, but the operation is economically viable in a way that much of conventional small-scale commodity agriculture is not. At the scale of the continental food supply, the economic axis becomes much harder to score because the system as currently practiced has never operated at that scale. Scaling a direct-marketed pastured-poultry model from one operation in the Shenandoah Valley to, say, the broiler demand of a metropolitan Atlantic seaboard would require land, processing infrastructure, labor supply, and concentrated feed in volumes that the existing direct-market architecture has not produced and the existing commodity architecture cannot produce without compromising the ecological distinctions that make the pasture-based operation worth doing in the first place. This is not a case against the operation at its own scale. It is a case for treating the operation's achievements as operation-scale achievements, which is how Salatin himself has generally framed them. The claim that what Polyface does could be extended to feed the country is a claim that has rarely been made by Polyface itself. It has more often been made by journalists and advocates around the operation, and the operation is not responsible for the claims made on its behalf.

On the survivability axis, pasture-based operations score substantially better than industrial ones, which is the axis the industrial system scored worst on. The operation does not depend on continuous diesel for its own field work in the same way a row-crop operation does, because most of the field work is being done by the animals themselves. It does not depend on continuous antibiotic administration, because the stocking densities and the rotation pattern handle the disease pressure that makes antibiotics necessary in confined systems. It does not depend on continuous federal subsidies for its own outputs, because those outputs are priced into a direct market rather than a commodity one. The system is more survivable than what it replaced.

But the survivability score is not the maximum possible score, and the reason the score is not maximum is the subject of the rest of this chapter. The operation, as currently practiced, still depends on something the industrial system supplies — that dependency is the structural ceiling.

The Structural Ceiling

Regenerative agriculture, in its current operative form in the United States, is structurally tethered to the industrial commodity system it opposes. This is not a statement about any particular operator's motives or honesty — it is a statement about how the economics and the supply chain of the current US agricultural system constrain even the operations explicitly trying to operate outside it.

The ceiling shows up in different places at different operations. For operations that raise ruminants on pasture — grass-finished beef, pastured lamb, grass-fed dairy — the ceiling is lower and the tether is thinner, because ruminants can live most or all of their lives on forage produced on the farm itself and require relatively little from outside the operation. For operations that raise monogastric animals on pasture — broilers, layers, pigs — the ceiling is higher and the tether is thicker, because monogastrics cannot live on forage alone and must be supplemented with concentrated protein and energy that, under current conditions, comes from somewhere else.

Polyface is the worked example because it is the operation whose numbers are most completely in the public record. The numbers are worth walking through carefully.

The Polyface Math

Polyface's broiler operation, at the scale Michael Pollan described in The Omnivore's Dilemma, processes approximately twelve thousand broiler chickens per year in mobile field shelters moved daily across pasture.10 Salatin's own Pastured Poultry Profit$ gives the feed conversion ratio for the birds as roughly three pounds of feed for each pound of finished body weight — with an average finished weight in the neighborhood of four pounds per bird.11 The arithmetic from those numbers is direct. Twelve thousand birds, at four pounds of body weight each, at three pounds of feed per pound of body weight, comes to approximately one hundred and forty-four thousand pounds of feed per year for the broiler flock alone.

The composition of that feed is also in the public record. Polyface has published its layer and broiler ration specifications; independent writing on the farm, including longer profiles and follow-up interviews over the years, has confirmed the proportions. The ration is built around roughly forty-nine to fifty percent corn and roughly thirty to thirty-one percent soy, with the remainder made up of mineral premix, fish meal, kelp, and a few other supplements.12 Applied to the total feed figure, that means roughly seventy-one thousand five hundred pounds of corn and roughly forty-four thousand five hundred pounds of soybean meal per year, for the broiler flock alone. The layer flock, smaller but year-round, adds to these totals. So do the pigs that spend part of their finishing period on concentrate.

The corn and soy do not come from Polyface's own acres. The farm's perennial pasture system is not arranged for row cropping, and the Shenandoah Valley's climate and soils are not particularly suited to corn on the scale required. The feed comes from off-farm sources — grain sold by neighboring farmers and feed mills in the broader region of the valley and adjacent states. Salatin has written openly about this sourcing, and has emphasized that the farm works to buy from local non-GMO producers rather than from anonymous commodity channels.13 The sourcing preference is real. The underlying point is that even with the most scrupulous regional sourcing, the corn and soy feeding the Polyface birds are grown under the conditions that most corn and soy in the United States are grown under: annual row crop, tillage or herbicide-based residue management, synthetic nitrogen inputs, petroleum-powered field operations, and — directly or indirectly — the federal commodity support system that keeps the price of corn and soy low enough for a pastured-poultry operation to purchase them profitably. The industrial substrate is the substrate.

I want to be careful about how this finding is registered, because the framing matters.

It is not a gotcha. Salatin publishes these numbers himself, in his own books, for farmers who want to replicate his system. The feed proportions are printed in Pastured Poultry Profit$; the scale is in The Omnivore's Dilemma and subsequent profiles. There is no concealment to expose. Anyone who has read the source material with any attention has encountered the math. The math was already public, in that sense, when any of us started having this conversation.

It is also not a personal failure. The operator is working inside the structural conditions of US agriculture as those conditions currently exist. Grass-finishing a broiler in any meaningful volume is not, under present conditions, economically possible at a price point that sustains a family farm. The price of corn and soy has been held down by half a century of agricultural policy; the cost of land, labor, and processing has not. An operator deciding, in 1995 or 2005 or 2025, whether to build a pastured broiler enterprise has faced a narrow set of real choices, and at every point in that window the economically survivable choice has involved purchasing grain from someone else. The alternative — growing the grain on the farm under perennial-compatible practice — is not absent from the regenerative literature, but it has not, to my knowledge, been implemented at any scale that makes broiler operations like Polyface's replaceable.

The point is worth sharpening, because the sharpening matters for everything that comes after. An operation like Polyface is doing the most that is currently doable inside the existing system. The ceiling on what it can do sits not at the gate of the farm but at the edge of the commodity market the farm buys from. Raising the ceiling would require changing the commodity market, which is a different kind of problem than changing how a farm is managed. An individual farmer can change how the farm is managed; an individual farmer cannot change the structure of US corn and soy production. The work Salatin and operators like him have done on the manageable side is the work they could do. The work on the substrate side is the work the rest of the food system has not done.

What the finding is, then, is a structural observation. Even the best version of regenerative agriculture as currently practiced, the operation with the most documented soil gains, the most direct-marketed income, the most visible animal welfare improvements, the most sustained rural economic impact, still has its monogastric-animal enterprises running on carbohydrate and protein inputs produced by the industrial commodity system it opposes. The system it is offering as the alternative is, in its concentrate-dependent component, riding on the substrate it wants to replace.

This is the structural ceiling. It is not the end of the argument — it is the thing the argument has to take seriously before it continues.

The McWilliams-Salatin Exchange

The substrate question became public in a way most non-specialists would remember when James McWilliams published "The Myth of Sustainable Meat" in the New York Times in April of 2012.14 McWilliams, a historian at Texas State, had been arguing for several years that the environmental advantages of pasture-based animal production were overstated. The op-ed compressed the argument. It made several claims, some of which were wrong, one of which was right, and the combination produced a public exchange that illustrated the limits of the conversation as then framed more clearly than either side would likely have wanted.

McWilliams's central environmental claim, that pasture-raised beef produces substantially more methane per unit of beef than feedlot beef because grass-finished animals live longer and eat more lignocellulose, was overstated as applied. The methane-per-unit-beef numbers are genuinely higher on pasture, but the comparison is incomplete without the soil carbon side of the ledger, which can offset a meaningful fraction of the methane difference in well-managed systems. The Teague analysis and the subsequent literature have laid this out in more detail than the op-ed allowed for.7 The land-use claim, that pasture requires more acres per pound of finished animal than feedlot, was true on its own terms but also incomplete. The relevant question is not acres-per-pound but which kind of land is being used, for what, under what set of effects on downstream ecosystems. A great deal of pastureland is land that cannot productively support row crops.

The claim McWilliams made that landed, and landed in a way neither he nor his critics particularly wanted to admit, was the feed-sourcing claim. He pointed out that pasture-based operations raising monogastric animals still relied on purchased grain produced in the conventional industrial system, and that this reliance made the sustainability advantages of the pasture component narrower than their advocates claimed. On the feed-sourcing point, the math is the math, and it was already public in Salatin's own books.

Salatin's public response came through Polyface Farm's own channels and through subsequent essays and interviews.15 The response was vigorous and, in places, sincere in a way the op-ed had not been about Salatin's work. Several of his counterpoints were substantive: the methane comparison was being handled more simplistically than the science warranted; the land-use comparison was ignoring land-use distinctions; Polyface's local non-GMO sourcing was real and deserved to be distinguished from anonymous commodity sourcing. One argument in particular deserves engagement on its own terms, because it is the most serious thing said in the exchange and because it gets close to the structural question without quite reaching it.

Salatin argued, in effect, that the movement of grain from grain-growing regions into grazing regions via animal feed is not a pathology of the industrial system. It is an ancient pattern. Historically, lowland grain-growing regions and upland grazing regions have exchanged nutrients through exactly this kind of trade: grain flowed to the uplands as animal feed, and animal products flowed back to the lowlands as food. The pattern long predates petroleum agriculture, commodity subsidies, and the US Department of Agriculture. A pastured-poultry operation buying corn from a farmer fifty miles away is, under this framing, participating in a trade relationship as old as agriculture itself.

There is real history in that framing. The highland-lowland nutrient exchange is genuine, and it is defensible as a way of organizing agricultural landscapes. What the framing does not quite reach, in my reading of Salatin's reply, is the point underneath McWilliams's otherwise flawed argument. The historical highland-lowland exchange was regional and operated at a scale that did not require a continental commodity system. The modern version of the exchange operates at a scale that does. When Polyface sources non-GMO corn from within the Shenandoah region, it is still sourcing from farms whose own inputs, seed, fertilizer, herbicide, field equipment, transportation to market, run through the continental industrial substrate that corn and soy have been embedded in since roughly the Nixon administration's rewrite of the farm bill.16 The regional-sourcing move reduces the transportation footprint and the genetic-engineering exposure. It does not detach the feed from the underlying substrate.

Salatin's response, read carefully, is best understood as a defense of the farming model's integrity at the farm-gate scale, and at that scale it is largely correct. It is not a response to the structural observation that the farm, as a node in a larger agricultural system, cannot fully escape that system as currently organized. McWilliams had framed the feed-sourcing point as an accusation, which it did not need to be and which made the response defensive. The point was better made as a structural observation. This is how I am trying to make it now.

What the 2012 exchange illustrated, in retrospect, was the limit of the conversation as then conducted. McWilliams wanted to indict the pasture model. Salatin wanted to defend it. Between the two positions there was not much room for the observation that the pasture model was genuinely doing most of what it claimed to do and was still riding on the industrial substrate to do it. Both of those are true. Neither side's framing in the 2012 exchange made that doubled picture easy to see. The conversation has moved somewhat since then. Versions of the structural observation now appear in places where they did not appear in 2012: in Civil Eats coverage, in the peer-reviewed agroecological literature, and in the internal conversations of regenerative-agriculture advocates who have been working on perennial-grain and on-farm feed alternatives precisely because the ceiling is a ceiling they can see from inside the movement.

Grazed and Confused

The climate side of the ledger deserves the same honest treatment. The case for well-managed grazing as a substantial climate mitigation lever is real but bounded. It is bounded in ways the most enthusiastic popular framing has not always absorbed.

Tara Garnett and colleagues at the Food Climate Research Network at the University of Oxford published Grazed and Confused? in 2017, which remains the most rigorous systematic assessment of grazing's climate claims.17 The report reviewed the accumulated peer-reviewed evidence for soil carbon sequestration under improved grazing management and concluded that the sequestration effect is genuine, measurable, and at a global scale able to offset somewhere on the order of twenty to sixty percent of annual ruminant greenhouse gas emissions under optimistic assumptions. The report also concluded that the effect saturates after several decades. A grassland accumulating carbon under improved management does not continue accumulating at the initial rate indefinitely. The upper end of the estimated sequestration potential requires management conditions that are difficult to achieve and maintain at scale.

Teague and colleagues have pushed back on parts of the Grazed and Confused? analysis, arguing that the report underestimates the sequestration potential of adaptive multi-paddock grazing specifically and that it aggregates the grazing-management literature in a way that flattens differences between management regimes that are in fact ecologically distinct.18 Their case is not implausible. The specific management regime matters: a continuous grazing system and an AMP system are not interchangeable from the soil's perspective, and the literature has not always treated them as distinct.

The saturation mechanism in the Garnett analysis deserves a moment of its own, because its implications are easy to miss in popular framings. Soil carbon accumulation under improved grazing is fastest in the early years after management change, when the soil is most below its equilibrium carbon level for the new management regime. As the soil approaches the new equilibrium, a process that takes in temperate grasslands somewhere on the order of twenty to forty years, the annual sequestration rate declines toward zero. The soil is not permanently scrubbing carbon out of the atmosphere the way an unsaturated chemical sink would. It is moving from one equilibrium to a higher equilibrium, and the movement is bounded. The total amount of carbon ultimately stored is meaningful; the rate of annual accumulation has an arc. Any long-run climate accounting for grazing has to incorporate this shape, and the popular framings often do not. This is not a criticism of Polyface or of any particular operator; it is a criticism of the rhetorical compression that has sometimes attended the climate argument for regenerative agriculture in the broader public conversation.

Read against each other, Garnett and Teague produce an honest calibration. Well-managed grazing is genuinely carbon-positive at the operation scale. It is not climate-neutral for ruminant production overall, even in the most optimistic scenarios. It is a meaningful contribution to the climate side of the agricultural ledger, substantially better than poorly managed grazing or feedlot, and not by itself a climate solution for ruminant agriculture. The claim that pastured livestock operations are climate-positive, stated carefully, is about the operation's own carbon balance rather than about the emissions the operation is also producing. Stated carefully, the claim is defensible. Stated as a blanket net-negative framing for ruminant agriculture as a sector, it is not.

None of this undoes the soil, welfare, or rural-economy case. All of those stand on their own. It does mean that the climate claim specifically needs to be stated with care. This care is compatible with the operation being ecologically serious, which it is.

What the Ceiling Means

The chapter has, I hope, been doing two things at once. It has been taking regenerative agriculture seriously as the most developed alternative to the industrial system on offer — and it has been looking at what the most developed alternative can and cannot do as currently practiced. Both of those are honest descriptions. The operation does what it does, and it does not do what it does not do, and the honest account requires holding both.

What the structural ceiling means, stated as plainly as I can state it, is this. Regenerative agriculture as currently organized in the United States is significantly better than what it is trying to replace. It is also still riding on the substrate it is trying to replace. Its monogastric-animal enterprises import tens of thousands of pounds of corn and soy per year from the industrial commodity system. Its ruminant enterprises depend less directly on that system but still operate inside the broader infrastructure: the diesel for equipment, the steel for fencing, the mineral supplements, the veterinary care, that the industrial substrate supplies. An operation like Polyface is an achievement inside a system whose boundaries the operation cannot fully escape.

This is not a defeat. It is a limit. Limits can be mapped and worked against. A substantial fraction of the regenerative-agriculture literature over the last decade has been exactly this work: perennial-grain breeding programs trying to make cereal production compatible with perennial root systems,19 silvopastoral systems integrating timber, browse, and ruminant production on the same acreage, and multi-species rotation schemes that reduce the dependence on external concentrated feed. Every one of these is a real response to the structural ceiling. None of them has, as of this writing, produced the fully untethered alternative the framework I have been using would score highest.

The Land Institute's intermediate wheatgrass program, marketed under the trade name Kernza, is the most commercially advanced of these responses. After decades of breeding work in Salina, Kansas, Kernza now has commercial acreage in the low thousands of acres across the upper Midwest, a published grain market, and a small but real downstream supply chain in the craft-brewing and specialty-baking sectors. It is not yet a corn replacement at any scale that would matter to a Polyface feed order. It is not even a credible partial replacement at that scale. What it is is existence-proof that a deep-rooted perennial can be bred toward commercial grain production, and that a seed company and a farmer can, between them, make the numbers work at the margin. Silvopasture research in the southeastern United States and the Mediterranean dehesa literature offer similar existence proofs on the animal side: systems in which ruminants, browse, mast, and tree crops coexist on the same acreage under management regimes that resemble pre-industrial agroforestry more than they resemble modern pasture-based operations. The point is not that any of these is ready to replace the current substrate. The point is that the work of designing an alternative substrate is actively under way, even if the finished substrate is not yet here.

The question that closes this chapter is the question Part III of the book exists to take up. If the best version of the currently-available alternative is structurally tethered to what it opposes, even at the scale of a farm as studied and careful as Polyface, what would actually escape the substrate? What would an operation look like whose feed comes from the landscape rather than from the commodity system, whose animals are selected for the landscape rather than for the commodity market, whose relationship to the land the continent sits on runs through the land's deeper history rather than through the last two centuries of row-crop engineering?

I think the answer lives in the direction Paul Martin has been pointing us since the 1970s. The landscape this continent is, considered on the scale the continent has actually existed on, is not a landscape that evolved under row-crop corn and feedlot cattle. It is a landscape that evolved under a suite of large-bodied animals most of which have not been present for twelve thousand years. The animals we use to feed ourselves now are, in the main, animals whose ecological partnership is with somewhere else. The animals whose ecological partnership is with this continent are, in the main, not being used to feed us at all. That mismatch is the hinge of what follows.

Regenerative agriculture has taken the food system as far as it has been able to take it while working within the cast of species it inherited and the commodity substrate that cast depends on. The distance it has covered is real and should not be given back. What comes next is a different question: whether the cast of species can be reopened, and whether, reopened, it changes what sustainability looks like on this continent. The next chapter starts there.

CHAPTER SEVEN

The Wrong Proxy in the Right Place

◆ ◆ ◆
From Paul S. Martin,
Twilight of the Mammoths (2005)

“From fossils we know that the grasses, forbs, shrubs, and trees of the Americas coevolved with a much greater variety of large herbivores than exists today.”

The thorns are not decorative. They are anti‑herbivore defenses calibrated to animals the continent has not carried for twelve thousand years.

— P. S. Martin, p. 185

The first time I saw Allan Savory speak in person was at one of his very first conferences, and there were maybe twenty of us in the room. It was a small classroom: no podium, no stage, no microphone. A handful of ranchers, a few people who had read the book and wanted to meet the man behind it, and me, in my twenties, with a marked-up copy of Paul Martin's Twilight of the Mammoths in my bag. I had read Savory's book twice by that point. I was not there to be convinced. I was there because I had a question.

The room was small enough that you could see everyone's face. Savory spoke with the calm authority of someone who had been making the same argument for decades and had outlasted most of his critics, and even at his age, there was something about him that felt younger than I had expected: a quickness, an energy, a sense that the ideas were still alive in him rather than calcified into a script. The talk he gave was genuinely good, substantive, grounded in decades of observation. He showed photographs of degraded rangeland in Zimbabwe and the same rangeland after years of planned grazing. He showed soil profiles. He showed vegetation transects. The evidence was real, and he presented it without overselling, which I respected. A lot of people in the regenerative space oversell. Savory did not need to. Only a small number of people can say they were in that room to witness such a great man on the way to becoming who he is today. I photobombed him afterward.

I asked him whether holistic management worked everywhere, or whether it worked better in some landscapes than others.

He paused. Then he said it was not working in the arid Southwest.

The room was small enough that you could hear the shift — the way twenty people adjusted in their chairs when the answer was not the one they expected. Savory did not hedge. He did not qualify it into nothing. He said the results had not tracked in the Southwest the way they had tracked in southern Africa or the Great Plains. He said it with the particular kind of candor that serious people use when they are being honest about something they have not yet resolved — not defensive, not dismissive, just honest.

I spent the rest of the session thinking about it. The talks continued. I took notes on a few of them, but my attention kept circling back to what Savory had said. The arid Southwest. The results had not tracked. I thought I knew why.

The food truck was parked at the edge of the conference grounds, one of those converted Airstream trailers with a sliding window and a short menu of things that could plausibly be called lunch. I got a plate, took a bottle of water, and looked for a place to sit that was not inside the main hall. Savory was already outside, sitting alone at a folding table with his own plate, and the chair across from him was empty. I sat down.

I should say what I had with me, because it matters. I had my conference bag, my phone, and a copy of Paul Martin's Twilight of the Mammoths. It was not a new copy. I had read it three times by that point, and it looked like it. The spine was cracked, the pages were dog-eared in places I had found worth returning to, and I had marked passages in the margins with the kind of shorthand that means nothing to anyone else: page numbers cross-referencing other pages, small arrows connecting ideas across chapters, question marks next to claims I had wanted to verify. It was a working copy of a book I had been thinking with for several years. I did not bring it to impress him. I brought it because I thought I might need to show him specific passages, and I did.

The conversation started where the Q&A had ended. I told him I thought I understood why the Southwest had been difficult, and that the answer was not in his framework but in the fossil record beneath the landscape his framework was being applied to. He looked at the book on the table between us. I opened it.

I introduced myself. I told him I ran a certification organization and that I had been working on food-system evaluation for years, which was true and also not the point. The point was the book. I told him I had been reading Paul Martin, the paleoecologist at the University of Arizona who had spent his career on the late Pleistocene extinctions and their ecological consequences. I thought Martin's work explained something about the Southwest that Savory's framework, on its own, could not.

He looked at the book on the table between us. He said he actually did know who Paul Martin was. And something in me shifted — I remember holding my hand out toward the book, a little wide-eyed, thinking: Why has this work not been integrated with your work yet? The fossil record and the management framework were sitting in the same intellectual space, pointed at the same landscapes, and no one had connected them. I told him the version I had been turning over in my head for months: that the Pleistocene fossil record preserves, in extraordinary detail, the specific animals that co-evolved with specific landscapes across the American continent, and that the record for the Southwest tells a story that does not involve cattle.

What I walked him through, and what I want to walk the reader through now because the argument is the hinge of everything that follows in this book, is a specific claim about the relationship between animal species and landscape. The claim is this: the fossil record tells us which animals co-evolved with which landscapes, and the American Southwest's fossil record does not say cattle.

Martin had spent decades building the evidentiary case. His chapter on the Grand Canyon, titled "Grand Canyon Suite," is the most concentrated version of it, and it is the chapter I had marked most heavily. The cave deposits along the Colorado River and its tributaries preserve one of the most detailed records of late Pleistocene fauna in the American West. Rampart Cave, a limestone overhang above the river in western Grand Canyon, contained stratified deposits of Shasta ground sloth dung, five feet of it in places, layered over thousands of years, preserved so well that the plant material in it could be identified to genus and sometimes species.1 Stanton's Cave, downstream in Marble Canyon, held the bones and dung pellets of Harrington's mountain goat, an extinct species whose remains turned up alongside those of bighorn sheep, small equids, and a scattering of bison. Condor remains appeared at multiple cave sites. The assemblage that emerged from the deposits was specific: ground sloths, mountain goats, equids, camelids, condors. Not one of those animals is a bovid.

I explained this to Savory as plainly as I could. The ground sloths at Rampart Cave had been eating globe mallow, Mormon tea, saltbush, catclaw acacia, yucca, and cactus. Dick Hansen's composition analysis of the dung showed that desert globe mallow leaves and stem fragments made up roughly half the plant parts in the deposit, with Mormon tea pollen accounting for eighteen percent and the remainder distributed among saltbush, acacia, common reed, and various succulents.2 What Hansen found almost none of was grass. The Shasta ground sloth, a thousand-pound browser that had occupied the Southwest for tens of thousands of years, was not a grazer. Neither were the camelids whose bones turn up across the basin-and-range country. The equids grazed, and so did the mammoths, but the overall assemblage was weighted heavily toward browsing, toward animals whose digestive systems and foraging behavior were calibrated to the woody, thorny, armed vegetation that still dominates the arid Southwest today.

Martin made the connection explicit in his later chapters. The thorny, oil-rich, tannin-laden shrubs of the Southwest — creosote, mesquite, catclaw acacia, the entire armored understory of the Sonoran and Chihuahuan deserts — carry the structural signatures of vegetation that evolved under heavy browsing pressure from large-bodied animals.3 The thorns are not decorative. They are anti-herbivore defenses, and the herbivores they evolved against are the ones the fossil record preserves: ground sloths, camelids, and to a lesser extent the equids and proboscideans that ranged through the region. Martin put the principle as directly as anyone has: "the grasses, forbs, shrubs, and trees of the Americas coevolved with a much greater variety of large herbivores than exists today."4 The vegetation is still shaped by the animals. The animals are no longer there.

Field Note
The Contract

For the record, when I eat fruits, I eat the seeds. I have ever since it occurred to me that there was a contract that we made with these fruits.

The contract is simple. The plant grows a sweet, fragrant, nutritious package around a small hard seed. Something eats the package, carries the seed some distance from the parent tree, and deposits it in its own fertilizer. The plant invests in the package because the seed gets dispersal and a fertilized start. The animal invests in finding the fruit because the package is calorie-dense, and its appearance is, literally, an advertisement.

Most of the animals the fruit trees of this continent made their contract with are gone. Mastodons, gomphotheres, ground sloths. The mouths that osage orange and honey locust and pawpaw grew their fruit for are not coming. The tree drops its crop every autumn and most of what is down there will rot where it fell.

I am not a mastodon. But I can keep one small piece of the contract. I eat the seeds when I eat the fruit. Dispersal in fertilizer is not guaranteed the way it once was. But there are few things in this world that actually want to be eaten. Fruits want to be eaten. Milk wants to be drunk. Grass wants to be grazed down. It seems to me that if a thing is asking, the polite response is to answer.

Savory listened. He did not interrupt, which I noticed. People who are performing attention interrupt. People who are actually paying attention do not. He asked a question about the camelids: where specifically the fossil evidence placed them, whether the Arizona deposits had been dated reliably. I turned to the pages I had marked and showed him the relevant passages. He read them. He asked about the equids, and I walked him through the Grand Canyon deposits and the broader Western record that Chapter 3 of this book has already covered. He asked about the dating methods, and I explained that the radiocarbon dates on the Rampart Cave deposits were among the most robust in the North American Pleistocene record: tens of thousands of years of stratified organic material, independently dated by multiple laboratories, cross-referenced against the geological stratigraphy of the cave itself.

He sat back in his chair. His plate was mostly untouched. He knew Martin's work. He said so. But he had not read it from this angle, had not connected the proxy question to the management question. He said something I will paraphrase rather than quote, because I did not record the conversation and I do not trust my memory to preserve his exact words across the years since: he said that in southern Africa, where the framework had been developed, the large herbivores were still present. The wildebeest, the zebra, the buffalo, the elephant — the animals the African grasslands had evolved under were still there, and the management methodology had been designed around their behavior. He had always known that the American situation was different in some respects. He had not thought about it from the direction I was pointing.

This was the moment the conversation pivoted, and it is the moment I want the reader to sit with, because the pivot matters. Savory was not admitting a flaw in his framework. He was recognizing a variable his framework had not needed to specify in the context where it was developed. In southern Africa, the proxy question does not arise, because the original animals are still there. You do not need to ask "which herbivore?" when the wildebeest and the zebra and the buffalo are standing in front of you. The question only becomes visible when the original animals are gone and you are choosing a substitute. And in the Americas, the original animals have been gone for twelve thousand years, and every herbivore currently on the landscape is a substitute, some closer to what the fossil record prescribes and some further away.

What I was suggesting was simple enough to say at a folding table over lunch, and difficult enough that almost no one had said it yet. The arid Southwest was calling for equid species: donkeys, horses, the animals whose lineage actually belonged to that landscape. It made sense to take a look at the fossils to get a better idea of the match with the flora of the area. Not just the animal, but the relationship between the animal and the vegetation: the specific co-evolutionary partnership that the fossil record preserves and the current management regime ignores.

I want to state the broader version of this argument with the same care I stated the lunch version at that table.

Allan Savory's holistic management framework is a genuine intellectual achievement. I do not say this as a courtesy before a critique, because what follows is not a critique. It is a refinement, one that I believe makes the framework significantly stronger.

The core of holistic management, as Savory and Jody Butterfield laid it out in Holistic Management: A New Framework for Decision Making, is the proposition that the relationship between large herbivores and grassland is not incidental but constitutive.5 The grassland needs the animal. Not the animal standing in place, overgrazing a single patch until the roots give out, but the animal moving — concentrated by predation pressure or management pressure into dense herds that graze intensively, trample standing dead material into contact with the soil surface, deposit urine and dung, and then move on, leaving the grazed area to recover for an extended period before the herd returns. The cycle of impact and recovery is what the grassland evolved under, and in its absence the grassland degrades: the standing dead material oxidizes in place rather than being incorporated into the soil, the root systems weaken without the stimulus of defoliation, and the bare ground between grass plants widens until the system tips into desertification.

The framework is powerful, and I do not use that word casually. It explains a great deal about the dynamics of grassland degradation that rest-based conservation management has struggled to address, and the empirical results from southern Africa, from the Great Plains, and from managed operations around the world are real. The ranchers and land managers who have adopted holistic management and seen their landscapes recover are not imagining it. The grass is coming back. The soil biology is rebuilding. The water-infiltration rates are improving. The framework works.

What the framework does not do — and this is the refinement, not the critique — is specify which animal.
