Holistic management tells you that the landscape needs a large herbivore, managed in a way that mimics the evolutionary pressure the landscape evolved under. It tells you how to manage the animal: the timing, the density, the recovery period, the monitoring. What it does not tell you is which animal belongs in which landscape. It treats the herbivore slot as generic: fill it with whatever large ruminant is available, manage it correctly, and the system responds.

For the Great Plains, this works. The shortgrass prairie evolved under bison, and cattle, which are bovids like bison, occupy a similar enough ecological niche that the substitution holds. The grass species, the root architectures, the soil microbiology, the grazing-and-recovery dynamics are all calibrated to a large-bodied bovid grazer, and cattle are a large-bodied bovid grazer. The proxy is close enough that the framework's management methodology can do the rest of the work. Holistic management on the Great Plains is, in a meaningful sense, restoring a relationship that the landscape recognizes.

The Southwest is different. And the reason it is different is the reason Savory's results had not tracked there, and the reason I had come to the conference with Martin's book.

The Southwest did not evolve under bovids. The fossil record is unambiguous on this point. The dominant large herbivores of the late Pleistocene Southwest were ground sloths, camelids, and equids: browsers and mixed feeders whose digestive systems and foraging behaviors were adapted to the woody, thorny, browse-heavy vegetation of the arid basins and canyons. Bison were present in the Southwest in the late Pleistocene, but they were a minor component of the fauna, not the dominant one. The bones in Stanton's Cave included some bison, but the assemblage was overwhelmingly Harrington's mountain goat and equids.6 The Rampart Cave deposits were ground sloth from top to bottom. The landscape's primary large-herbivore relationships were with animals that no longer exist, and those relationships were not with grazers.

When you put cattle on that landscape, even well-managed cattle under the best holistic management protocol available, you are asking a bovid grazer to fill an ecological role that was held by browsers and mixed feeders. The grass species of the arid Southwest, to the extent that they exist, did not evolve under the kind of intensive grazing pressure that the Great Plains grasses did. The shortgrass species of the Plains — the blue grama, the buffalo grass, the sideoats grama — co-evolved with bison over millions of years; their growth points sit at or below the soil surface, their root systems respond to defoliation with renewed vigor, their entire architecture is an adaptation to heavy, periodic grazing. The browse species of the Southwest — the acacias, the mesquite, the saltbush, the globe mallow, the yucca and prickly pear — evolved under a fundamentally different kind of pressure. They evolved to be eaten by animals with the body mass and the gut capacity to process woody, thorny, chemically defended plant tissue. Cattle do not browse acacia. Cattle do not eat globe mallow. Cattle do not strip mesquite pods. Cattle graze, and when the grass in the arid Southwest gives out under grazing pressure, the system does not recover the way a Great Plains grassland recovers, because the system was never organized around that kind of pressure in the first place. The browse thickens. The mesquite encroaches. The bare ground between the surviving grasses widens. And the conventional diagnosis — overgrazing, poor management, insufficient rest — misses the deeper cause. The animal is wrong for the landscape.

This is what I told Savory at the folding table outside the food truck. The holistic management framework provides the management methodology — the how. Martin's fossil record provides the species guide — the which. Together, they are stronger than either one alone. Separately, the framework has a blind spot in exactly the landscapes where the proxy mismatch is largest.

He did not reject it. He sat with it. He asked me whether anyone had tried running camelids in the Southwest under managed grazing protocols, and I told him the honest answer, which was that almost no one had, because the conversation about proxy selection had barely begun. Josh Donlan and his colleagues had published their provocative essay in Nature in 2005 proposing Pleistocene rewilding as a conservation strategy, arguing that the ecological histories of the Americas make the case for restoring large vertebrates, including proxies for extinct species, to landscapes that had co-evolved with them.7 Donlan's longer treatment in The American Naturalist the following year had laid out the agenda more carefully, with specific proposals and specific risk assessments.8 But the rewilding conversation had remained largely in the conservation-biology lane. Almost no one had connected it to the food-systems conversation. Almost no one had asked the question that seemed, to me, obvious: if the fossil record tells us which animals belong in which landscapes, and if those animals can be managed for food production, then the rewilding agenda and the food-systems agenda are the same agenda.

Savory, to his credit, saw the connection immediately. The man had spent his career arguing that the landscape needs the animal. He did not need to be persuaded of the principle. What the fossil record offered was a specification of the principle that his framework had left unspecified. The landscape needs this animal, in this place, because this is the animal the landscape's vegetation and soil biology evolved under. The proxy is not interchangeable. The proxy is chosen by the fossil record.

The principle extends far beyond the Southwest, and it extends far beyond the conversation I had with Savory at that folding table. Let me widen it.

If the fossil record provides the species guide, and I believe the evidence from multiple continents and multiple research programs shows that it does, then the question of which animals belong in which landscapes is answerable on a continental scale. The answer is not uniform. It varies by region, by biome, by the specific evolutionary history of the specific landscape in question. And the variation is exactly what makes the principle useful, because the variation tells you something the current food system does not know it needs to know.

The Great Plains, as I have said, want bovids. The shortgrass prairie is a bovid landscape, and the best available proxy for the extinct Bison antiquus is its living descendant, Bison bison. Bison restoration on the Plains, the work American Prairie is doing in Montana and tribal nations are doing across the northern Plains, is proxy selection that the fossil record endorses. The management methodology that Savory provides is appropriate here, because the proxy is appropriate here. The framework and the fossil record agree.

The Southwest wants something different. The arid basins and canyons of Arizona, New Mexico, and the northern Sonoran and Chihuahuan deserts evolved under camelids, equids, and ground sloths. The ground sloths have no living proxy; no extant species closely replicates a thousand-pound herbivorous xenarthran. But the camelids and equids do. Camels are already in the Americas: feral in parts of the West, stabled on hobby ranches, used in tourism and sport. Dromedaries thrive in arid heat, browse thorny shrubs that cattle will not touch, and produce milk with higher fat and protein content than cow's milk, a fact that matters if the question is not just which animal belongs here but what the animal provides. Horses and donkeys are already in the Americas: feral in vast herds across the basin and range, managed on public and private land, embedded in the cultural and economic fabric of the West. Donkeys in particular are superbly adapted to the arid Southwest: hardy in heat, efficient on sparse forage, and browsers as readily as grazers. The small equids whose bones turned up in Stanton's Cave were closer in size to a donkey than to a modern ranch horse. The species are here. What is missing is the ecological framing that would put them in the role the fossil record identifies as theirs. A camel on Southwest rangeland is not an exotic introduction. It is a proxy for Camelops, returning to a landscape its lineage shaped for millions of years before a twelve-thousand-year interruption. A donkey or a horse on that same rangeland is not a feral pest. It is the closest living relative of the Pleistocene equids whose bones lie in the canyon deposits, returning to an ecological role that the vegetation still remembers. The Southwest does not need more cattle. It needs the animals it lost.

The eastern hardwood forests present yet another case, and one that connects directly to the ecological-anachronism argument the Prologue introduced. The Pleistocene fauna of the eastern woodlands included mastodons, the dominant large-bodied browser of the deciduous forests, whose range extended from the Great Lakes to the Gulf Coast, along with woodland-adapted ground sloths, elk, deer, and a suite of smaller herbivores. The osage orange, the pawpaw, the honey locust, the Kentucky coffee tree. These megafaunal-dispersed fruit trees, which Janzen and Martin identified as ecological anachronisms, are eastern species, and the mouths they evolved for were mastodon and ground-sloth mouths.9 There is no living proxy for an American mastodon. But the principle still operates: the eastern hardwood food system should be oriented around the animals that the eastern hardwood fossil record identifies, which are browsers and mixed feeders, not open-grassland grazers. Silvopastoral systems in the eastern woodlands — integrating ruminant browse, mast, and tree crops on the same acreage — are closer to the fossil record's prescription than any open-pasture cattle operation, even a well-managed one.

The Gulf Coast and the subtropical Southeast present their own puzzle. The Pleistocene fauna of Florida and the coastal plain included tapirs, capybara, llamas, ground sloths, glyptodonts, and a suite of animals whose nearest living relatives now occupy Central and South America. The fossil presence of vampire bats and tapirs in Arizona and California, animals whose modern ranges are exclusively tropical, tells us that the ecological communities of the southern United States were connected to Neotropical faunas in ways that the current landscape does not reflect. Martin noted that tapirs ranged from the Sonoran lowlands to over six thousand feet on the Colorado Plateau, and that their late-Quaternary disappearance is more reasonably modeled as a retreat to tropical refugia under predation pressure than as a response to climate change.10 The proxy implications for the subtropical Southeast are different from those for the Southwest or the Plains: the animal assemblage the landscape evolved under was more diverse, more Neotropical in character, and farther from anything the current food system provides.

The boreal and subarctic tell a different story again. The Pleistocene fauna of the far north included woolly mammoths, steppe bison, horses, muskoxen, and caribou. Caribou and muskoxen survive. The mammoth and the steppe bison do not. The proxy question here is whether elephants, African or Asian, could serve as mammoth proxies in the boreal, a question Donlan and his colleagues raised explicitly and that the Pleistocene Park experiment in Siberia has begun to test with horses, bison, muskoxen, and yak.11 The point is not that elephants are about to be introduced to Alaska. The point is that the principle, the fossil record tells you which animal, applies to every landscape on the continent, and the answers it gives are not the same from one landscape to the next.

The map that emerges from this exercise is nothing like the map the food system currently operates from. The current map is simple: cattle everywhere, with regional adjustments for breed and forage type. The fossil-record map is complex: bovids on the Plains, camelids and equids in the arid West, browsers and mixed feeders in the eastern hardwoods, Neotropical assemblages in the subtropical South, Arctic-adapted species in the far north. The current map treats the continent as a single production surface. The fossil-record map treats it as what it actually is — a mosaic of distinct evolutionary histories, each with its own set of animal relationships, most of which have been unreciprocated for a hundred and twenty centuries.

This is what the current food system misses entirely. The industrial system treats animals as interchangeable production units and landscapes as interchangeable production surfaces. The regenerative-agriculture movement, for all its real achievements, has treated the proxy question as settled — cattle for grasslands, chickens and pigs for everything else — without consulting the record that would tell it whether those proxies are correct. Savory's framework gets the management right and leaves the species unspecified. Martin's fossil record specifies the species and does not provide the management. The synthesis, holistic management guided by paleontological proxy selection, is what neither framework achieves alone.

I am not proposing that the synthesis is easy to implement. I am proposing that it is the correct framework, and that the difficulty of implementation is not an argument against its correctness. The permits for running camels on Southwest rangeland do not currently exist in most jurisdictions. The infrastructure for processing camel or horse meat for the US market does not currently exist at any meaningful scale. The cultural resistance to eating horses in the United States is real and historically deep, and I am not dismissing it. That resistance has its own history, its own logic, and its own emotional weight, and a framework that requires people to change what they eat had better take seriously the reasons they eat what they eat now. The ecological risk assessments for introducing proxy species to landscapes they have not occupied in twelve thousand years are genuinely complex, and the critics of Pleistocene rewilding, Rubenstein and colleagues' careful objections in particular, deserve the engagement they will receive in Chapter 9 of this book.12 None of these obstacles are trivial. All of them are navigable.

What I am saying is that the question "which animal belongs here" has an answer, and the answer is in the ground beneath our feet. The fossil record is not a metaphor. It is a dataset. It contains the bones and the dung and the pollen and the diet profiles of the animals that co-evolved with every landscape on this continent, and those profiles tell us — specifically, empirically, verifiably — which species the landscape's vegetation, soil biology, and ecological dynamics evolved under. When we ignore that record and put cattle on a landscape that evolved under camelids, we are making a proxy-selection error. When we compound that error by building an entire food system around the wrong proxy, we are making the error structural.

The chapter before this one ended with a question: what would actually escape the substrate? What would an operation look like whose animals are selected for the landscape rather than for the commodity market?

The answer begins with the fossil record and a worn copy of Martin's book on a folding table between two people who care about the land. Savory spent his career arguing that the landscape needs the animal. Martin spent his career identifying which animal the landscape needs. The food-truck conversation was the moment I understood that the two frameworks are halves of the same argument, and that the argument, stated whole, reorganizes everything the food system thinks it knows about which species belong where.

I think about that conversation often. I think about Savory sitting with the information, not arguing, not deflecting, just absorbing it and asking the right questions. I think about Martin's book on the table between us — marked up, worn out, full of someone else's lifetime of work distilled into a set of observations so straightforward that the only mystery is why they have not yet reorganized the conversation they belong to. I think about the folding table, the food truck, the conference going on inside without us, and two people outside eating lunch and talking about which animals belong in which places on a continent that has been making do without most of its animals for longer than there has been agriculture.

The synthesis is not mine. The management framework belongs to Savory. The species framework belongs to Martin. What I am doing, what this book is doing, is putting the two together and asking what follows. What follows is the subject of the next chapter: from the principle to the plate. What does it actually look like to eat from landscapes managed under proxy assemblages the fossil record endorses? The question is not abstract. The animals are real, some of them are already here, and the landscapes are waiting.

CHAPTER EIGHT

Rewilding the Plate

◆ ◆ ◆

The previous chapter ended with a principle: the fossil record tells you which animal belongs in which landscape, and the management framework tells you how to manage the animal once it is there. The principle is clean. What I want to do now is show what the principle looks like when it touches actual ground: real landscapes, real animals, real food being produced and consumed by real people, today, not in some aspirational future that requires a revolution before breakfast.

The vision this chapter describes is not hypothetical. Parts of it are already operating. Other parts are within reach of deliberate effort. The honest assessment of scale, what rewilded food systems can and cannot do at present, comes later in the chapter, because the honest assessment matters and the book's credibility depends on not overselling. But the starting point is what exists, because what exists is more substantial than most people in the food-systems conversation realize.

What Already Exists

Start with the feral hog.

Texas is home to an estimated 2.6 million feral hogs (Sus scrofa), descendants of domestic pigs escaped or released over the centuries since Spanish colonization, now established across every one of the state's 254 counties.1 The animals are ecological demolition crews. They root through native grassland, destroy ground-nesting bird habitat, contaminate waterways, damage agricultural crops to the tune of an estimated $52 million per year in direct agricultural losses in Texas alone, and compete with native wildlife for mast and forage.2 They are, by any ecological measure, an invasive species causing measurable harm. They are also, by any nutritional measure, a substantial source of animal protein living on land that did not ask for them.

The dual value is underappreciated in the food-systems conversation. Texas Parks and Wildlife Department reports that hunters harvest approximately 750,000 feral hogs per year in the state, a figure that does not keep pace with the animals' reproductive rate. Feral sows can produce two litters per year, averaging five to six piglets per litter, and populations can double in four months under favorable conditions.3 The harvest, even at three-quarters of a million animals annually, is not reducing the population. It is converting a fraction of the ecological damage into usable food. The meat is lean, free-range in the most literal sense of the term, and produced without any of the inputs that industrial pork requires: no commodity feed, no confinement infrastructure, no antibiotics, no manure lagoons, no federal subsidies for corn. The animal feeds itself on the landscape, damages the landscape in the process, and the harvest simultaneously produces food and reduces the damage.

I am not romanticizing feral hogs. They are genuinely destructive, and their management is a serious and expensive problem. What I am pointing out is that the food-system value of their harvest is almost never counted in the food-systems literature, because the animals do not fit any of the categories the literature recognizes. They are not livestock. They are not game in the managed sense. They are not rewilded proxy species. They are an accident of colonial history that happens to produce millions of pounds of protein per year as a byproduct of pest management. The protein is real. The ecological benefit of the harvest is real. The fact that neither appears in any sustainability assessment I have encountered tells us something about the narrowness of what the assessments are measuring.

Bison are a different case: not an accident but a deliberate restoration, and one already well under way.

The American bison, Bison bison, is the only surviving North American megaherbivore from the Pleistocene assemblage this book has described. It survived the terminal extinction pulse, persisted through the Holocene in herds that European observers described in the millions, was reduced by the commercial hide hunt of the 1870s and 1880s to fewer than a thousand individuals, and has since been rebuilt to a continental population of roughly 500,000, most of them in commercial herds managed as livestock, roughly 20,000 in conservation herds managed primarily for genetic diversity and ecological function.4

The conservation herds are the ones that matter for this chapter's argument. American Prairie, a private land conservation initiative in northeastern Montana, is assembling the largest contiguous tract of rewilded grassland in the temperate United States: currently over 450,000 acres of deeded and leased land along the Missouri River breaks, with a long-term acquisition target that would create a continuous habitat block adjoining the Charles M. Russell National Wildlife Refuge.5 Bison are central to the project. American Prairie's herd, managed for genetic diversity and free-ranging behavior rather than for commercial production metrics, is intended to restore the grazing dynamics the northern Great Plains evolved under: concentrated impact, long recovery, nutrient redistribution across a landscape large enough that the animals' movement patterns can express themselves without fencing constraints.

The food-system dimension is real and deliberate. American Prairie's bison produce meat, marketed under the Wild Sky Beef brand, that is sold commercially as a revenue stream supporting the conservation mission. The animals are not supplemented with grain. They eat what the prairie grows. They are harvested by skilled marksmen in the field rather than transported to a processing facility, which eliminates both the transport stress and the infrastructure dependency. The product that reaches the consumer is, in a meaningful sense, food from a rewilded landscape: an animal that belongs on that landscape by any baseline the fossil record supports, managed under conditions that approximate the ecological dynamics the landscape evolved under, producing food as a function of its ecological role rather than in spite of it.

Tribal nations across the northern Plains are doing related work on a different institutional and cultural foundation. The InterTribal Buffalo Council, a cooperative of more than seventy-six tribal nations, has facilitated the restoration of bison to tribal lands across nineteen states, with a collective herd now numbering in the tens of thousands.6 The restoration is simultaneously cultural and ecological: bison are central to the spiritual and subsistence traditions of many Plains nations, and their return to tribal land is an act of cultural restoration as much as an ecological one. The food produced from these herds feeds tribal communities directly, in some cases through programs that distribute bison meat as a healthier alternative to the commodity foods that federal nutrition programs have historically provided to reservation communities. The survivability profile of these operations is notable: the animals require no purchased feed, no confinement infrastructure, and no industrial inputs beyond the fencing and water infrastructure that any large-herbivore operation requires. The dependency profile is thin. The system functions because the animal and the landscape have a relationship that predates agriculture itself.

Wild-caught fish from intact fisheries represent another existing component that is already producing food from systems the industrial model has not organized. The Alaska salmon fisheries are the clearest case. Alaska's constitution mandates sustainable management of its fisheries, and the state's management regime, built on the stock-assessment methodology that Ray Hilborn and Carl Walters systematized in their foundational Quantitative Fisheries Stock Assessment, is among the most rigorous in the world.7 The system works by monitoring escapement: the number of spawning adults that pass upstream past counting stations to reproduce. Harvest is permitted only when escapement targets are met. In years when returns are low, fisheries are restricted or closed entirely. The result is a fishery that has sustained commercial-scale harvest for decades without the stock collapses that have characterized poorly managed fisheries elsewhere.

Hilborn and colleagues, in a 2020 analysis in PNAS, demonstrated that this kind of science-based management works at a global scale: fisheries under intensive management have, on the whole, been improving in stock status, while unmanaged or poorly managed fisheries continue to decline.8 The finding matters for the food-systems argument because it establishes that wild-harvest food production from intact ecosystems is not inherently extractive or unsustainable. It is extractive when it is unmanaged. It is sustainable when the management matches the biology. Alaska's salmon fisheries produce hundreds of millions of pounds of protein per year from a system that requires no feed inputs, no freshwater diversion, no antibiotic administration, and no arable land. The system's primary requirement is that the ecosystem remain intact and that the harvest be governed by the stock's reproductive capacity. The dependency profile is thin in the same way the bison operation's is thin: what the system requires is the ecosystem itself, and management disciplined enough to not take more than the ecosystem can give.

Shellfish restoration on both coasts extends the marine case. Oyster reef restoration in the Chesapeake Bay, driven by federal and state partnership programs, has rebuilt reef acreage that was functionally destroyed by a century of overharvest and disease pressure. A 2011 synthesis by Michael Beck and colleagues in BioScience documented that approximately eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reef habitat had been lost, and called for restoration as both an ecological and an economic priority: oyster reefs provide water filtration, shoreline stabilization, and habitat for commercially important fish and invertebrate species, in addition to producing a harvestable food product.9 The restored reefs in the Chesapeake, on the Gulf Coast, and in Puget Sound are early-stage examples of what marine rewilding looks like when it produces food: a system in which the harvested organism is simultaneously performing ecological work — filtering water, building reef structure, providing habitat — and producing a food product for human consumption. The dependency profile is, again, thin. The oyster needs the water column. It does not need feed, antibiotics, or industrial infrastructure.

Managed elk and deer populations across much of the American West round out the existing picture. State fish and wildlife agencies manage ungulate populations through regulated hunting seasons calibrated to population surveys, habitat carrying capacity, and predator-prey dynamics. The system is imperfect (chronic wasting disease is a serious and growing problem in cervid populations, and the politics of predator management complicate the ecological picture), but the food produced is substantial. The US Fish and Wildlife Service estimates that hunters harvest approximately six million white-tailed deer per year nationally, producing roughly 600 million pounds of venison.10 Elk harvests in the western states add to the total. This is food from landscapes that have been managed, at least in part, for the ecological relationships the animals participate in. It is not food from a rewilded system in the full sense this book describes — most state wildlife management operates under the 1492 baseline the book has critiqued — but it is food from animals on landscapes they belong to, managed for population health rather than for maximum commodity throughput, and it represents an existing infrastructure of harvest, processing, and distribution that a more deliberately rewilded food system could build upon.

What Could Exist

The distance between what already exists and what could exist with deliberate effort is shorter than most people assume. It is a distance of policy, of infrastructure, and of imagination, not of technology or of ecological feasibility.

Knepp Estate in West Sussex, England, is the most thoroughly documented case of what happens when a conventional farm is released into a rewilding process, and Isabella Tree's 2018 account of the project in Wilding is the single best available narrative of rewilding as a food-producing enterprise.11

The Knepp story is worth telling in some detail, because the details are what make it instructive. The estate had been farmed conventionally for generations: arable crops, dairy cattle, the full infrastructure of a productive English lowland farm. By the late 1990s, the operation was losing money. The heavy Weald clay soils were marginal for arable production, and the costs of maintaining the fertility and drainage that intensive cropping required on those soils exceeded what the crops returned. Charlie Burrell and Isabella Tree made a decision that was, at the time, seen by neighboring farmers as somewhere between eccentric and irresponsible: they stopped farming the land conventionally and allowed it to begin reverting.

What happened over the subsequent two decades is one of the most closely observed ecological transitions in the European literature. The key decision — and the decision that makes Knepp relevant to this book rather than merely to the conservation literature — was to stock the land with proxy herbivores chosen for their ecological role rather than for their production characteristics. Old English longhorn cattle, Exmoor ponies, Tamworth pigs, and fallow deer were introduced, not managed in paddocks or rotations, but allowed to range freely across the estate, creating their own grazing and browsing patterns in response to the vegetation as it developed. The ponies browsed scrub and maintained open rides. The cattle grazed the grassland areas and created the dung-based invertebrate habitat that supports insectivorous birds. The pigs rooted through the woodland floor, creating the soil disturbance that germination of many native plant species requires. The deer browsed the woodland edge. Each species was performing a different ecological function, and the combination of functions produced a landscape that neither species could have produced alone. The results, documented by ecological surveys conducted by Imperial College London and other research institutions, were striking. Species that had been absent from the region for decades reappeared: turtle doves, nightingales, peregrine falcons, purple emperor butterflies, and all five British species of owl now breed on the estate.12 Soil biological activity increased. Water retention improved. The landscape shifted from a simplified agricultural surface into a structurally complex mosaic of scrub, wood pasture, grassland, and wetland, a mosaic that ecologists recognized as closer to the natural vegetation structure of lowland Britain than either the arable farmland it had replaced or the dense closed-canopy woodland that "natural" often implies.

The food produced from Knepp is not incidental. The estate sells pasture-fed organic meat from its free-roaming herds — beef, venison, pork — and the revenue from the meat operation, combined with income from ecotourism and conservation stewardship payments, has made the estate financially viable in a way the conventional farming operation had not been for years. The animals are not supplemented with purchased feed. They eat what the rewilding landscape produces. The meat is marketed as a premium product, and the market absorbs it. The estate demonstrates, at a scale large enough to be taken seriously (roughly 3,500 acres), that a rewilded landscape can produce food, support ecological recovery, and generate revenue simultaneously.

Tree's documentation of the Knepp process is careful about what the project does and does not prove. It proves that a specific piece of marginal English farmland, released from conventional management and stocked with proxy herbivores, produced measurable ecological recovery and a financially viable food product over a twenty-year period. It does not prove that every piece of farmland would respond the same way, or that rewilding is universally more profitable than conventional agriculture, or that the Knepp model scales to feed a nation. What it proves is that the model works: that rewilding and food production are not in opposition, that the landscape can do both, and that the animals doing the ecological work are also the animals producing the food.

Case
The Knepp Experiment

Knepp Estate in West Sussex, England — 3,500 acres of former Weald-clay farmland released from conventional management beginning in 2001 — is the most thoroughly documented ecological rewilding experiment in Europe.

Charlie Burrell and Isabella Tree stocked the land with proxy herbivores chosen for ecological function rather than production characteristics: Old English longhorn cattle, Exmoor ponies, Tamworth pigs, fallow deer. No paddocks, no rotations, no purchased feed. Within two decades, turtle doves, nightingales, peregrine falcons, purple emperor butterflies, and all five British owl species were breeding on the estate.

The meat operation (beef, venison, pork), combined with ecotourism and conservation stewardship payments, made the estate financially viable where the preceding conventional operation had failed. Tree's 2018 Wilding is the narrative record, and the ecological monitoring literature around Knepp continues to grow.

American Prairie, which I described above in its current form, represents the most ambitious version of this model currently under development in the United States. The long-term vision — a continuous habitat block of several million acres along the Missouri River — would create a landscape large enough for bison herds to express genuinely free-ranging behavior, for predator-prey dynamics to operate without intensive human management, and for the prairie ecosystem to function at something approaching its pre-industrial ecological capacity. The food-system implications are direct: bison managed at that scale, on that landscape, under those conditions, would produce food as a function of their ecological role. The harvest would be governed by population dynamics rather than by production targets. The product would be genuinely wild-harvested meat from a genuinely rewilded landscape.

Smaller-scale rewilding efforts on private and public land across the United States represent a distributed version of the same trajectory. Ranchers in the southern Plains who have replaced cattle with bison on shortgrass prairie are running operations that the fossil record endorses and that produce food with dependency profiles substantially thinner than conventional beef. Landowners in the Southwest who are experimenting with managed burro and horse populations on degraded rangeland are testing, in practice, the proxy-selection hypothesis Chapter 7 described. Coastal restoration programs that combine oyster reef rebuilding with regulated harvest are producing food from ecosystems they are simultaneously restoring.

The distributed model deserves attention on its own terms, because the distributed model is how most food-system transitions actually proceed. The shift from draft horses to tractors did not happen by decree. It happened farm by farm, across decades, as individual operators made individual decisions based on what worked for their land and their economics. A shift toward rewilded food production would likely follow the same pattern: individual ranchers choosing bison over cattle on land that responds better to bison, individual landowners leasing acreage for conservation grazing rather than for commodity production, individual coastal communities investing in reef restoration that produces both ecological benefit and harvestable shellfish. Each individual decision is small. The aggregate is a trajectory, and trajectories compound. A hundred small operations producing food from rewilded landscapes, spread across a dozen bioregions, constitute a proof-of-concept network that no single demonstration site can provide. They test the model across soils, climates, regulatory environments, and market conditions. They generate the data that policy needs to scale what works. And they produce food while they do it: food that people eat, food that enters local and regional markets, food that gradually makes the idea of eating from rewilded systems less exotic and more ordinary.

None of these operations is large enough, individually, to register in the national food-supply statistics. Collectively, they represent a direction the food system could move in, with deliberate support, at a pace that is honest about what is currently possible and ambitious about what could become possible.

The Honest Scaling Assessment

The trajectory matters, and stating it honestly matters more.

Rewilded food systems cannot, today, feed every person currently eating from industrial systems. The numbers do not support that claim and I am not making it. The roughly 500,000 bison in the United States, even if all of them were managed as conservation herds on rewilded grassland, would produce a small fraction of the roughly 27 billion pounds of beef the US market consumes annually.13 The Alaska salmon fisheries, impressive as they are, produce roughly 700 million to 1 billion pounds of fish per year, a substantial total, but a fraction of the roughly 6.3 billion pounds of seafood Americans consume annually.14 Feral hog harvest, managed elk and deer harvest, restored shellfish, and the scattered rewilding operations on private land add real volume without changing the fundamental arithmetic. Rewilded food systems today occupy a margin. The margin is real and productive, but it is a margin.

The argument this book is making is not that the margin should replace the center overnight. The argument is about trajectory: about which direction the food system should be moving, about which investments in land, infrastructure, and policy would produce the most durable food systems over the next century, and about what the framework developed in this book reveals when it is applied honestly to the options available.

Three things are true simultaneously, and the honest position requires holding all three.

First, rewilded food systems score well across all four axes of the framework this book has developed. They score well on the economic axis where they have been given the chance to operate: Knepp is financially viable, American Prairie's bison operation generates revenue, Alaska's salmon fisheries support a multi-billion-dollar industry. They score well on the social axis: tribal bison restoration has direct community-health and cultural-restoration dimensions, managed hunting supports rural economies and wildlife management infrastructure, coastal shellfish restoration employs restoration workers and supports fishing communities. They score well on the environmental axis: by definition, a system that produces food as a function of ecological restoration is not trading ecological health against food production but producing both together. And they score well on the survivability axis, which is the axis that matters most for the argument this book has been building.

The survivability score is where the distinction sharpens. Recall the axis as Chapter 4 defined it: survivability asks what a system requires to keep functioning, and what happens when those requirements are not met. An industrial feedlot requires continuous commodity grain, continuous diesel, continuous antibiotics, continuous federal subsidies, and continuous stable climate. A regenerative pasture operation requires most of what the industrial system requires for its monogastric enterprises, as Chapter 6 documented. A bison herd on rewilded prairie requires grass, water, and management. The dependency profile is not zero — the management requires human skill, the fencing requires materials, the harvest requires processing infrastructure — but the profile is structurally thinner than anything the industrial or regenerative alternatives can offer. A system whose primary input is an intact ecosystem is a system that does not collapse when the commodity supply chain is disrupted, because it was never on the commodity supply chain. It does not require diesel for feed delivery, because the feed is growing under the animals' feet. It does not require antibiotics for disease management, because the stocking densities and movement patterns that produce disease pressure in confined systems do not exist. It does not require federal subsidies to make the economics work, because the economics are not built on the gap between input costs and commodity prices that the subsidies exist to bridge.

This is not a theoretical advantage. It is the practical consequence of a food system organized around ecological relationships rather than around industrial inputs. Consider what this means concretely. When the 2022 disruption of Ukrainian grain exports drove US feed costs to levels that Chapter 4 described as existential for some pastured-poultry operations, American Prairie's bison herd was unaffected. The animals did not eat commodity grain. The price of corn was irrelevant to their diet, to their management, and to the economics of their harvest. When the Texas winter storm of 2021 knocked out grid power across the state and killed livestock in operations dependent on electrically pumped water and heated shelters, free-ranging elk and deer in the same state continued to drink from streams and shelter in draws, as they had always done. The resilience was not engineered. It was inherent in the relationship between the animal and the landscape. That relationship predates, and does not depend on, any of the industrial infrastructure that the conventional system treats as a permanent condition. The poultry operation in the Piedmont that opened Chapter 4, the one that scored well on three axes and collapsed when the corn supply was disrupted, could not have collapsed on the fourth axis if it had never been on the commodity corn supply chain in the first place. Rewilded food systems are not on that supply chain. Their vulnerability surfaces are different: they are vulnerable to ecosystem degradation, to climate shifts that alter the vegetation the animals depend on, to disease events in wild or semi-wild populations, to political decisions about land use and access. Those vulnerabilities are real and should not be minimized. They are also vulnerabilities of a fundamentally different kind from the ones the industrial system carries, because they are vulnerabilities of the ecosystem itself rather than vulnerabilities of the industrial substrate layered on top of the ecosystem.

Second, the margin is expandable. The amount of land currently managed for rewilded food production is a small fraction of the land that could be managed that way. American Prairie's eventual footprint, if the acquisition and leasing program reaches its long-term target, would be measured in millions of acres, a single project on a single section of the northern Great Plains. Equivalent projects on equivalent landscapes across the continent — the shortgrass prairies of the central Plains, the basin-and-range country of the Southwest, the Gulf Coast marshlands, the eastern hardwood silvopastoral opportunities — would multiply the acreage by an order of magnitude. The constraint is not ecological feasibility. It is capital, policy, and the cultural willingness to manage landscapes for ecological function and food production simultaneously rather than treating the two as competing uses.

Third, the question is one of trajectory, not of overnight replacement. No serious person proposes dismantling the industrial food system on Monday and replacing it with bison and salmon on Tuesday. The industrial system feeds people. It feeds people badly, in the sense that the system's externalities are substantial and its survivability is poor, but it feeds them, and any transition away from it has to account for what people eat during the transition. The trajectory this book proposes is the deliberate expansion of rewilded food systems alongside — not instead of — the existing infrastructure, with the understanding that every acre moved from industrial production to rewilded production is an acre that scores better on all four axes and is more likely to still be producing food in fifty years under conditions that may look nothing like today's.

The trajectory framing is not a concession. It is the honest version of the argument. A food system that scores well on all four axes, that produces food as a function of ecological restoration rather than in spite of ecological degradation, and that does not depend on the persistence of an industrial substrate whose vulnerabilities the last decade has made starkly visible — that system deserves expansion. It deserves investment. It deserves the policy support and the cultural attention that have historically been directed at the systems it would gradually, partially, honestly replace. The argument is not that rewilded systems are the only answer. The argument is that they are the most underexplored answer, and that the framework this book has developed reveals them as the direction most consistent with the conditions the next century is likely to impose.

From the Framework to the Fork

The systems described in this chapter are not abstractions. They produce food that people eat. The bison from American Prairie and from tribal herds becomes steaks and roasts and ground meat that appears in kitchens and restaurants. The salmon from Alaska's managed fisheries fills freezers and feeds families. The venison from managed elk and deer harvests is processed and consumed by millions of American hunters and their communities every year. The pork from feral hog harvest in Texas, still underutilized relative to the volume available, feeds the hunters who take the animals and, increasingly, supplies processors who sell it into regional markets. The beef and pork and venison from Knepp feeds people in West Sussex and beyond.

What eating from rewilded systems looks like, in practice, is not exotic. It looks like a bison burger. It looks like grilled salmon. It looks like venison stew. It looks like oysters from a restored reef. It looks like food: recognizable, preparable, delicious in the ways that animal protein from well-managed systems is always delicious, because the animals ate what they were supposed to eat and lived on land that supported them. The plate is not strange. The system behind the plate is different, and the difference is what the framework measures.

There is a deeper point here about the relationship between the food and the landscape it comes from, and the point is worth stating because it connects to the book's larger argument about what sustainability means. When a bison grazes the northern Plains, it is not merely producing meat. It is performing the ecological work the prairie requires — the trampling, the grazing, the nutrient deposition, the seed-coat scarification — and the meat is a consequence of that work. When an oyster filters water in a restored Chesapeake reef, it is not merely producing a shellfish product. It is cleaning the estuary, building reef structure, creating habitat for juvenile fish and crustaceans, and the harvestable oyster is a consequence of that function. When a managed deer herd is held at carrying capacity by regulated hunting, the hunting is performing the predator function that wolves and cougars once performed at broader scale, and the venison is a consequence of that ecological substitution. In each case, the food is not the purpose of the system: the ecological function is the purpose, and the food is what the function produces. This inverts the relationship that industrial agriculture assumes. Industrial agriculture organizes landscapes around food production and treats ecological consequences as externalities to be managed. Rewilded food systems organize landscapes around ecological function and treat food production as an output of that function. The inversion is not merely philosophical. It produces systems that score differently on the framework this book has developed, because the framework asks what a system requires to keep functioning, and a system whose purpose is ecological function requires the ecosystem — not the industrial substrate — to keep functioning.

The previous chapter established the principle that the fossil record provides the species guide for which animals belong in which landscapes. This chapter has shown that the principle is not merely a principle. It is an operational reality in the places where it has been given the chance to operate. Bison on the northern Plains are the fossil record's recommendation in practice. Salmon in Alaska's rivers are an intact fishery managed for persistence rather than for maximum extraction. Elk across the western mountains are a managed population of animals that belong where they stand. Even the feral hog — the accidental case, the invasive species converted into food — demonstrates that the landscape produces animal protein whether we plan for it or not, and that the question is whether we harvest it intelligently.

I want to pause on that inversion, because it is the thing this chapter has been building toward. The food systems described in Chapters 5 and 6 (industrial production and regenerative agriculture, respectively) both organize the landscape around the production of food. They differ enormously in how they do it, and the regenerative version is substantially better by every measure this book has applied. But both start from the same premise: the landscape is a production surface, and the question is how to manage the production surface responsibly. Rewilded food systems start from a different premise: the landscape is an ecosystem, the ecosystem has a set of relationships it evolved to maintain, and the food is what those relationships produce when they are allowed to operate. The distinction is not semantic. It produces food systems with fundamentally different dependency profiles, fundamentally different vulnerability surfaces, and fundamentally different relationships to the conditions the next century is likely to impose.

The next chapter takes the serious critics of Pleistocene rewilding head-on. The vision this chapter has described is not uncontested. Biologists have raised legitimate concerns about proxy-species equivalence, about ecological risk, about the cultural and political feasibility of the proposals the rewilding literature has advanced. Those concerns deserve the engagement Chapter 9 will give them. What this chapter has tried to establish, before the critics are heard, is that the vision is not speculative. Parts of it are already here. The animals are real. The landscapes are real. The food is real. The question is not whether rewilded food systems can work: they already do, at the scales where they have been tried. The question is whether the scales can grow, and what it would take to grow them honestly. The framework says the direction is right. The next chapter asks whether the objections are answerable.

CHAPTER NINE

Proxies and Critics

◆ ◆ ◆

In 2005, Josh Donlan and twelve co-authors published a one-page essay in Nature titled "Re-wilding North America."1 The essay proposed, in compressed form, what the previous three chapters of this book have been working toward at length: that the ecological histories of the Americas make the case for restoring large vertebrates, including Old World proxies for extinct Pleistocene species, to landscapes that had co-evolved with them. Donlan and his co-signatories included Paul Martin, Michael Soulé, Dave Foreman, and several prominent conservation biologists. The essay was short, ambitious, and deliberately provocative. It ended with a call for an "optimistic agenda for twenty-first century conservation" centered on the phased introduction of large mammals to carefully selected North American sites.

The response was substantial and, in large part, skeptical.

Within a year, multiple peer-reviewed rebuttals had appeared. The critiques ranged from the procedural to the ecological to the political, made by serious scholars whose concerns were grounded in real knowledge of conservation biology, invasion ecology, and the practical realities of land management. The critiques deserve to be engaged, not summarized, not caricatured, not dismissed. This chapter's job is to present the strongest version of each major critique, engage it on its merits, and arrive at a position the reader can evaluate for themselves.

I want to say at the outset what this chapter does not do. It does not decry the critics. The scholars who questioned the Donlan proposal were doing what scholars are supposed to do: subjecting an ambitious claim to rigorous scrutiny and identifying where the claim outran its evidence. Some objections I accept substantially. Some I think are answerable on empirical grounds. Some miss the larger point, for reasons I will try to articulate. In no case do I think the objections are trivial, and in no case do I think the person making them was wrong. The rewilding proposal asks for much. The scrutiny is proportionate to the ask.

The Proxy-Equivalence Problem

The most ecologically grounded critique of Pleistocene rewilding was articulated by Tim Caro in a 2007 commentary in Trends in Ecology & Evolution titled "The Pleistocene Re-wilding Gambit."2 Caro is a behavioral ecologist whose career has been built on careful empirical work — mostly on African mammalian ecology, where the large-bodied fauna that the rewilding proposal seeks to restore or proxy is still intact. His critique cuts to the heart of the proxy-selection argument this book has been making.

Caro's central concern is what might be called the proxy-equivalence assumption: that a closely related living species will perform the same ecological function as its extinct relative when placed in the same landscape. The rewilding proposal depends on this assumption. Donlan and colleagues proposed that African or Asian elephants could serve as proxies for mammoths, African lions or cheetahs as proxies for the extinct American lion and cheetah, and Bactrian camels or dromedaries as proxies for Camelops. The assumption underlying each proposal is that phylogenetic proximity — the degree of relatedness between the proxy and the extinct species — predicts ecological equivalence. A close relative, the assumption holds, will occupy a similar niche, exert similar effects on vegetation and soil, and restore something like the ecological function the extinct species performed.

Caro argued the assumption is unsupported by available evidence. Ecological function is not a simple derivative of phylogenetic relatedness. Two closely related species can occupy very different niches if they have diverged in diet, behavior, habitat preference, or body size. An African elephant is not a mammoth. The lineages diverged millions of years ago and adapted to different climatic regimes, vegetation communities, and predator assemblages. Placing an African elephant on the Great Plains and expecting it to perform the ecological role of Mammuthus columbi is an extrapolation from phylogeny to ecology that available evidence does not license. The same concern applies to every proxy pairing the Donlan proposal advanced. A modern dromedary is not Camelops. A modern African cheetah is not Miracinonyx. The degree to which proxy actual behavior in the actual landscape would replicate the extinct species' ecological function is an empirical question that cannot be answered from phylogeny alone.2

Caro went further. He pointed out that introductions of large-bodied species into novel ecosystems have, historically, produced ecological consequences that introducing parties did not anticipate and could not control. The history of species introductions is not reassuring. Cane toads in Australia, rabbits in Australia, Nile perch in Lake Victoria—each was introduced with some expectation of ecological benefit, and each produced cascading damage still being managed decades or centuries later. The proxy-species proposal, Caro argued, was asking conservationists to introduce large-bodied animals with enormous individual ecological footprints, capable of transforming vegetation structure and soil dynamics across large areas, into ecosystems that have not experienced those animals for twelve thousand years. The ecosystems have reorganized in the interim. The vegetation communities have shifted. The predator-prey dynamics have stabilized around a different cast of species. Introducing a proxy megaherbivore into a system operating without megaherbivores for a hundred and twenty centuries is not a restoration in any straightforward sense. It is an experiment with uncertain outcome, and the scale of potential consequences, both positive and negative, is proportional to the size of animals being introduced.

I find this critique serious and, in its strongest form, largely correct. The proxy-equivalence assumption is unsupported as a blanket proposition—phylogenetic proximity does not guarantee ecological equivalence. The history of species introductions warrants caution. Caro is right that the burden of evidence lies with proponents, not skeptics, and that evidence as of 2007 was not sufficient to justify continental-scale proxy introductions on phylogenetic reasoning alone.

Where the critique reaches its limit is in what follows from it. Caro's argument establishes that the proxy-equivalence question is empirical—that it cannot be assumed but must be tested. It does not establish that the question is unanswerable, or that the answer will necessarily be negative—it establishes only that the empirical work remains undone. The proposition that a dromedary camel would browse the thorny vegetation of the American Southwest in a manner ecologically similar to Camelops is not a proposition that can be confirmed or denied from phylogeny. It is a proposition that can be tested by putting dromedaries on Southwest rangeland and measuring what happens to vegetation, soil, the animal, and the surrounding ecological community. The test would need to be designed carefully, monitored rigorously, and conducted at a scale small enough that negative outcomes could be managed without ecological catastrophe. It would need control sites and long time horizons, the kind of sustained empirical attention that conservation biology gives to reintroduction programs for endangered species. What it would not need is certainty in advance. It would need a hypothesis worth testing and a methodology capable of testing it.

The proxy-equivalence question is a question about experimental design rather than about ecological principle. The principle that an ecosystem missing its keystone herbivores functions differently than one in which they are present is well established in the ecological literature and has been demonstrated empirically in multiple contexts. The question is whether a specific proxy species, in a specific landscape, restores a specific ecological function to a measurable degree. That question is answerable by experiment, not by argument. The fact that the answer is not yet known is an argument for conducting the experiment, not for declining to conduct it.

Donlan and colleagues, in their fuller 2006 treatment in The American Naturalist, anticipated this line of response.3 They proposed a phased approach: beginning with species that are already present in North America in managed or feral populations (horses, burros, camels in small numbers), moving to species with closer ecological parallels to extinct fauna (bolson tortoises, which have since been the subject of a small-scale reintroduction program), and proceeding to larger introductions only as evidence accumulated from earlier phases. The phased approach directly responds to the proxy-equivalence concern: rather than assuming equivalence and introducing at scale, it proposes testing equivalence at small scale and scaling only what the evidence supports.

The phased approach is the right framework. It also requires honesty about what it can and cannot deliver. A small-scale experiment with dromedaries on a single Arizona ranch would tell us something about dromedary browsing behavior on Sonoran vegetation. It would not tell us what a self-sustaining population of dromedaries would do to the landscape over decades. The experimental evidence accumulates incrementally, and the increments may not resolve the proxy-equivalence question as cleanly as either side would like. What the experiments would produce is data: specific, empirical, measurable data about the interaction between proxy species and the landscapes they are being considered for. The current state of the conversation, in which advocates assert equivalence and critics deny it on the basis of argument rather than evidence, is less useful than a conversation in which both sides work from data generated by carefully designed field experiments.

The Political and Cultural Obstacle

The second major critique came from Daniel Rubenstein and colleagues, most forcefully in a 2006 paper in Biological Conservation titled "Pleistocene Park: Does Re-wilding North America Represent Sound Conservation for the 21st Century?"4 Rubenstein, a behavioral ecologist at Princeton whose work on equid ecology is among the most cited in the field, brought different concerns than Caro had raised. Where Caro focused on the ecological risks of proxy introductions, Rubenstein focused on the practical, political, and cultural barriers.

The Rubenstein critique is, in essence, a feasibility argument. Suppose the ecology works. Suppose the proxy-equivalence question, when tested, yields favorable results for at least some species-landscape pairings. The proposal still requires land, regulatory approval, and the consent of communities living adjacent to the introduced animals, or at least their tolerance. It requires a political and cultural environment in which the introduction of large, potentially dangerous animals onto working landscapes is regarded as an acceptable use of public and private land. Rubenstein and colleagues argued that none of these conditions obtained in the United States of the early twenty-first century, and that the distance between the ecological vision and the political reality was large enough to make the proposal practically unworkable.

The argument was specific. Americans do not, on the whole, want large predators near their homes, livestock, or children. The political conflict over wolf reintroduction in the Northern Rockies, which had been ongoing for a decade at the time of the Rubenstein paper, illustrated the scale of resistance that even a single native predator species could generate. Extending the reintroduction concept to African lions, elephants, or cheetahs, the authors suggested, was to move from a politically difficult proposal to a politically impossible one. The same reasoning applied to megaherbivores. A free-ranging elephant on the Great Plains would be an extraordinary management challenge: property damage, liability exposure, risk to human safety, conflict with existing agricultural land use. None of these concerns were ecological. All were practical, and all were, in the judgment of the critics, sufficient to render the proposal dead on arrival in any real political context.

Rubenstein and Rubenstein returned to this line of argument a decade later, in a 2016 letter to PNAS prompted by Svenning and colleagues' synthesis on trophic rewilding.5 The updated critique noted that the intervening decade had not produced the political or cultural conditions necessary for the kind of introductions the Donlan proposal envisioned, and that the conversation about rewilding had largely shifted from Pleistocene-proxy introductions toward trophic rewilding, a framework that emphasized the restoration of ecological processes (top-down regulation, herbivory, seed dispersal) rather than the reintroduction of specific Pleistocene analogs. The shift, they suggested, itself acknowledged that the original proposal's ambitions exceeded its political reach.

I accept this critique in significant part. The cultural and political obstacles to large-scale megafaunal proxy introductions in the United States are real, substantial, and not likely to be resolved by ecological argument alone. A proposal that requires Americans to accept free-ranging elephants on the Great Plains is not a proposal that will be enacted in any political environment I can foresee. The obstacles are not irrational—they are the reasonable concerns of people who live on working landscapes and have legitimate interests in what lives on those landscapes alongside them. The rewilding conversation cannot proceed as though those interests do not exist or as though they will yield to the force of ecological reasoning.

Where the critique warrants a response, and here the response is more modest than the original Donlan proposal, is in the scope of what is being proposed. The political obstacles that Rubenstein identifies are real for the proposal as Donlan et al. stated it in 2005: a continental-scale reintroduction program involving African megafauna. The political obstacles are substantially different for a more modest version: small-scale proof-of-concept experiments, conducted on private or willing public land, using species that are already present in the Americas, at scales small enough that the management challenges are manageable and the risks are bounded.

Consider what a modest proof-of-concept program would actually look like. A rancher in southern Arizona, one of many whose land is degraded enough and whose relationship to the conventional cattle economy is marginal enough to be open to alternatives, places twenty dromedary camels on a thousand acres of Sonoran desert rangeland under a research protocol developed in partnership with a university. The camels are fenced. The vegetation response is monitored. The soil biology is sampled. The animal behavior is documented. The experiment runs for five years. At the end of five years, the data either support the hypothesis that dromedary browsing produces measurable ecological benefit on that landscape, or they do not. If they do, the experiment is expanded to additional sites, species, and scales. If they do not, the experiment is ended and the hypothesis is revised.

The political barriers to this version of the proposal are not zero. They include permitting, liability, neighbor relations, and the cultural unfamiliarity of camels on Arizona rangeland. But they are barriers of a different order than those to placing elephants on the Great Plains. They are navigable, and in fact are already being navigated in analogous contexts: Bolson tortoise reintroduction in New Mexico, experimental bison management on tribal and private land across the northern Plains, and the ongoing management of feral horse and burro populations on public land in the West. Each of these involves the placement of large-bodied animals on working landscapes under regulatory frameworks that already exist.

The political critique is strongest when aimed at the most ambitious version of the proposal. It is weakest when aimed at the most modest version. The distance between the two versions is not small. One asks a nation to accept a fundamentally different relationship to its landscapes. The other asks a handful of willing landowners and researchers to run a bounded experiment and publish the results. The book's ask is the modest version: not a continental program but a set of experiments, not certainty but data, not political transformation but the kind of incremental, evidence-based expansion that conservation biology already practices with endangered-species reintroduction programs.

The Baseline-as-Fantasy Critique

The third critique cuts deeper because it challenges not a specific proposal but the frame within which all rewilding proposals are made.

The argument, advanced by various scholars with different emphases, is that the Pleistocene baseline is itself a kind of conservation fantasy, a longing for a world that existed under conditions so different from the present that its restoration is not merely difficult but conceptually incoherent. Conservation, under this view, should focus on protecting what currently exists: the species, ecosystems, and ecological processes that are present now, under current conditions, and that face measurable threats from current human activity. The late-Pleistocene assemblage is interesting as paleontology, not a useful reference point for conservation in the Anthropocene. The world has moved on. The ecosystems have reorganized. The question is not how to get back to a Pleistocene state that no living person has experienced but how to protect and improve the ecological communities we actually have.

This is not a fringe position. Versions of it appear in thoughtful conservation writing across the last two decades, and the version embedded in the trophic-rewilding framework that Svenning and colleagues articulated in their 2016 synthesis in PNAS is, in part, a response to the force of the objection.6 Svenning and colleagues shifted emphasis from "restore the Pleistocene assemblage" to "restore the ecological processes that large-bodied animals perform," a shift that sidesteps the historical-baseline question by making the argument functional rather than historical. If a landscape needs top-down regulation by large predators, the relevant question is whether large predators can provide that function now, not whether the specific predators being considered are phylogenetically correct proxies for Pleistocene species.

I respect the pragmatism of the trophic-rewilding reframe. It is a useful bridge between the Donlan proposal's ambitions and the political and ecological realities the proposal confronted. Where the baseline-as-fantasy critique ultimately falls short is in what it asks us to accept.

The argument that conservation should focus on protecting what currently exists takes the current ecological state as its reference point. But the current ecological state of North America, as Chapter 2 of this book argued at length, is not a stable equilibrium that happens to have arrived at its present form through natural processes—it is a depleted state, one that resulted from the loss of more than forty species of large mammals in near time, a loss that reshaped every terrestrial ecosystem on the continent. The vegetation communities are different. The soil dynamics are different. The predator-prey relationships are truncated. The seed dispersal networks are broken. The grasslands that evolved under proboscidean herbivory operate differently without it. The canyons that evolved under camelid browsing are being colonized by thorny vegetation the camelids used to suppress. The pronghorn still runs at speeds calibrated to a predator that no longer exists.

To argue that conservation should protect "what currently exists" is to argue that we should protect the depleted state, that we should defend the post-extinction baseline against further degradation without asking whether the baseline itself represents a condition worth defending on ecological grounds. It is the argument from Chapter 2 returning in a different register: the Columbian Curtain, extended. Except that here the curtain is not drawn at 1492. It is drawn at the present moment, and what it hides is the same thing the Columbian Curtain hides: the depth of the loss and the possibility that addressing the loss would produce ecological outcomes that the current baseline cannot.

Granted, the comparison has limits. The Pleistocene is gone in a way that 1491 is not. The ecosystems have had twelve thousand years to reorganize, and the reorganization is not nothing. New ecological relationships have formed, new equilibria have been reached, and disrupting those equilibria carries its own risks. Caro's concern about the consequences of introducing megafauna into systems that have stabilized without them is a version of this point, and it is a legitimate one. The twelve-thousand-year gap is real. The ecosystems on the other side of it are not frozen in time, waiting for the return of the animals they lost. They have moved, and the moving is itself an ecological fact.

But the moving has not been unconstrained. The ecosystems have reorganized within the limits of what was available to them—and what was available was a diminished cast of species. A grassland that loses its proboscideans does not replace them with an ecological equivalent. It reorganizes around the absence: vegetation structure shifts, nutrient cycling changes, fire regime adjusts, soil biology adapts to a world with less large-mammal disturbance. The reorganization is real, but it is a reorganization around a loss, not a recovery from one. The new equilibrium is a depleted equilibrium. To take the depleted equilibrium as the baseline and to argue that conservation should protect it against disturbance is to naturalize the depletion, to treat the consequence of a mass extinction as the reference condition for all subsequent ecological management.

I do not think this is a defensible position when stated plainly. The fact that it is the de facto position of most conservation practice in the United States does not make it defensible—it makes it familiar.

The response is not to demand that every conservation agency immediately adopt a Pleistocene baseline—that would be as politically unrealistic as the most ambitious version of the Donlan proposal, and for similar reasons. The response is to recognize that the current baseline is contingent, that it results from a specific historical event (the late-Pleistocene megafaunal extinction), that it represents a depleted state rather than a natural one, and that proposals to address the depletion deserve evaluation on their ecological merits rather than dismissal on the grounds that the baseline is the baseline.

What the Wolf Taught Us

There is one case in which the rewilding conversation moved from argument to evidence, and the evidence is worth pausing on, because it is the closest thing the field has to a completed experiment.

In 1995 and 1996, gray wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park after an absence of approximately seventy years. The reintroduction was politically contentious: ranching communities adjacent to the park opposed it vigorously, and the legal and regulatory battles surrounding it lasted years before and after the animals arrived. The wolves were not Pleistocene proxies. They were a species reintroduced to a landscape it had occupied within living human memory, under a regulatory framework that already existed for endangered-species recovery. The case is relevant to this chapter not because it is a Pleistocene-rewilding experiment but because it is a controlled case of restoring a large-bodied animal to a landscape that had reorganized in its absence and measuring what happened.

What happened was substantial. William Ripple and Robert Beschta, in a 2012 synthesis covering the first fifteen years of wolf presence in the park, documented a series of measurable ecological changes that cascaded from the reintroduction through the park's trophic structure.7 Elk, which had been the park's dominant herbivore in the wolves' absence, altered their behavior and distribution in response to predation risk. Riparian areas that had been heavily browsed by elk—willows, aspens, and cottonwoods along streams and rivers—began to recover as elk browsing pressure declined. Luke Painter and colleagues, in a 2015 study in Ecology, documented that the proportion of young aspen browsed by elk in northern Yellowstone dropped from ninety percent in 1998 to thirty-seven to sixty-three percent by 2012, with corresponding increases in aspen recruitment and height growth in areas of high wolf activity.8 The vegetation recovery had downstream effects: stream-bank stabilization improved as willow and cottonwood root systems reestablished, channel morphology began to shift, and beaver — absent from much of the park's northern range for decades — returned to streams where the recovering riparian vegetation provided both food and building material.

The trophic cascade was not simple. Creel and Christianson, in a 2009 study also in Ecology, found that individual elk willow consumption actually increased in the presence of wolves, suggesting that vegetation recovery was driven primarily by elk population reduction rather than by behavioral avoidance alone.9 The mechanism was more complex than the initial "landscape of fear" narrative suggested. What was not in dispute was the outcome: the reintroduction of a top predator to a landscape that had reorganized in its absence produced measurable, cascading, and largely beneficial ecological effects within two decades.

The Yellowstone wolf case is not a perfect analogy for the proxy-species proposals this book has discussed. Wolves are a native species reintroduced to a landscape from which they had been absent for decades, not centuries or millennia. The ecological continuity between the pre-removal and post-reintroduction periods is much tighter than the continuity between the late Pleistocene and the present. The political and regulatory framework for wolf reintroduction, while contested, was grounded in the Endangered Species Act, a legal infrastructure that does not exist for Pleistocene-proxy introductions.

What the wolf case does demonstrate—and this is what I want to hold before moving to the chapter's close—is that the principle works. An ecosystem missing a keystone species reorganizes around the absence. When the species is returned, the ecosystem begins to reorganize around the presence. The reorganization is measurable, cascading, and ecologically significant. The fact that the wolf was absent for seventy years rather than twelve thousand does not change the principle; it changes the degree of uncertainty about the outcome. A twelve-thousand-year absence produces more reorganization, more ecological distance between the pre-removal and the present state, and more uncertainty about what reintroduction would produce.
