The uncertainty is real. It is not a reason to decline the experiment but a reason to design the experiment carefully.

The Ask

The book has now made its case across eight chapters. It has argued that the dominant framework for evaluating food systems is incomplete. It has proposed a fourth axis — survivability — that captures what the existing framework misses. It has applied the framework to industrial systems, to regenerative agriculture, and to the rewilded food systems that score best across all four axes. It has taken the fossil record seriously as a guide to which animals belong in which landscapes. It has described what rewilded food systems look like where they already exist, and what they could look like with deliberate effort.

This chapter has taken the critics seriously. Caro's concern about proxy equivalence is valid: the assumption that phylogenetic proximity equals ecological equivalence is unsupported as a blanket claim and should be tested rather than assumed. Rubenstein's concern about political and cultural feasibility is valid: the most ambitious version of the rewilding proposal exceeds what the current political environment can absorb, and the conversation has to meet that reality where it is. The baseline-as-fantasy critique has force: the Pleistocene is gone, the ecosystems have reorganized, and any intervention carries risks that must be weighed against the potential benefits.

What the book asks for, in light of all three critiques, is not certainty—not a continental program, not the introduction of elephants to the Great Plains or lions to the Rockies, not a demand that the conservation establishment abandon its existing frameworks or that ranching communities accept megafauna on their land without consent.

What the book asks for is proof of concept. Rewilding hypotheses can be tested at small scales: camels on a single ranch in Arizona, managed burro populations in the Grand Canyon watershed, bison on tribal and private land across the northern Plains (where this is already happening, with measurable results), wolves in specific Northern Rocky Mountain ecosystems (where this has already been done, with the trophic cascade data to show for it). Each of these is a bounded experiment that produces data and can be expanded if the data are favorable and ended if they are not.

The proof-of-concept approach does not require resolving the proxy-equivalence debate in advance; it generates the data the debate needs. It does not require overcoming the political obstacles to continental-scale megafaunal introduction; it works within the political space that already exists for small-scale research and land-management experimentation. It does not require accepting the Pleistocene baseline as the one true reference point for conservation; it requires only the recognition that the current baseline is contingent, that the depletion it represents is real, and that proposals to address the depletion deserve to be evaluated rather than dismissed.

The experiments are not hypothetical. Some of them have already been run. Wolves in Yellowstone. Bison on tribal and conservation land across the northern Plains. Bolson tortoises in New Mexico. Feral horse and burro management—contested and imperfect, but generating ecological data in real time about what happens when equids occupy landscapes their lineage shaped. Each of these is a data point that says something about the interaction between large-bodied animals and the landscapes they are placed on. The data are not yet sufficient to answer the proxy-equivalence question for every species-landscape pairing the rewilding literature has proposed. They are sufficient to show that the question is answerable, that it yields to empirical investigation rather than resolving only to argument.

The critics have earned the book the obligation to state its ask carefully. The ask is this: try the experiments honestly, at small scales, with rigorous monitoring and genuine willingness to accept negative results. Try them on landscapes where the fossil record and the current ecological evidence both point to an animal that is not there. Measure what happens. Publish the results. Let the data accumulate. And then decide not from argument, not from longing, not from the fossil record alone, but from the evidence of what actually happens when the animals return to the land that shaped them.

The critics say the proposal is uncertain. They are right. The response is not to retreat from the uncertainty but to design experiments that reduce it. The response is not certainty. The response is science.

CHAPTER TEN

Water and Indigenous Systems

◆ ◆ ◆

The argument this book has been making rests on a particular frame. The Pleistocene baseline, the proposal that restoration should be guided by what the fossil record tells us about the ecological assemblages that preceded the near-time extinctions, is the frame the previous nine chapters have developed, applied, and defended against its serious critics. The frame is, I believe, defensible. It is not the only one.

This chapter exists because intellectual honesty requires saying so. The Pleistocene baseline is one way of answering the question "what should this landscape look like?" It is not the only way the question has been answered, and it is not the only way the question should be answered. Indigenous peoples across the Americas managed their landscapes for millennia between the terminal Pleistocene extinctions and the arrival of Europeans, and the management traditions they developed constitute a parallel intellectual framework for thinking about restoration, one that the Pleistocene frame should be in dialogue with rather than in competition with, and one that the rewilding conversation has been slower to engage than it should have been.

The chapter also exists because the rewilding conversation, as conducted in the conservation biology literature and in this book through Chapter 9, has been almost entirely terrestrial. The oceans are missing. Kelp forests, oyster reefs, pelagic fisheries, the entire marine domain: the water column has been largely absent from popular rewilding discourse, and the absence is a gap the book needs to address before it moves into practical application. The marine systems are as depleted as the terrestrial ones, and the case for their restoration is, if anything, more empirically advanced.

Two expansions, then. The first is a framework expansion: from the Pleistocene baseline alone to the Pleistocene baseline in dialogue with Indigenous management traditions. The second is a domain expansion: from terrestrial rewilding alone to terrestrial rewilding alongside marine restoration. Both expansions strengthen the book's argument by making it less parochial. Neither contradicts what has come before.

A Parallel Tradition

Robin Wall Kimmerer opens Braiding Sweetgrass with a creation story, the Skywoman story of the Potawatomi, and then spends the next three hundred pages demonstrating what it looks like when Indigenous ecological knowledge and Western scientific training are held in the same mind simultaneously.1 The book is not an argument against Western science. It is an argument that Western science, when practiced in isolation from the traditions of people who have lived with and managed specific landscapes for thousands of years, is working with an unnecessarily thin dataset. The observation has force. It has force because Kimmerer is not making it from outside the scientific tradition. She is a plant ecologist at the SUNY College of Environmental Science and Forestry, a researcher who publishes in peer-reviewed journals, and she is making the argument from inside both traditions at once.

What Kimmerer describes, and what a substantial body of scholarship by Indigenous researchers has documented across multiple ecosystems, is a form of landscape management that does not fit cleanly into either the conservation or the agriculture categories that Western land-use thinking provides. The management is neither preservation (leaving the landscape alone) nor cultivation (reorganizing the landscape around a production target). It is something in between, and the in-between space is where the framework this book has been building meets a tradition it needs to reckon with.

M. Kat Anderson's Tending the Wild, a study of Native American management of California's ecosystems, provides the most thoroughly documented case for the scale and sophistication of this management.2 Anderson spent years working with Indigenous elders and with the ethnobotanical and fire-ecology literatures to reconstruct what California's landscapes looked like under active Indigenous management before European contact. The picture that emerges is not wilderness in the sense that the word is usually used. It is a managed landscape: managed through fire, through selective harvesting, through the cultivation of specific plant communities, through the deliberate maintenance of habitat for specific animal species. The management was not incidental. It was systematic, it was transmitted across generations, and it produced landscapes that early European observers described as paradisiacal — open, productive, teeming with game — without understanding that the condition they were admiring was the product of human management rather than the absence of it.

Anderson documented that California's Indigenous peoples used fire as a primary management tool across virtually every vegetation type in the state. Grasslands were burned to maintain open habitat and to promote the growth of seed-bearing grasses and bulb-producing plants. Oak woodlands were burned to reduce competition from conifers, to promote acorn production, and to clear the understory for ease of harvest. Chaparral was burned on rotation to produce a mosaic of successional stages, each of which provided different resources: early successional stages for basket materials and herbaceous food plants, later stages for browse and mast.3 The burning was not random. It was timed, targeted, and calibrated to the specific ecology of each vegetation type. The resulting landscapes were not natural in the sense of being unmanaged, and they were not agricultural in the sense of being reorganized around a single crop. They were something the Western vocabulary does not have a clean word for: managed ecosystems that produced food, fiber, and habitat as functions of ecological management rather than as alternatives to it.

The scale of this management was not marginal. Anderson estimates that Indigenous burning in California shaped millions of acres annually, a figure that challenges the assumption, embedded in the word "wilderness," that the landscapes Europeans encountered were unmanaged. The ecological consequences of the management's cessation after European contact were rapid and, in many cases, still unfolding. The dense conifer forests that now dominate much of California's Sierra Nevada, forests that burn catastrophically in modern wildfires because they have accumulated fuel loads that historical fire regimes would have prevented, are not the forests that existed under Indigenous management. They are the forests that grew in the absence of Indigenous management, the same way the grasslands that Chapter 7 described are the grasslands that exist in the absence of their Pleistocene grazers. The parallel is structural. In both cases, the current state of the landscape is a consequence of a relationship that has been interrupted, and the interruption is legible in the landscape's present condition.

The parallels to the rewilding argument are striking, and they are also incomplete. The rewilding framework this book has developed asks: what did this landscape look like before the near-time losses, and how do we restore the ecological relationships the losses disrupted? The Indigenous management framework asks a different question: how did the people who lived on this landscape between the Pleistocene and 1492 manage it, and what can that management tradition teach us about the landscape's productive capacity under human stewardship? Both questions are valid. Both produce different answers. The difference matters, and collapsing the two frameworks together would be a mistake that serves neither well.

Two Baselines, Not One

The Pleistocene baseline and the Indigenous management baseline are not the same thing, and I want to be precise about why.

The Pleistocene baseline looks backward past human presence on the continent: past the near-time extinctions, past the arrival of the first peoples, to the ecological assemblages that existed before the megafaunal losses. The question it asks is ecological in the deep sense: what was the full complement of species and relationships this landscape evolved under, and what has been lost? The answer involves animals — mammoths, ground sloths, camelids, equids — that no living human on this continent has ever seen in the wild. The restoration it envisions is a restoration of ecological function that predates human management of any kind.

The Indigenous management baseline looks at a different window: the millennia between the Pleistocene losses and European colonization. During that window, Indigenous peoples across the Americas were not passive inhabitants of a depleted landscape. They were active managers of it. The management traditions they developed were responses to the landscape as it existed after the megafaunal extinctions, a landscape already missing its mammoths and ground sloths, already reorganized around a reduced fauna, and managed within those constraints by people whose knowledge of the land's ecology was deep, specific, and accumulated over hundreds of generations. The restoration this framework envisions is a restoration of human-landscape relationships that were disrupted not by the Pleistocene extinctions but by European colonization, forced removal, and the suppression of Indigenous land-management practices.

The two baselines produce different prescriptions. A Pleistocene baseline applied to the American Southwest asks about camelids and equids, the animals the fossil record identifies as co-evolutionary partners of the landscape's vegetation, as Chapter 7 described. An Indigenous management baseline applied to the same landscape asks about fire regimes, about the cultivation of agave and mesquite, about the management of riparian corridors, about the specific practices of the O'odham, the Diné, the Apache, and other peoples whose management shaped the region's ecology for thousands of years before Spanish contact. Both sets of questions are worth asking. They are not the same questions, and they lead to different places.

Lyla June Johnston, a scholar of Diné and Tsétsêhéstâhese descent whose work on Indigenous regenerative land management has drawn attention across disciplinary boundaries, has articulated a version of this distinction with particular clarity. Johnston's research documents Indigenous land management practices across North America that produced what she describes as food forests: complex, multi-story, species-diverse managed ecosystems that provided food, medicine, fiber, and habitat simultaneously.4 The management was not the management of wild landscapes by people who happened to live in them. It was the management of landscapes by people who understood themselves as participants in the ecological relationships the landscape maintained, people who saw their role not as external managers imposing order on nature but as keystone species whose management was itself a form of ecological function.

The keystone-species framing is the point at which the two traditions come closest to each other, and it is also the point at which the distinction between them is most important. The Pleistocene rewilding framework asks: which keystone species are missing from this landscape, and how do we restore them? The Indigenous management framework asks: what happens when humans function as keystone species, when human management is itself the ecological process the landscape depends on? The first question imagines restoration as the return of non-human animals to landscapes they have been absent from. The second imagines restoration as the return of human management practices to landscapes they have been absent from. Both involve restoring something the landscape has lost. What they propose to restore is different.

Notice that the two frameworks produce different models of what restoration looks like in practice. The Pleistocene frame, in its strongest version, imagines large tracts of land managed for ecological function with relatively minimal human intervention: proxy species reintroduced and then allowed to interact with the landscape in something approaching the self-organizing dynamics the Pleistocene assemblage would have produced. The Indigenous management frame imagines something closer to what Kimmerer calls the "honorable harvest": a landscape under continuous, attentive, knowledgeable human management, in which the management itself is the ecological process and the food is what the management yields. The two models are not opposites. They exist on a continuum, and any real landscape restoration would likely draw from both. But the emphases are different, and the difference in emphasis reflects a difference in what each tradition identifies as the primary loss to be restored.

I do not think one framework is correct and the other mistaken. I think they are complementary in a way that neither is sufficient to capture alone. A landscape like the American Southwest has lost both its Pleistocene megafauna and its Indigenous management traditions. A complete restoration framework for that landscape would need to engage both losses: the twelve-thousand-year absence of the camelids and equids, and the five-hundred-year suppression of the fire regimes, the managed harvesting, and the cultivation practices that Indigenous peoples maintained for the intervening millennia. The two losses are different in kind, different in timescale, and different in what their restoration would require. They are both real.

What This Chapter Does Not Do

I want to be explicit about the limits of this engagement, because the limits matter.

This chapter does not speak for Indigenous peoples. It engages the published work of Indigenous scholars (Kimmerer, Johnston, Anderson) on the terms those scholars have set. Their work represents a parallel intellectual tradition with its own epistemology, its own standards of evidence, and its own relationship to the landscapes it describes. The Pleistocene framework this book has developed is not a superior framework into which Indigenous knowledge can be slotted as supporting evidence. It is a different framework, developed from a different disciplinary tradition, asking a different set of questions. The dialogue between the two is valuable precisely because it is a dialogue between genuinely different perspectives, not because one subsumes the other.

This chapter does not romanticize Indigenous land management as a monolith. The practices Anderson documents in California are specific to California's ecosystems and to the peoples who managed them. The practices Johnston describes across the continent vary enormously by region, by nation, by ecosystem, and by the specific cultural and ecological knowledge of the specific peoples involved. There is no single "Indigenous land management practice" any more than there is a single "Western agricultural practice." The diversity is the point. The scholarship this chapter engages is valuable because it is specific: specific peoples, specific landscapes, specific practices, specific ecological outcomes.

This chapter does not instrumentalize Indigenous knowledge as support for the book's argument. The Pleistocene rewilding case stands or falls on its own evidence, as the previous chapters have presented it. What the Indigenous management literature adds is not evidence for the Pleistocene case but a parallel framework that the Pleistocene case should be in conversation with, a framework that reveals dimensions of the restoration question the Pleistocene frame alone cannot see. The conversation is additive. It is not appropriative.

The Water Column

The Indigenous management framework just described establishes something the Pleistocene frame alone cannot: a model of restoration in which the restored relationship, not just the restored species, is the unit of ecological function. Fire regimes, managed harvests, food forests maintained across centuries: these are relationships between people and landscapes that produced measurable ecological outcomes and that were lost not in the Pleistocene but within the last five hundred years. The question the framework raises is whether restoration of this kind can be demonstrated at scales and on timescales that make the case legible to policymakers and skeptics, not just to ecologists and Indigenous scholars who already understand it.

The marine domain is where that demonstration is furthest along. The rewilding conversation, in both the popular and the peer-reviewed literature, has been overwhelmingly terrestrial. The animals that dominate the discussion — mammoths, bison, wolves, camels, horses — are land animals. The landscapes that dominate the discussion — grasslands, forests, deserts, savannas — are land surfaces. The oceans have been present in the book only as a source of analogy (Daniel Pauly's shifting baseline syndrome in Chapter 2, the Alaska salmon fisheries in Chapter 8) rather than as a domain with its own restoration framework and its own set of losses to reckon with. That absence is a gap, and the gap matters for a specific reason: marine restoration operates on a faster clock than terrestrial restoration, and the results are correspondingly more concrete. If the argument this book has been making — that restoring ecological relationships produces food systems that score well on all four axes — needs a proof of concept, the water column is where the proof is most advanced.

The marine losses are enormous. They are also, unlike the Pleistocene megafaunal extinctions, primarily historical rather than prehistoric. They occurred within the window of human record-keeping, often within the last two centuries, and the documentation of what was lost is correspondingly detailed.

Kelp forests are the marine equivalent of old-growth terrestrial forests: structurally complex, biologically diverse, and foundational to the ecosystems they anchor. Kira Krumhansl and colleagues, in a 2016 analysis in PNAS, assembled the most comprehensive global dataset on kelp forest change to date, drawing on more than a hundred time series spanning multiple decades across the world's temperate and subpolar coastlines.5 The synthesis found that kelp forests had declined in thirty-eight percent of the ecoregions studied, with particularly severe losses in parts of the North Atlantic, the southern coast of Australia, and several Pacific regions. The declines were driven by a combination of ocean warming, sea urchin population explosions following the removal of urchin predators — otters, large predatory fish — and coastal water-quality degradation from nutrient runoff and sedimentation. The pattern echoes the terrestrial trophic cascades this book has described: remove the predator, and the herbivore population expands, and the foundation species collapses, and the entire community reorganizes around the loss.

Definition
Trophic Cascade

A trophic cascade is the amplification of ecological change through predator-prey relationships.

When a top predator is removed, herbivore populations expand without control, consuming the foundation species (kelp, grass, coral) those herbivores depend on. The herbivores eventually starve, but not before the foundation species has been destroyed. The system reorganizes around the loss: kelp forests become urchin barrens, grasslands become shrublands, coral reefs become algal deserts.

The cascade demonstrates that ecosystems are held in shape by relationships rather than by species individually. The predator does not merely eat the herbivore. It holds the entire structure in place by eating the herbivore. Remove the predator and the structure inverts. The pattern runs both directions, and the restoration record (sea otters, wolves, large sharks) shows the inversion can often be reversed if the predator returns in time.

The difference between the kelp story and the mammoth story is that the kelp story is reversible on timescales that matter for policy. Where sea otter populations have recovered, through legal protection under the Marine Mammal Protection Act and through active reintroduction programs along the Pacific coast, kelp forests have recovered with them. James Estes and colleagues documented this relationship in foundational work beginning in the 1970s, showing that the presence or absence of sea otters was the primary determinant of whether a given stretch of coastline supported a kelp forest or an urchin barren.6 The mechanism is straightforward trophic regulation: otters eat urchins, urchins eat kelp, and the presence of the predator controls the herbivore, which allows the foundation species to persist. Remove the otters and the system collapses to an urchin barren. Return the otters and the system recovers to a kelp forest. The time required for recovery, once the predator is restored, is measured in years rather than in millennia. The marine trophic cascade operates on a faster clock than the terrestrial one, and the restoration results are correspondingly more visible within a human career.

Oyster reefs present a parallel case with an additional dimension: the harvested organism is simultaneously performing ecological work. Michael Beck and colleagues, in their 2011 synthesis in BioScience, documented that approximately eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reef habitat had been lost, primarily through overharvest, disease, and habitat degradation.7 The loss is not merely a fisheries loss. Oyster reefs are ecosystem engineers, the marine equivalent of the keystone herbivores this book has described on land. A healthy oyster reef filters water: a single adult oyster can filter approximately fifty gallons of water per day, removing suspended sediment, excess nitrogen, and algal biomass from the water column.8 Oyster reefs provide structural habitat for hundreds of species of fish, crustaceans, and invertebrates. They stabilize shorelines against wave erosion. They sequester carbon and nitrogen in their shell matrix. The loss of eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reefs is not a loss of one species. It is the loss of an ecosystem function that maintained water quality, supported fisheries, and protected coastlines across every temperate and subtropical estuary where oysters once grew.

The restoration case is as strong for oyster reefs as for any terrestrial system the book has described. Federal and state partnership programs in the Chesapeake Bay, the Gulf of Mexico, and Puget Sound have rebuilt reef acreage on substrates of recycled shell and limestone, and the restored reefs have shown rapid colonization by native oyster populations, with measurable improvements in water clarity, fisheries productivity, and shoreline stability within years of construction.9 The food-system dimension is direct: restored oyster reefs produce a harvestable food product while simultaneously performing the ecological work the estuary depends on. The dependency profile is thin in exactly the way the survivability framework measures: the oyster needs the water column, and the water column needs the oyster, and neither requires commodity feed, antibiotics, diesel, or federal subsidies to keep the relationship functioning.

The food-system dimension of kelp forest restoration is also worth noting. Kelp forests are not merely habitat — they are productive ecosystems that support fisheries directly. The rockfish, lingcod, sea urchin, and abalone populations that kelp forests sustain are commercially harvested species. A coastline that supports a kelp forest supports a fishery. A coastline that has collapsed to an urchin barren supports neither. The restoration calculus is not an abstraction: it is a calculation about whether a specific stretch of coast produces food or does not, and the variable that determines the answer is whether the trophic structure is intact.

The broader marine picture extends the argument. The distinction between farmed salmon and wild Alaskan salmon is not merely a culinary preference or a marketing category. It is a distinction between two fundamentally different food systems with fundamentally different dependency profiles. Farmed Atlantic salmon — produced in net-pen aquaculture operations predominantly in Norway, Chile, Scotland, and British Columbia — requires continuous inputs of processed fish meal and fish oil derived from wild forage-fish harvests, continuous antibiotic and antiparasitic administration to manage the disease pressure inherent to high-density aquaculture, and continuous management of the waste stream the operations produce.10 A major farmed-salmon operation is, in structural terms, the marine equivalent of a terrestrial CAFO: a confined animal feeding operation in the water column, with many of the same dependency vulnerabilities the terrestrial version carries. Wild Alaskan salmon, managed under the stock-assessment methodology Chapter 8 described, requires the river system to remain intact, the ocean ecosystem to remain productive, and the harvest to remain within the bounds the stock's reproductive capacity sets. The dependency profile is the ecosystem itself. The difference between the two systems, scored on the survivability axis, is the same difference the book has documented on land: a system organized around industrial inputs versus a system organized around ecological function.

The salmon case connects, moreover, to the Indigenous framework this chapter has been developing. Pacific Northwest salmon fisheries were managed by Indigenous peoples for thousands of years before European contact, under systems that sustained both the harvest and the ecological function the salmon perform: nutrient transport from ocean to riparian forest, bear and eagle feeding, marine-derived nitrogen deposition in inland soils. The industrial replacement of those management systems with commodity extraction is a marine parallel to the terrestrial replacement of Indigenous fire management with fire suppression. In both cases, a management tradition that sustained the ecosystem's productive capacity across millennia was replaced by an extractive regime that degraded it within decades. In both cases, the restoration question is not merely ecological but cultural: whose knowledge of the system is being restored alongside the system itself?

What marine ecosystems would look like restored to historical baselines is a question the fisheries-history literature has begun to answer in remarkable detail. Jeremy Jackson and colleagues, in a landmark 2001 paper in Science, assembled historical, archaeological, and paleontological evidence to reconstruct the ecological state of coastal marine ecosystems before the onset of industrial exploitation.11 The reconstruction was sobering. Coastal ecosystems that are now managed as though their current depleted state were normal had, within the last few centuries, supported populations of marine vertebrates — whales, sea turtles, sharks, large predatory fish, seabirds — at densities that no living marine scientist has observed in the wild. The Chesapeake Bay, before industrial oyster dredging, contained oyster reefs sufficient to filter the bay's entire water volume in approximately a week; the current remnant population takes approximately a year to filter the same volume.12 Caribbean coral reefs, before the sequential removal of green turtles, manatees, and parrotfish, maintained algal communities in states that no modern reef ecologist has studied in the absence of the animals whose grazing controlled them. The marine shifting baseline, in Jackson's reconstruction, is at least as severe as the terrestrial one this book has documented.

The convergence of the marine and Indigenous frameworks is worth noting here, because it is not incidental. Many coastal Indigenous peoples managed marine systems with the same sophistication Anderson documented for terrestrial ones. The clam gardens of the Pacific Northwest, intertidal rock-wall terraces constructed by First Nations peoples to increase the productivity of clam beds, illustrate this pattern precisely. Research by Dana Lepofsky, Nicole Smith, and colleagues has documented that clam gardens increased clam productivity by factors of two to four compared with unmodified beaches, through deliberate modification of substrate, tidal exposure, and sediment dynamics.13 The management was not fishing in the extractive sense. It was mariculture: the active management of a marine ecosystem to increase its food-producing capacity while maintaining the ecological relationships the system depended on. The clam gardens were, in structural terms, the marine equivalent of the managed fire regimes Anderson documented on land: human management that enhanced rather than degraded the ecosystem's productive capacity, transmitted across generations, and disrupted by colonization.

Case
The Clam Gardens of the Pacific Northwest

Between what is now British Columbia and Southeast Alaska, First Nations peoples engineered the intertidal zone for millennia through the construction of rock-wall terraces that modified substrate angle, sediment dynamics, and tidal exposure.

These clam gardens increased clam biomass by factors of two to four compared to unmodified beaches, not through extraction but through deliberate ecological manipulation that enhanced the beach's productive capacity. Research by Dana Lepofsky, Nicole Smith, and colleagues has documented systematic construction, transmission across generations, and rapid recovery of productivity under restoration.

Clam gardens represent the marine equivalent of California's Indigenous fire management: human-engineered ecosystems that produced food while maintaining, and often increasing, the ecological health they depended on. The archaeological record of the practice extends back thousands of years.

The marine domain, in short, has its own version of every argument this book has made about land. It has its own missing keystone species. It has its own depleted baselines mistaken for natural states. It has its own trophic cascades demonstrating what happens when the missing species return. It has its own food systems — wild fisheries, restored shellfish, intact pelagic ecosystems — that produce food as functions of ecological health rather than as alternatives to it. And it has been almost entirely absent from the popular rewilding conversation, which has focused on charismatic terrestrial megafauna to the exclusion of the water column that covers seventy percent of the planet's surface.

Integration

The Indigenous management framework adds something the Pleistocene frame alone cannot provide: a model of restoration that includes people. The Pleistocene rewilding conversation, as it has developed in the conservation biology literature, has tended to imagine restoration as the return of non-human animals to landscapes from which humans are largely absent, or at least from which human management is minimized. Indigenous management traditions offer a different vision: restoration as the return of human-landscape relationships in which human management is itself an ecological function. The two visions are not incompatible. A landscape could support both the return of proxy megafauna and the restoration of Indigenous fire and harvesting practices. The proxy species and the management practices would interact (as they always have, throughout the history of human habitation of every landscape on Earth), and the interaction would be the ecology of a managed, inhabited, restored system rather than the ecology of a wilderness emptied of people.

The marine framework adds something the terrestrial frame cannot: completed experiments. The terrestrial rewilding proposals this book has described operate on timescales of decades to centuries. The marine domain has already demonstrated what restoration looks like when it works: kelp forests recovering in years, oyster reefs becoming functional habitat within seasons, fish populations rebounding within a few reproductive generations under calibrated harvest. The clam gardens of the Pacific Northwest sit at the intersection of both expansions: Indigenous mariculture that enhanced marine productivity for centuries before colonization, disrupted by contact, and now being documented in ways that make its restoration empirically tractable.

Both expansions operate within the four-axis framework the book has developed. Indigenous management traditions, where they have been practiced and documented, produce food systems that score well on all four axes — economically viable within their scale, socially equitable by design, environmentally generative rather than extractive, and highly survivable because their dependency profiles are thin and their relationship to the landscape is constitutive rather than instrumental. Marine restoration programs produce food systems with the same profile: the ecosystem remains intact, the management remains disciplined, and the food is what the intact ecosystem produces when the management holds.

The book's argument is not weaker for admitting that the Pleistocene frame is one frame among several. It is stronger. A single-frame argument is brittle: it stands or falls on the validity of one analytical lens. An argument that holds the Pleistocene frame in dialogue with Indigenous management traditions and with marine restoration is an argument with multiple foundations. The frames support each other not by agreeing on everything but by covering each other's gaps. If the Pleistocene proxy-selection hypothesis proves more difficult to test than its advocates hope, the Indigenous management framework and the marine restoration framework remain. If the political obstacles to terrestrial rewilding prove as formidable as Rubenstein has warned, marine restoration offers a domain where the ecological case is already demonstrated and the political barriers are lower.

Part IV of this book, which this chapter opens, is about doing. The preceding chapters made the case. The remaining chapters ask what follows from it: how the framework adapts to where readers actually live, what practical guidance it offers, what must change in policy and culture for the trajectory to shift. The Pleistocene frame asks what the land was before the losses. The Indigenous frame asks what the land was under the management of the people who knew it best. The marine frame asks what the water was before the baselines shifted. Each question produces a different restoration target. Together, they produce a picture of what this continent could be — on land and at sea, in managed landscapes and in rewilded ones — that is richer, more honest, and more defensible than any single frame can draw alone.

CHAPTER ELEVEN

Bioregional and Urban

◆ ◆ ◆

The framework this book has developed does not produce a single answer. It produces different answers for different places, and the difference is not a weakness in the framework. It is the framework working correctly.

The previous chapters built the argument in general terms: a four-axis assessment, a proxy-selection principle guided by the fossil record, a set of restoration frameworks (Pleistocene, Indigenous, marine) that converge on the proposition that food systems organized around ecological function score better across every axis than food systems organized around industrial inputs. The argument, as stated, is continental in scope. It applies, in principle, to every landscape on the continent. What it does not do, stated at that level, is tell anyone what the argument means for the specific piece of ground they are standing on. A rancher in southern Arizona, a fisherman on the Gulf Coast, a family in the hardwood hills of New England, and a software engineer in a Chicago apartment all inhabit different ecological realities, and the framework, if it is worth anything, must be able to speak to each of them in terms that are specific rather than abstract.

This chapter does two things. First, it walks through specific bioregional examples to show what the four-axis framework looks like when it is applied to particular ground: specific landscapes, specific species assemblages, specific food-system possibilities. Second, it addresses the readers who do not have ground to apply it to: the urban readers, the apartment dwellers, the people whose relationship to the food system runs through a grocery store rather than through a pasture. Most readers of this book will be those people. The chapter takes them seriously.

The Arid Southwest

Chapter 7 established the proxy-selection argument for the Southwest through the Savory food-truck conversation and the Grand Canyon fossil record. I will not repeat that material here. What I want to do is take the argument one step further: from the proxy-selection principle to a worked picture of what a food system guided by that principle would look like across the region.

The arid Southwest — the Sonoran and Chihuahuan basins, the Colorado Plateau, the sky-island ranges of southeastern Arizona and southwestern New Mexico — is a landscape whose vegetation is still calibrated to animals that have been absent for twelve thousand years. The thorny, browse-heavy plant communities that dominate the region — creosote, mesquite, catclaw acacia, palo verde, saltbush, yucca, prickly pear — evolved under browsing pressure from camelids and ground sloths whose foraging kept the woody vegetation in check and whose dung cycled nutrients through soils that receive less than twelve inches of rainfall per year.1 The current management regime puts cattle on this landscape. The cattle graze what grass exists, avoid the browse, and leave the woody species to expand unchecked. The result, documented across decades of range-science literature, is mesquite encroachment: the steady conversion of open grassland to dense mesquite thicket, driven not by climate change but by the absence of the browsers that historically suppressed the mesquite.2 The range is not degrading because it is being used. It is degrading because it is being used by the wrong animal.

A food system guided by the fossil record would look different. Dromedary camels browsing mesquite and palo verde on Sonoran rangeland. Horses and burros (already present in the region by the hundreds of thousands, currently managed as nuisance populations on BLM land) reframed as proxy species occupying an ecological role their lineage held for millions of years before a twelve-thousand-year interruption.3 The browse species that cattle refuse are the species the fossil record's animals consumed. The mesquite pods that accumulate uneaten under the current regime are a calorie-dense feed source for camelids whose digestive systems evolved to process exactly that kind of material. The prickly pear fruits that drop and rot are fruits the ground sloths once dispersed. The landscape is not short of food. It is short of the mouths the food was made for.

The products of such a system would be unfamiliar to the American consumer but not to the global one. Camel milk (higher in fat and protein than cow's milk, lower in lactose, and already a significant commercial product in the Middle East, East Africa, and parts of Central Asia) is produced by an animal that thrives on arid-adapted vegetation without irrigation or supplemental feed.4 Horse and burro meat, while culturally unfamiliar in the United States, is consumed widely in Europe, Central Asia, and South America, and the protein is lean, low in intramuscular fat, and nutritionally comparable to venison. The cultural barriers are real, and I do not dismiss them. The ecological logic is also real. The framework does not insist that everyone eat camel. It observes that a food system organized around the animals the Southwest's landscape evolved under would score better on every axis (environmental integrity, survivability, and the ecological dimension of social equity) than the cattle-based system currently imposed on that landscape.

The survivability advantage is particularly sharp. A camel on Sonoran rangeland requires no irrigation, no commodity feed, no antibiotics, and no subsidized grain. It drinks infrequently, tolerates heat that would kill cattle, and feeds itself on vegetation the landscape produces without human input. The dependency profile is as thin as anything the framework can measure. When the corn supply is disrupted, when the diesel price spikes, when the aquifer drops another foot, the camel is still eating mesquite. The landscape is still producing. The system is still functioning, because the system's primary input is the landscape itself, and the landscape has been producing browse for twelve thousand years without asking anyone for help.

The Northern Great Plains

The Great Plains are the bioregion where the framework's prescription comes closest to what is already being practiced. The shortgrass prairie evolved under bison, and bison are still here: roughly 500,000 of them, most in commercial herds, roughly 20,000 in conservation herds managed for genetic diversity and ecological function.5 The proxy-selection question, for this landscape, is largely answered. The animal the prairie evolved under is the animal that should be on the prairie. The question is not whether bison belong on the Plains. It is what happens when you take the relationship seriously.

American Prairie, in northeastern Montana, is the most ambitious attempt to answer that question on the ground. The project, which began acquiring private ranchland and grazing leases in Phillips County in 2004, is assembling a contiguous landscape of more than three million acres — private land, BLM grazing allotments, and the Charles M. Russell National Wildlife Refuge — and managing it for a functioning prairie ecosystem with bison as the keystone grazer. The herd, as of 2025, numbers roughly 800 animals on approximately 90,000 acres of deeded and leased land, managed under a protocol that allows the bison to graze freely across the landscape without the rotational confinement that cattle operations impose. The results are visible from satellite imagery: the bison-grazed sections show a mosaic of close-cropped and tall-grass patches (the heterogeneous structure the prairie's plant diversity depends on) that the adjacent cattle-grazed sections, managed under standard rotational grazing, do not produce. The difference is not management philosophy. It is the behavior of the animal. Bison graze selectively, return to preferred patches repeatedly, wallow in depressions that become ephemeral wetlands used by breeding shorebirds and amphibians, and move across the landscape at scales that create the spatial variability the prairie's full community of grasses, forbs, insects, and ground-nesting birds requires. Cattle, even under the best rotational management, do not produce this mosaic because they are not bison and do not behave like bison on grass.

The food-system dimension of American Prairie is still developing, but the economics of bison production on native grassland are already legible from the commercial herds that have been operating across the northern Plains for decades. A bison cow on native prairie feeds herself year-round. She requires no commodity grain, no supplemental hay in most winters if the range is adequate, no antibiotics (bison are notably resistant to the respiratory diseases that plague cattle in confined conditions), and no subsidized inputs of any kind. The animal's maintenance cost is the grass, and the grass is what the prairie produces without being asked. The calves grow on their mothers' milk and on the prairie's forage, and the harvested animals (processed through the small but growing network of bison-certified processing facilities in Montana, South Dakota, and Colorado) produce a lean, nutrient-dense meat that commands a retail premium of roughly two to three times the price of commodity beef. The premium reflects consumer demand for a product perceived as healthier and more environmentally responsible, and it is large enough to sustain the operations at their current scale.

The framework adds specificity to what this arrangement means in survivability terms. The cattle ranching economy of the northern Plains operates under the dependency structure the book has documented: commodity markets set the price, federal subsidies smooth the volatility, and the rancher's margin depends on conditions the rancher does not control. A bison operation on native prairie operates under a different structure. The animal feeds itself on the prairie's own production. The harvest is governed by the herd's population dynamics and the range's carrying capacity rather than by the commodity cycle. The ecological services the bison provide while alive — the grazing that maintains grass vigor, the wallowing, the selective foraging — are not incidental. They are the ecological process that keeps the prairie functioning as prairie, and the food the operation produces is a byproduct of that maintenance rather than an extraction from it. The survivability score is high not because bison are exotic or novel but because the relationship between bison and prairie is the oldest working partnership on the landscape, and it requires the least from outside itself to keep functioning.

The tribal dimension is equally important and should not be treated as secondary. The InterTribal Buffalo Council, a consortium of more than seventy tribal nations, manages bison herds across reservations from Montana to Oklahoma, and the herds serve cultural, ecological, and food-sovereignty purposes simultaneously. For the Blackfeet, the Fort Peck Assiniboine and Sioux, the Rosebud Sioux, and other northern Plains nations, the bison's return to tribal land is not merely an ecological restoration. It is the restoration of a relationship that was deliberately severed as an instrument of colonial policy. The nineteenth-century slaughter of the bison herds was, among other things, a military strategy to deprive Plains nations of their primary food source and force dependence on federal commodity rations. The return of the bison to tribal management is, in this context, simultaneously a food-system intervention, an ecological restoration, and a reassertion of sovereignty over a relationship the federal government spent a century trying to destroy. The framework scores the tribal bison operations well because the operations score well. The historical weight behind the scores is something the framework does not capture but the reader should know.

The scaling question for the Plains is not ecological but institutional. The land exists. The northern Great Plains contain millions of acres of native grassland, much of it under cattle grazing leases that could, in principle, support bison instead. The animal exists. The ecological relationship is intact. What does not yet exist at scale is the market infrastructure, the processing capacity, and the policy framework that would allow bison production on native grassland to move from a conservation-niche market to a meaningful fraction of the region's protein supply. The USDA's inspection requirements, the geographic concentration of bison-certified processing facilities, and the absence of bison from the commodity-support programs that underwrite cattle production all create friction that is institutional rather than ecological. The constraint is not the landscape. The constraint is the institutional architecture built around cattle, and the question is whether that architecture can be expanded to accommodate the animal the landscape actually wants.

Two Further Bioregions, Briefly

The Gulf Coast and the Pacific Northwest each deserve extended treatment, and each illustrates a different dimension of the framework's application. I will sketch them here rather than develop them fully, because the principles the Southwest, Plains, and New England sections have established apply to these regions with modifications of detail rather than of structure.

The Gulf Coast (from the Texas barrier islands through the Louisiana marshes to the Florida panhandle) is where the marine and terrestrial frameworks converge most visibly. On land, the region's Pleistocene fauna included tapirs, capybaras, llamas, and ground sloths, a Neotropical assemblage whose proxy-selection question is more complex than for the arid Southwest or the grassland Plains.6 On water, the Gulf is where the oyster-reef restoration arguments from Chapter 10 come into sharpest focus: reef restoration programs across four states are rebuilding habitat that simultaneously filters water, stabilizes shoreline, and produces a harvestable food product whose dependency profile is the estuarine ecosystem itself.7 The wild-caught Gulf shrimp fishery, battered by competition from farmed imports whose dependency profile runs through the industrial substrate, is a food system the framework can distinguish from its competitor on the fourth axis alone.8

The Pacific Northwest (western Oregon and Washington, coastal British Columbia) is the bioregion where all three frameworks converge on a single watershed. The salmon is the organizing species: Pacific salmon connect the ocean to the interior through their spawning migrations, transporting marine-derived nitrogen upstream into riparian forests where it is measurable in the growth rings of old-growth trees miles from the coast.12 The terrestrial side runs through the region's extraordinary soil productivity and the Indigenous management traditions Chapter 10 described: the fire-maintained prairies of the Willamette Valley, the camas cultivation, the oak savannas that Europeans mistook for natural meadows.13 The shellfish dimension (Puget Sound's geoduck, Dungeness crab, and Pacific oyster industries) adds marine food systems whose dependency profiles run through ecosystem health rather than the industrial supply chain. The Northwest's food-system potential, viewed through the framework, is a network of interlocking marine and terrestrial systems whose collective survivability depends on the health of the watershed that connects them. Restore the salmon, and the riparian forests receive their nitrogen. Restore the otters, and the kelp forests recover, and the nearshore fisheries follow. The systems are coupled, and restoring one restores the conditions the others depend on.

The New England Hardwoods

New England is a landscape that has been farmed, logged, abandoned, and reforested within the span of written American history. The stone walls that run through second-growth forest across Connecticut, Massachusetts, Vermont, and New Hampshire mark the boundaries of fields that were cleared in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries and released back to forest as New England agriculture moved west or collapsed under competition from the Midwest's deeper soils.9 The forest that has regrown is young by ecological standards (most of it is less than a hundred and fifty years old), and it is missing most of the large-bodied fauna that the region's Pleistocene and early-Holocene forests supported.

The mastodon was the eastern hardwoods' dominant large browser. Its range extended from the Great Lakes to the Gulf Coast, and its feeding behavior (browsing the understory, stripping bark, creating canopy gaps) shaped the structure of the deciduous forest in ways the current forest does not reflect.10 There is no living proxy for a mastodon. The framework does not pretend otherwise. What the framework does suggest is that the New England food system should lean into the ecological character of the landscape it sits on — a forest landscape, not a grassland — and that the food-system possibilities of managed forest are more extensive than the region's current agricultural economy recognizes.

The food-system potential of the New England forest is not hypothetical. It is already being demonstrated at small scale across the region, though the formal economy barely registers it. Consider what the forest produces without being asked: mast crops — acorns, beechnuts, hickory nuts, black walnuts — that fall by the ton across millions of acres every autumn and are consumed primarily by deer, turkeys, and squirrels because no commercial infrastructure exists to harvest them at scale for human use. Maple syrup, already a $150-million-per-year industry in the Northeast, produced from trees that require no planting, no irrigation, no fertilizer, and no annual disturbance: a food product whose dependency profile is the forest itself and a late-winter freeze-thaw cycle. Wild mushrooms — chanterelles, hen-of-the-woods, black trumpets, morels — that fruit reliably in the same forests, sought by restaurant buyers who pay $15 to $30 per pound for a product that grows on dead wood without human intervention. Ramps, fiddleheads, sumac, elderberry, and the broader category of wild edibles that the foraging economy has begun to rediscover but that the formal food system still treats as marginal curiosities rather than as products of an ecosystem's inherent productivity.

What distinguishes these products from niche delicacies is that the ecosystem produces them as a function of its own ecological processes. The maple sap flows because the tree is cycling nutrients between root and crown in late winter. The mushrooms fruit because the mycelial network is decomposing wood and cycling carbon. The mast falls because the oaks and hickories are reproducing. None of these require the forest to be anything other than a forest. The food is what the forest does. The economic question is not whether the forest produces value (it does, continuously) but whether the institutional and market infrastructure exists to capture that value as food rather than leaving it on the ground.

Silvopastoral systems in the New England hardwoods take the argument one step further: not merely harvesting what the forest produces passively but managing the forest as a food-producing ecosystem through deliberate integration of animals under the canopy. Pigs on forest floor, processing mast and rooting through the understory in a pattern that mimics the soil disturbance the extinct peccaries once produced. The heritage breeds suited to this work — Berkshires, Large Blacks, breeds whose foraging behavior and fat-deposition patterns were selected over centuries for woodland production rather than confinement — produce a pork whose flavor and fat profile reflect the mast-based diet in ways that commodity pork, raised on corn and soy in climate-controlled buildings, does not. A pig finished on acorns and beechnuts under a New England hardwood canopy is producing Ibérico-grade pork (the same mast-finishing principle that makes Spanish jamón ibérico among the most valued cured meats in the world) from an ecosystem that is reproducing the mast without human input. The economics of mast-finished pork are not commodity economics. The product commands a premium because it is a different product, and the premium is supportable because the input costs are low: the feed is what falls from the trees.

Sheep and goats browsing the understory in managed rotation add a second layer. The Janzen-Martin anachronism fruits — pawpaws, honey locust, Kentucky coffee tree — are eastern species whose oversized fruits, thick pods, and large seeds are calibrated to dispersal by animals that have been absent for twelve thousand years.11 Cultivating these species in silvopastoral systems would begin to restore a plant-animal relationship that has been one-sided since the mastodons disappeared. The pawpaw, in particular, is a fruit whose commercial potential has been recognized only recently: a custard-textured, tropical-flavored native fruit that grows in the understory of eastern hardwood forests and requires almost no management once established. The tree is shade-tolerant, pest-resistant, and productive for decades. It is also almost entirely absent from the commercial food system because the fruit is soft, perishable, and does not ship well in the cold-chain infrastructure built for apples and oranges. The market limitation is logistical, not ecological. The fruit exists. The demand exists. What does not exist is the supply-chain infrastructure calibrated to a forest product rather than an orchard product.

The New England case illustrates the framework's sensitivity to history in a way the other bioregions do not. The region's agricultural landscape was created by clearing forest. Its ecological trajectory, in the absence of active management, is reforestation. A food-system framework that asks New England to maintain open pasture at scale is asking the landscape to maintain a condition it is actively trying to leave. A framework that asks New England to produce food from its forest (the ecological state the landscape is returning to on its own) is working with the trajectory rather than against it. The survivability implications are direct: a food system aligned with the landscape's own ecological direction requires less energy to maintain than one that fights that direction.

The stone walls in the woods are the evidence. They mark the boundaries of a food system that required constant labor to hold the forest back (clearing, plowing, fencing, draining) on soils that were thinner and rockier than the Midwest bottomland that ultimately outcompeted them. The forest won that contest. It reclaimed the fields within decades of abandonment, and the walls now run through maple and oak and birch as monuments to a wager the landscape refused to honor. The next food system for this landscape should not make the same wager against the same opponent. It should ask, instead, what the forest is willing to produce. And the answer, if you look at what the forest is already producing without being asked, is more than the formal economy has begun to measure.

The Urban Reader

I have been writing, so far in this chapter, as though the reader has land. Most readers of this book do not. Most readers live in cities, in apartments or houses without acreage, and their relationship to the food system runs through a grocery store, a farmers' market, a restaurant menu, or a delivery app. If the book's argument ends at the pasture gate, it loses its audience. The argument does not end there.

The framework this book has developed is, at its core, an evaluative tool. It tells you how to assess a food system (any food system, at any scale) by asking four questions: is it economically viable, is it socially equitable, is it environmentally sound, and will it survive when conditions change? Those questions are as useful to a consumer in a grocery aisle as they are to a rancher on a thousand acres. The consumer cannot raise bison or manage a kelp forest. The consumer can ask, of the food they are buying, what system it comes from, what that system depends on, and whether what it depends on is likely to persist. The framework does not require land. It requires attention.

The urban reader has three forms of agency that the book's argument speaks to directly, and none of them require moving to the country.

The first is purchasing. Every food purchase is a vote for the system that produced it. This is not a novel observation, but the framework gives it specificity that the familiar formulation lacks. The question is not merely "is this organic" or "is this local" (labels whose limitations Chapter 1 documented) but "what does the system behind this product depend on, and how does it score on the fourth axis?" Wild-caught salmon from a managed fishery and farmed salmon from a net-pen operation sit side by side in the same case. The framework distinguishes them. Bison from a conservation herd on native prairie and beef from a feedlot finished on commodity corn both produce a burger. The framework distinguishes them. The distinction is not a matter of taste or preference. It is a structural difference in the systems' relationships to the conditions the next century will impose, and the consumer who understands the distinction is making a different kind of choice than the consumer who does not.

I do not want to overstate this. Individual purchasing decisions, even aggregated across millions of consumers, will not by themselves restructure the food system. The structural constraints — subsidy architecture, commodity markets, processing infrastructure, regulatory frameworks — are larger than any individual's grocery cart. The consumer who buys bison instead of feedlot beef has made a choice that the framework supports. That choice, multiplied, shifts demand. Shifted demand creates market signals. Market signals, over time, influence what gets produced and how. The mechanism is real but slow, and it operates within a larger system that individual choices alone cannot redirect. The purchasing power of urban consumers is a lever. It is not the only lever.

The second form of agency is political. The policy architecture that sustains the current food system — the farm bill, the commodity support programs, the regulatory definitions that Chapter 1 described, the land-management frameworks that determine what happens on public land — is built and rebuilt by political decisions that urban voters participate in. The farm bill is reauthorized roughly every five years. Its provisions determine which crops are subsidized, which conservation programs are funded, which land-management practices are incentivized. An urban voter who understands the survivability framework understands that the subsidy architecture is propping up systems that score poorly on the fourth axis while systems that score well receive little or no support. That voter can advocate for policy that redirects support toward the systems the framework identifies as more durable.

Conservation policy is equally relevant. The management of public lands — BLM rangeland, national forest, national wildlife refuge — is governed by plans that are subject to public comment and political influence. The question of whether feral horses on BLM land are managed as nuisance animals or reframed as proxy species occupying an ecological role is a policy question, not an ecological one. The ecology is what it is regardless of the policy. The policy determines whether the ecology is managed in a way that produces food, restores function, and scores well on the framework, or whether it continues to be managed under assumptions the book has spent ten chapters questioning. Urban voters participate in these decisions. Their participation matters, not least because urban voters constitute the majority of the electorate and the majority of the political pressure on the representatives who write the farm bill and confirm the heads of the agencies that manage public land.

The third form of agency is advocacy. The experiments this book has called for (proof-of-concept trials with proxy species on willing private land, expanded bison restoration, marine-restoration programs that produce food alongside ecological recovery) require funding, institutional support, and public attention. Urban readers are the primary audience for the public-attention component. They are the readers of the journalism, the supporters of the nonprofits, the donors to the research programs, the consumers whose demand signals tell producers what is worth producing. The book's argument reaches the landscape through the people who care about the landscape, and most of those people live in cities.

I want to be direct about this because the alternative is a book that talks past its own readers. A food-systems book that speaks only to people with acreage is a book written for a small and shrinking fraction of the American population. The framework this book has developed is relevant to everyone who eats, which is everyone. The urban reader is not a bystander to the food-system conversation. The urban reader is the majority of the food-system conversation, and the conversation will go nowhere without them.

Specificity as Feature

The chapter has walked through five bioregions, and the five have produced five different pictures. The Southwest wants camelids and equids on browse. The northern Plains want bison on grass. The Gulf Coast wants silvopastoral systems and restored marine ecosystems. New England wants food from its forests. The Pacific Northwest wants its salmon and its managed lowland agriculture integrated across a single watershed.

The variation is the point. The four-axis framework does not produce a single prescription for every landscape. It produces a method for arriving at the prescription the landscape calls for, and the prescriptions differ because the landscapes differ. The arid basins of Arizona and the rain-soaked valleys of Oregon have different evolutionary histories, different vegetation communities, different fossil records, different Indigenous management traditions, and different food-system possibilities. A framework that produced the same answer for both would be ignoring everything that makes each landscape what it is. The framework's value lies precisely in its sensitivity to the specific. It asks: what did this landscape evolve under? What does this landscape need? What does this landscape produce when its ecological relationships are allowed to function? The answers are local. They have to be.

This specificity is also, I think, what distinguishes the framework from the approach it is meant to improve upon. The industrial food system treats the continent as a single production surface: corn from Iowa to feed cattle in Texas to produce beef for consumers in New York. The commodity system is designed to erase local difference, to make a bushel of corn from Nebraska interchangeable with a bushel from Indiana, to make a pound of beef from a Kansas feedlot indistinguishable from a pound from a Colorado one. The erasure of local difference is the system's efficiency, and the efficiency is real. It is also the mechanism by which the system's survivability deficit is produced, because a system that treats every landscape the same cannot respond to the ways in which landscapes are not the same.

The framework proposed in this book asks the opposite question. It asks what each landscape is, specifically, and what each landscape can do, specifically, when its ecological relationships are allowed to operate. The answers are diverse. The diversity is the feature. A food system that produces camel milk in Arizona, bison on the Montana plains, oysters in the Chesapeake, forest-raised pork in Vermont, and salmon in the Columbia River watershed is a food system that is not vulnerable to a single point of failure, because it is not organized around a single commodity, a single animal, a single supply chain, or a single set of assumptions about what the future will look like. It is a food system that survives because it is many food systems, each adapted to its own place, each drawing on its own ecological relationships, each capable of continuing to function when the conditions that any one of the others depends on change.

The four-axis framework does not produce a universal answer. It produces the right question — what does this place need, what does this place produce, what will this place still be doing in a hundred years? — and the right question, asked of every landscape on the continent, produces a set of answers that are collectively more durable, more diverse, and more honestly sustainable than any single answer could be.

The next chapter asks what these answers look like at the scale of an individual meal, an individual household, an individual set of economic constraints. The framework has been applied to landscapes. It is time to apply it to the plate.

CHAPTER TWELVE

Eating Toward Vision

◆ ◆ ◆

The framework is built. Over eleven chapters, this book has assembled a way of seeing food systems that most sustainability conversations leave incomplete — four axes rather than three, survivability alongside economic viability, social equity, and environmental integrity. The question now is what to do with it.

This chapter is not a meal plan. It does not tell anyone what to eat for breakfast. It offers no prescriptions, no ranked lists of approved foods, no weekly menus calibrated to a particular body weight or health outcome. What it offers instead is a set of thinking tools: ways to evaluate what is in front of you at the point of purchase, ways to ask better questions of the people who produce your food, and ways to think about the real cost of what you are buying without either ignoring that cost or pretending it does not matter.

The distinction matters. A meal plan is a finished answer. A framework is a way of generating answers that fit circumstances the author cannot predict: your bioregion, your household budget, your access to producers, your cooking capacity, your values. The framework respects the distance between the person who wrote this book and the person reading it. That distance is real, and any guidance that pretends it does not exist is guidance that will fail the moment circumstances diverge from the author's assumptions.

This is also, it should be said, the chapter of this book most likely to slip into a register the rest of the book has worked to avoid. Practical guidance chapters in food-systems writing have a well-established failure mode: they begin by acknowledging that food choices are complex and structurally constrained, and then they proceed to tell the reader what to buy as if those constraints do not exist. The chapter you are reading was written with that failure mode in mind. Where it succeeds in avoiding it, the framework is doing its job. Where it does not — and the author invites the reader to notice — the draft needs revision.

Seeing Through Labels

The reader who has stayed with this book since Chapter 1 entered it at a meat counter in Overland Park, Kansas, standing in front of grass-fed beef that turned out to be grain-finished. The labels said one thing; the practice was another. The observation that opened the book was that this gap between label and practice is not a failure of the labeling system — it is what the labeling system is designed to do. Labels communicate less, not more, because communicating more would make visible the practices that the current food system depends on remaining invisible.

Eleven chapters later, the reader has the framework to see through this. The four-axis assessment does what labels cannot: it asks not just whether an animal was raised on pasture or whether a crop was grown without synthetic pesticides, but whether the system that produced the food can survive the removal of its key inputs. A label tells you what a product is, within a narrow and often misleading set of categories. The framework asks what a product requires — and what happens when those requirements are not met.

Consider a carton of eggs labeled "cage-free." The term certifies a specific thing: the hens were not kept in battery cages.^[1] It does not certify that they went outdoors. It does not certify what they were fed. It does not certify the stocking density of the barn they were housed in. It does not address survivability at all. The operation may depend entirely on commodity soy feed shipped from a thousand miles away, and the label will not tell you this.^[2] The consumer who reads "cage-free" and imagines hens on grass is not being stupid. They are being managed. The label is doing exactly what it was designed to do: communicating enough to justify a price premium while leaving the structural dependencies of the operation invisible.

The framework changes this. When you stand in front of that carton and ask the four-axis questions — what does this system require economically, what are its social conditions, what is its environmental footprint, and what happens to it under stress — the label becomes what it actually is: a single data point in a much larger assessment. It is not worthless. Cage-free is better than battery-caged, for the hens and for the person eating the eggs. But it is radically incomplete, and treating it as a sufficient guide to a food system's sustainability is the mistake the labeling architecture is designed to encourage.

The same analysis applies across the label landscape. "Organic" certifies the absence of specific synthetic inputs but does not certify biodiversity, does not address survivability, and does not distinguish between a diversified small farm and a ten-thousand-acre monoculture that has simply substituted approved inputs for prohibited ones.^[3] "Grass-fed" has been diluted by the USDA's 2016 withdrawal of the federal standard, leaving the term's meaning dependent on which certifying body (if any) stands behind it.^[4] "Pasture-raised" means something different depending on whether it appears on a chicken label (where meaningful outdoor access standards exist through some third-party certifiers) or a beef label (where the term has no federal definition at all).^[5] "Humane" is perhaps the most variable of all: the gap between the American Humane Certified standard and the Animal Welfare Approved standard is enormous, and the consumer who sees "humane" on both products has no way to know this from the label alone.^[6]

The pattern extends beyond animal products. "Natural" on a processed-food label has no federal definition beyond the prohibition of artificial flavors, added colors, and synthetic substances — a bar so low that high-fructose corn syrup from an industrial monoculture system qualifies.^[17] "Sustainable" appears on seafood labels backed by certifiers whose standards range from rigorous stock-assessment science to industry self-reporting.^[18] "Local" has no regulated definition at all; a product grown three hundred miles away in a system identical to the industrial commodity system it purports to oppose may be labeled "local" in any jurisdiction where the seller chooses to do so.

Each of these terms occupies the same structural position. Each certifies something real but narrow. Each communicates less than the consumer assumes. And each leaves the survivability axis, the question the framework adds to the standard three, entirely unaddressed. No label currently in widespread use tells the consumer what happens to the system that produced this food when its key inputs are disrupted.

None of this means labels are useless. It means they are insufficient. The framework does not replace labels; it contextualizes them. A label is a claim about one dimension of a food system. The framework asks about all four dimensions, and it asks about the dependencies that no label is designed to reveal.

What to Ask a Producer

Labels mediate between producers and consumers at scale. They exist because most people buying food will never meet the person who produced it.
