But where direct purchasing is possible (at a farmers' market, through a CSA, from a ranch that sells direct, from a fishing cooperative), the framework generates a different set of questions than the ones most consumers have been taught to ask.

The conventional questions are about outcomes: Is this grass-fed? Is this organic? Were these hens on pasture? These are reasonable questions, and the answers matter. But they are questions about the product. The framework generates questions about the system.

What does your operation require to function? This is the survivability question, translated into a conversation. A producer who raises cattle on pasture and finishes them on grain purchased from a regional feed mill has a different dependency profile than a producer who raises cattle entirely on managed grassland with no purchased inputs. Both may produce excellent beef. Both may be doing good work. But their operations have different vulnerabilities, and the consumer who understands this is making a more informed choice than the consumer who knows only that both products are "grass-fed." The question is not accusatory. It is genuinely curious, in the way that Chapter 4 framed the survivability axis, not as a judgment but as a description of what a system needs to persist.

How many species does your operation depend on? This is a question most producers have not been asked before, and the answer is often revealing. A cattle ranch running a single breed on monoculture pasture has a different ecological profile than a diversified operation running cattle, sheep, and poultry across managed multi-species grassland. Ecological diversity on the production side correlates with resilience, a principle the book has traced from Pleistocene ecosystems through to the present. The producer whose operation depends on a single species, a single breed, a single feed source, and a single market channel has concentrated risk in the same way a monoculture concentrates risk in crop agriculture. The producer who has diversified across species, markets, and input sources has distributed it.

Where does your feed come from? For any operation that purchases feed (and as Chapter 6 established, even the best pasture-based poultry operations purchase substantial quantities), this question reveals the structural tether to the commodity system. The answer is not disqualifying. A producer who buys feed from a regional mill sourcing from regional farms has a shorter, more resilient supply chain than a producer whose feed originates in the industrial commodity belt. Neither is fully independent of the larger system, but the degree of dependence differs, and that difference matters on the survivability axis.^[7]

What happens to your operation if diesel doubles in price? If your primary feed supplier goes under? If the county changes its zoning? These are stress-test questions, and they sound unusual in the context of buying a dozen eggs. But they are the questions the framework generates, because survivability is about what happens under stress, not what happens under current conditions. A producer who has thought about these questions — who has contingency plans, who has diversified their inputs, who has reduced their dependence on any single external system — is running an operation that scores differently on the fourth axis than a producer who has not.

What does your land look like in twenty years if you keep doing what you are doing? This is the environmental-integrity question extended through time. Soil health, water quality, biodiversity, carbon sequestration: these are trajectory questions, not snapshot questions. A producer who is building soil organic matter year over year is on a different trajectory than a producer who is mining it, even if both operations look similar today.^[8]

These questions are not tests. They are conversations. A producer who answers them openly is telling you something valuable about their operation. A producer who is uncomfortable with them is also telling you something, though the discomfort may simply reflect the fact that most customers do not ask questions like these and the producer has not had occasion to articulate what they know.

The questions work differently in different purchasing contexts. At a farmers' market, they are face-to-face conversations. Through a CSA, they might be questions posed in a newsletter or at a farm visit. For direct-purchase meat or seafood, they might be part of the research a buyer does before committing to a quarter-beef or a fishing share. The form adapts; the substance is the same. The framework generates questions about systems, not just about products.

A note on the limits of this approach: direct purchasing is not available to everyone. The farmers' market with the knowledgeable rancher selling quarter-beefs is not equally distributed across geography, income levels, or time availability. The questions above are tools for contexts where they apply. In contexts where they do not (where the grocery store is the only option, where the nearest farmers' market is forty miles away, where the household's schedule does not permit a Saturday morning at a farm stand), the framework still operates, but it operates on the information available rather than on the information ideal. A reader who applies the four-axis assessment to the options actually in front of them at a conventional grocery store is using the framework correctly. The framework does not require access to a particular kind of retail environment. It requires a way of thinking about whatever retail environment the reader has.

Thinking Bioregionally

Chapter 10 introduced the principle that restoration looks different in different landscapes: that the Pleistocene baseline generates different answers in the arid Southwest than in the Pacific Northwest, and that Indigenous management traditions provide a parallel framework adapted to specific places over millennia. The same principle applies to food choices. What scores well on the four axes in one bioregion may score differently in another, because the ecological and economic conditions that determine survivability are local.

In the Pacific Northwest, wild-caught salmon from well-managed fisheries represents one of the highest-scoring protein sources available on all four axes. The fishery is ecologically adapted, the management science is among the most rigorous in the world, the economic structure supports coastal communities, and the system's survivability depends on intact river ecosystems rather than industrial inputs.^[19] In the landlocked interior of the country, that same salmon arrives by truck or plane, its survivability profile degraded by the transportation infrastructure it now depends on. The fish is the same; the system that delivers it is not.

In the Southern Plains, the feral hog population represents an ecological problem (invasive, destructive, and growing) that is simultaneously a food-system opportunity. Wild-harvested feral hog scores at the top of the survivability axis: the protein source requires no feed, no housing, no veterinary inputs, and no supply chain beyond the harvest itself. It also addresses an ecological damage vector. The framework scores this differently than a Pacific Northwest reader's access to managed salmon, but both represent the same principle: the highest-scoring food choices are often the ones most adapted to the specific ecological conditions of the place where the reader lives.

Case
The Feral Hog Paradox

Feral hogs descended from nineteenth-century escaped domestic stock and twentieth-century imported Eurasian wild boar now number roughly 2.6 million in Texas alone, with rapidly expanding populations across the Southeast and lower Midwest. USDA estimates the national herd at six to nine million animals and growing.

They cause roughly $1.5 billion in annual agricultural damage in the United States, destroy ground-nesting bird habitat, and accelerate soil erosion on the landscapes they colonize. They are, by any standard ecological accounting, an invasive species problem.

They are also a high-scoring protein source: wild-harvested feral hog requires zero feed, zero housing, zero veterinary care, and no supply chain beyond harvest. The harvest addresses the ecological damage while producing food. The contradiction is not a contradiction. It is what it looks like when a damage vector is also a food system.

This is not an argument for localism as ideology. It is an argument for localism as ecological logic. The framework does not score "local" as inherently superior. A greenhouse tomato grown a mile away using natural gas heating and hydroponic nutrient solution may score worse on survivability than a field tomato shipped from a region where tomatoes grow without climate-controlled infrastructure. What the framework scores is the fit between a food system and the ecological, economic, and social conditions of the place where it operates. That fit is often local, because ecological conditions are local. But "local" is a geographic descriptor, not a sustainability credential, and the framework treats it as such.

The bioregional principle also connects to Chapter 10's discussion of Indigenous management traditions. Where those traditions are accessible and active — through tribal food sovereignty projects, through Indigenous-managed fisheries and wild-harvest cooperatives, through the growing body of work that Robin Wall Kimmerer and M. Kat Anderson have documented — they represent a knowledge base calibrated to specific places over centuries. A reader in the Great Lakes region who has access to manoomin (wild rice) harvested under Anishinaabe management traditions is encountering a food system that has been adapted to its specific ecological context for longer than the agricultural systems surrounding it have existed. The framework scores this favorably not because it is Indigenous — identity is not a sustainability metric — but because the system's ecological fit, survivability profile, and intergenerational knowledge base are measurable strengths on the axes the framework evaluates.

Thinking About Cost

This is the section of this chapter most likely to go wrong, so it is worth being direct about what it will and will not do.

It will not pretend that food from well-managed, ecologically sound, survivable systems costs the same as food from industrial systems. It does not. A dozen eggs from a pasture-based operation with diversified feed sources will cost more at the point of purchase than a dozen eggs from an industrial cage-free barn. A pound of grass-finished beef from a ranch that purchases no commodity feed will cost more than a pound of conventional ground beef. Wild-caught Alaskan salmon will cost more than farmed Atlantic salmon. This is true, and pretending otherwise would insult the reader's intelligence and their lived experience.

It will not lecture anyone about priorities. The financial constraints people operate under are real, they are various, and they are not the business of this book to judge. A household spending thirty percent of its income on housing in a city where wages have not kept pace with costs is not making a lifestyle choice when it buys the cheaper eggs. It is navigating a set of structural conditions that no individual purchasing decision can resolve. The framework is a tool for thinking, and it is only useful if it can be applied honestly within the actual circumstances of the person using it.

What the cost discussion can do honestly is make visible what the price difference represents.

Industrial food is cheap at the point of purchase because a substantial share of its actual costs are externalized: borne by someone other than the buyer.^[9] The environmental remediation costs of CAFO waste lagoons are borne by municipalities and by the communities living near them. The healthcare costs associated with antibiotic resistance, which the FDA's own reporting links to agricultural antibiotic use, are borne by the healthcare system and by the individuals who develop resistant infections.^[10] The soil-depletion costs of continuous monoculture are borne by future farmers who will inherit degraded land. The subsidy costs that keep commodity corn and soy artificially cheap are borne by taxpayers. A 2019 analysis by the Rockefeller Foundation estimated that the total externalized cost of the U.S. food system (including environmental, health, and social impacts) exceeds $2.1 trillion annually, roughly three times the direct consumer expenditure on food.^[11]

Definition
Externalized Costs

Externalized costs are expenses borne by parties who are not party to the transaction that produced them.

When an industrial hog operation exports waste to groundwater, the cost of remediation falls to municipalities and affected residents rather than to the operator. When agricultural antibiotic use produces resistant bacteria, the cost appears as healthcare spending rather than as feed expense. When federal commodity subsidies hold corn below production cost, the cost is transferred from producer to taxpayer. When monoculture depletes topsoil, the cost is transferred to future farmers.

The price at checkout reflects only the portion of total cost the producer has internalized. The 2019 Rockefeller Foundation analysis this chapter cites estimated the externalized cost of the U.S. food system at roughly $2.1 trillion annually, approximately three times the direct consumer expenditure on food.

This does not mean industrial food is secretly more expensive than pastured food, if you do the math correctly. It means the cost comparison at the register is structurally misleading. The cheaper product is cheaper in part because some of its costs have been removed from the price and distributed elsewhere in the economy. The more expensive product is more expensive in part because it has internalized costs that the cheaper product has externalized. When a consumer pays $8 for a dozen pastured eggs instead of $3 for a dozen industrial eggs, some portion of that $5 difference represents costs the industrial operation has offloaded onto the public.^[12]

Understanding this does not change anyone's grocery budget. It changes the frame within which the budget operates. The question shifts from "why is the good food so expensive?" to "why is the cheap food so cheap, and who is paying the difference?" This is not a rhetorical question. It has answers, and the answers are specific: taxpayers, through commodity subsidies; rural communities, through environmental degradation and depressed wages; future generations, through topsoil depletion and aquifer drawdown; the healthcare system, through the downstream costs of antibiotic resistance and diet-related disease.

Within that frame, the practical question becomes: given my actual budget and my actual constraints, where do the highest-return choices lie? The framework suggests a way of thinking about this that respects financial reality.

Not all four-axis improvements cost more. Some of the highest-survivability food choices are among the cheapest. Dried beans and lentils — which require minimal processing, store indefinitely, grow in diverse climates, fix their own nitrogen, and depend on almost no external inputs — score remarkably well across all four axes and cost less per serving than almost any animal protein.^[13] Whole grains from regional mills, where available, offer better survivability profiles than processed grain products at comparable or lower cost. Frozen vegetables, which retain most of their nutritional value and eliminate the waste associated with fresh produce that spoils before it is eaten, are an economically efficient choice that the fresh-is-always-better orthodoxy tends to obscure.^[14]

Where animal products are concerned, the cost gradient correlates with the degree to which the operation has internalized its costs. The choices that represent the largest per-dollar improvement over industrial systems are often not the most expensive options on the shelf. Choosing conventional eggs from a regional producer over conventional eggs shipped from a distant industrial operation may represent a meaningful survivability improvement at minimal additional cost. Choosing whole chickens over boneless breasts (and learning to use the entire animal) reduces the per-serving cost of higher-quality poultry substantially. Buying less meat of better provenance, rather than the same quantity at a higher price point, is a strategy that the framework supports and that household budgets can often accommodate.^[15]

The wild-harvest and restoration-based protein sources that Chapter 8 described — feral hog in Texas, managed deer and elk across much of the West, wild-caught fish from well-managed fisheries — represent a different cost structure entirely. For households with access to hunting or fishing, these are among the most affordable protein sources available, and they score at the top of the survivability axis because they depend on intact ecosystems rather than industrial inputs.^[16] The barriers here are access and knowledge rather than price, and they are barriers that communities, not individuals, are best positioned to address.

There is also the question of time, which functions as a cost even when it does not appear on a receipt. Cooking from whole ingredients — whole chickens rather than boneless breasts, dried beans rather than canned, whole grains rather than processed — costs less money and more time. For households where time is the binding constraint, this trade-off is not trivially navigable, and the framework does not pretend otherwise. What the framework can do is make visible that the processed-food economy has externalized time costs onto the consumer in the same way the industrial food system has externalized environmental costs onto the public: by creating a default in which the time-saving option is also the option most deeply embedded in the industrial supply chain. Understanding this structure does not add hours to anyone's day. It does clarify why the choices available at the intersection of limited time and limited budget tend to cluster at the low end of the survivability axis — and why changing this requires structural intervention, not individual willpower.^[20]

The honest position on cost is this: the framework cannot make sustainably produced food cheaper than it is. What it can do is help a reader identify which choices, within their actual budget, represent the highest improvement across the four axes. Some of those choices cost more. Some cost the same. Some cost less. The framework makes the differences visible without pretending the reader's constraints are not real.

What the Framework Cannot Do

The framework is a tool for individual decisions. It helps a reader evaluate what is in front of them, ask better questions, and make choices that are more fully informed than the ones the labeling system is designed to support. This is worth doing. Individual choices aggregate, and the direction they aggregate in matters.

But individual choices, even fully informed ones, cannot produce the systemic changes the framework reveals as necessary. The reader who buys pastured eggs instead of industrial eggs is making a better choice on all four axes. They are not, by that choice, reforming the subsidy structure that makes industrial eggs artificially cheap. They are not changing the zoning laws that make small-scale pastured poultry operations difficult to establish near population centers. They are not funding the research that would test proxy-species reintroductions at meaningful scale. They are not restructuring the commodity feed system that tethers even the best regenerative operations to industrial inputs.

Consider the structural ceiling that Chapter 6 described. The best pasture-based poultry operation in the country still purchases tens of thousands of pounds of commodity feed annually, most of it produced under industrial monoculture conditions. No amount of consumer preference for pastured chicken, expressed at any price point, changes this structural dependency. The dependency exists because the alternative (a poultry operation that grows its own feed on-site, at the scale needed to support commercial production) requires land, labor, and capital configurations that the current agricultural economy does not reward and current zoning law often prohibits. The consumer's choice is constrained by a structure the consumer's choice cannot modify.

The gap between what individual choices can accomplish and what systemic change requires is the subject of the next chapter. It is worth naming here because the alternative (the suggestion that conscious consumerism is sufficient to transform a food system) is a form of dishonesty the framework does not permit. The framework reveals structural dependencies. Structural dependencies require structural responses.

This is not a reason for despair, and it is not a reason to conclude that individual choices are meaningless. It is a reason to hold both things at once: the choices matter, and they are not enough. The reader who has followed this book through eleven chapters of framework, evidence, and vision is equipped to make better choices at the point of purchase. They are also equipped to understand why better choices at the point of purchase, alone, will not produce the food systems this book has described as possible.

What those systems require (at the level of policy, culture, economics, and institutional will) is the question Chapter 13 takes up. The framework has done what a framework can do. It has made visible what was hidden, connected what was fragmented, and given the reader a way of seeing that the labeling architecture was designed to prevent. What remains is whether the seeing produces not just better shopping but better systems.

The reader who stood at the meat counter in Chapter 1, confused by labels that communicated less than they appeared to, now has something the labels were never going to provide: a way of asking what a food system actually requires, and what happens when those requirements are not met. That is not a meal plan. It is something more durable — a way of thinking that survives the next relabeling, the next marketing cycle, the next round of standards dilution. The framework is portable. It travels with the reader who has internalized it, into whatever purchasing context they encounter next.

The framework, applied honestly, produces this double awareness. It equips the reader to make better individual choices. It simultaneously reveals why individual choices are insufficient. Both of these are real. The temptation to collapse one in favor of the other, to conclude either that individual action is everything or that individual action is nothing, is a temptation the framework resists, because the framework is descriptive. It describes what is actually happening in the systems that produce food. What is actually happening is that individual choices matter and structural conditions matter more.

That is what this chapter can offer. Not a prescription, but a practice of seeing.

Notes

[1]: USDA Agricultural Marketing Service. "Shell Eggs from Farm to Table." The USDA defines cage-free as hens housed in a building or enclosed area with unlimited access to food and water during the laying cycle, but does not require outdoor access. Updated 2023.

[2]: Lusk, J. L. (2019). Consumer preferences for cage-free eggs and impacts of retailer pledges. Agribusiness, 35(2), 129–148. Lusk's work documents the gap between consumer perception of cage-free claims and the actual production conditions the label certifies.

[3]: Seufert, V., Ramankutty, N., & Foley, J. A. (2012). Comparing the yields of organic and conventional agriculture. Nature, 485(7397), 229–232. The study notes that organic certification addresses input restrictions but does not directly measure biodiversity outcomes or system resilience.

[4]: The USDA withdrew its grass-fed marketing claim standard in 2016, leaving the term's definition to individual certifying bodies. The American Grassfed Association, the Food Alliance, and A Greener World each maintain their own standards with different requirements for pasture access, supplemental feeding, and finishing practices. See: Cheung, R., & McMahon, P. (2017). "Back to Grass: The Market Potential for U.S. Grassfed Beef." Stone Barns Center for Food & Agriculture.

[5]: USDA Food Safety and Inspection Service labeling guidelines permit "pasture-raised" claims on poultry when supported by producer documentation, but the agency does not define the term with specific stocking-density or access requirements. Third-party certifiers such as Humane Farm Animal Care (Certified Humane) set specific standards — 108 square feet per bird for poultry — that the federal label does not require. No comparable federal or widely adopted third-party standard governs "pasture-raised" claims on beef.

[6]: The American Humane Certified program permits indoor-only production systems for poultry and does not require outdoor access. The Animal Welfare Approved program (now A Greener World) requires continuous outdoor pasture access and prohibits beak trimming. Both programs certify products as "humane." See: Consumer Reports. (2017). "Food Labels Survey." Consumers Union.

[7]: The National Sustainable Agriculture Coalition's 2022 report on regional feed systems documents the supply-chain resilience advantages of locally and regionally sourced animal feed, including shorter transportation distances, reduced exposure to commodity-market price volatility, and stronger producer relationships that permit quality verification. NSAC. (2022). "Building Regional Feed Supply Chains."

[8]: LaCanne, C. E., & Lundgren, J. G. (2018). Regenerative agriculture: merging farming and natural resource conservation profitably. PeerJ, 6, e4428. The study documents measurable differences in soil organic matter, water infiltration, and insect biodiversity between regenerative and conventional operations in the Northern Plains over a multi-year assessment period.

[9]: Hendrickson, M. K. (2015). Resilience in a concentrated and consolidated food system. Journal of Environmental Studies and Sciences, 5(3), 418–431. Hendrickson's analysis documents how concentration in the food system distributes environmental and social costs away from the point of sale and onto communities, ecosystems, and public budgets.

[10]: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. (2019). Antibiotic Resistance Threats in the United States, 2019. The report estimates more than 2.8 million antibiotic-resistant infections and 35,000 deaths annually in the U.S. and identifies agricultural antibiotic use as a contributing factor in the development and spread of resistant organisms. FDA. (2022). Summary Report on Antimicrobials Sold or Distributed for Use in Food-Producing Animals. Documents that approximately 6.0 million kilograms of medically important antimicrobials were sold for food-animal use in 2021.

[11]: Rockefeller Foundation. (2021). True Cost of Food: Measuring What Matters to Transform the U.S. Food System. The report estimates that the total cost of the U.S. food system — including health impacts ($1.1 trillion), environmental impacts ($900 billion), and biodiversity and livelihood impacts — exceeds $3.2 trillion annually, against approximately $1.1 trillion in direct consumer spending. The externalized portion thus represents roughly two additional dollars of cost for every dollar spent at the register.

[12]: This is a simplified illustration. The precise allocation of externalized costs to specific products is methodologically complex and contested. What is not contested is that significant costs of industrial food production are borne by parties other than the buyer. For a rigorous treatment of externality accounting in food systems, see: Pretty, J. N., et al. (2005). Farm costs and food miles: An assessment of the full cost of the UK weekly food basket. Food Policy, 30(1), 1–19.

[13]: Havemeier, S., et al. (2017). Dietary guidance for pulses: the challenge and opportunity to be part of both the vegetable and protein food groups. Annals of the New York Academy of Sciences, 1392(1), 58–66. Pulses — dried beans, lentils, chickpeas — require minimal water relative to animal protein, fix atmospheric nitrogen, store without refrigeration, and produce protein at a fraction of the land and energy cost of animal sources.

[14]: Bouzari, A., Holstege, D., & Barrett, D. M. (2015). Mineral, fiber, and total phenolic retention in eight fruits and vegetables: a comparison of refrigerated and frozen storage. Journal of Agricultural and Food Chemistry, 63(3), 951–956. The study found no consistent nutritional advantage of fresh over frozen for the eight produce items examined, and frozen storage eliminated spoilage-related waste entirely.

[15]: Heller, M. C., & Keoleian, G. A. (2015). Greenhouse gas emission estimates of U.S. dietary choices and food loss. Journal of Industrial Ecology, 19(3), 391–401. The study documents that reducing food waste and shifting toward less-processed, more whole-animal consumption patterns can reduce the environmental footprint of animal-protein consumption without requiring wholesale dietary change.

[16]: Nugent, R. (2011). Bringing agriculture to the table: How agriculture and food can play a role in preventing chronic disease. Chicago Council on Global Affairs. For the specific economics of wild-harvest protein, see also: Tidball, K. G., & Stedman, R. C. (2013). Positive dependency and virtuous cycles: from resource dependence to resilience in urban social-ecological systems. Ecological Economics, 86, 292–299.

[17]: FDA. "Use of the Term Natural on Food Labeling." The FDA has not established a formal definition for the term "natural" beyond its longstanding policy that the term means "nothing artificial or synthetic (including all color additives regardless of source) has been included in, or has been added to, a food that would not normally be expected to be in that food." This does not address production methods, environmental practices, or system dependencies.

[18]: Jacquet, J., et al. (2010). Seafood stewardship in crisis. Nature, 467(7311), 28–29. The study documents significant variation in the rigor and enforcement of seafood sustainability certifications, noting that some programs rely on industry self-assessment while others require independent stock assessment and ecosystem impact evaluation.

[19]: Hilborn, R., et al. (2020). Effective fisheries management instrumental in improving fish stock status. PNAS, 117(4), 2218–2224. Alaska's salmon fishery management, based on in-season stock assessment and escapement-goal enforcement, represents one of the most rigorous fisheries management systems in the world and has maintained sustainable yields for decades.

[20]: Darmon, N., & Drewnowski, A. (2015). Contribution of food prices and diet cost to socioeconomic disparities in diet quality and health: a systematic review and analysis. Nutrition Reviews, 73(10), 643–660. The analysis documents that time cost and monetary cost interact to constrain diet quality, and that the most nutrient-dense and ecologically sound food choices often require both more money and more preparation time than processed alternatives.

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

What Must Change

◆ ◆ ◆

This book has been, for the most part, an argument directed at readers, at people who buy food, think about food, and have some capacity to choose what food they buy. The framework it has proposed, the four-axis assessment of food systems, is a tool for individual evaluation: a way for a person standing in a grocery store or a farmers' market or a restaurant to think more clearly about what the food in front of them depends on, what it costs in ways the price does not capture, and whether the system that produced it could survive conditions different from the ones it was designed for. The practical guidance the previous chapter offered was guidance for individuals making decisions within the food system as it currently exists. That guidance is real, and I stand behind it.

It is also not sufficient.

The limits of consumer choice as an engine of systemic change are well documented in the food-systems literature and are worth stating directly, because the temptation to end a book like this with an appeal to individual action is strong and the appeal is, on its own, inadequate to the scale of the problem the book has described. A 2012 analysis by Daniel Welch in the Journal of Consumer Culture examined the structural assumptions behind the "vote with your fork" framing that has dominated the sustainable-food conversation since Michael Pollan's The Omnivore's Dilemma helped popularize it, and concluded that consumer choice operates within a system whose structure is determined by forces the consumer does not control: commodity policy, land-use regulation, subsidy allocation, infrastructure investment, and the path dependencies of an industrial food system built over seventy years of policy and capital accumulation.1 The consumer can choose among the options the system offers. The consumer cannot, by choosing, change what options the system offers.

This is not a criticism of the readers who have followed this book's argument. It is a structural observation about the relationship between individual action and systemic architecture. A reader who adopts the four-axis framework, shifts purchasing toward rewilded and sustainably managed food sources, supports local producers operating outside the industrial commodity system, and advocates for the experiments the book has called for is doing everything the individual can do. If a hundred thousand readers did the same, the effect on the food system would be measurable but bounded. If a million readers did the same, the effect would be larger but still bounded by the same structural constraints. The industrial commodity system, the subsidy architecture, the land-use regulatory framework, the conservation baselines, and the institutional infrastructure that governs what kind of food production is permitted, incentivized, and scaled in the United States are not determined by consumer purchasing decisions. They are determined by policy, by regulation, by the allocation of public resources, and by the institutional inertia of a system that has been building in its current direction since the middle of the twentieth century.

The book's argument, if it is correct, has implications that extend past the individual reader. If the four-axis framework reveals structural vulnerabilities in the dominant food system that three-axis assessment cannot see, then the response to those vulnerabilities cannot be purely individual. If the fossil record provides a guide to which animals belong in which landscapes, and if the proxy-species experiments the book has called for are worth conducting, then the institutional and regulatory conditions under which those experiments could occur need to exist. If the rewilding vision the book has described, in which food is produced from landscapes managed under ecologically appropriate species assemblages, represents a direction the food system should move toward, then the policy environment needs to make that direction possible rather than impossible.

This chapter walks through what would have to change at the structural level for the book's vision to move from argument to practice. I want to be direct about the difficulty. None of what follows is easy. None of it is politically assured. I do not have a theory of how these changes get enacted, because I do not think such a theory exists in honest form. What I have is a description of the structural conditions that would need to be different, offered in the hope that naming them clearly is a precondition for working toward them, even when the path from naming to enactment is not visible.

Subsidy Reform

The federal subsidy structure is the floor on which the current food system stands. The commodity support system, administered through successive iterations of the farm bill since the Nixon-era restructuring of agricultural policy in the early 1970s, has shaped what the United States grows, how it grows it, where the economic incentives in agriculture point, and what kinds of food production are financially viable at scale. The structural effects are not subtle. Between 1995 and 2021, the federal government disbursed approximately $478 billion in agricultural subsidies, with the majority flowing to commodity crop producers — corn, soybeans, wheat, cotton, and rice — and to crop insurance premium subsidies that further underwrite commodity production risk.2 The distribution is concentrated: Environmental Working Group analyses of USDA subsidy data have consistently shown that the top ten percent of subsidy recipients receive the majority of payments, that the payments flow disproportionately to the largest operations, and that the crops subsidized are overwhelmingly the commodity inputs to the industrial food system the previous chapters have scored.3

The consequence of this structure for the food system the book has described is direct. When commodity corn is subsidized below the cost of production, the feedlot system that depends on cheap corn is effectively subsidized as well. The Tufts analysis documented roughly $35 billion in implicit savings to the industrial livestock sector from below-cost feed between 1997 and 2005, and the structural dynamic has not fundamentally changed since.4 When crop insurance subsidies reduce the financial risk of planting commodity crops on marginal land, the incentive to convert that land to other uses (perennial pasture, agroforestry, rewilding) is suppressed. When conservation payments through the Conservation Reserve Program and its successors pay landowners to take land out of production but do not create pathways for that land to be managed under the kinds of ecological restoration the book has described, the policy creates a binary — produce commodities or produce nothing — that forecloses the middle option of producing food from ecologically managed landscapes.

Definition
Subsidy Incidence

Subsidy incidence is the economic principle that the party receiving a subsidy check is not necessarily the party capturing its economic benefit.

A federal payment supporting corn production below cost is technically paid to the corn farmer, but the real beneficiary is whoever purchases the subsidized corn downstream. Between 1997 and 2005, the industrial livestock sector captured an estimated $35 billion in implicit savings through below-cost feed enabled by commodity subsidies, the Tufts figure this chapter cites. The incidence of the subsidy determined what kind of livestock production became economically viable at scale.

Restructuring agricultural support toward ecosystem-service payments rather than commodity price supports would shift that incidence, and with it the kinds of land use the subsidy system incentivizes. Where the money ends up determines what gets built on the land.

Restructuring the subsidy system toward ecological outcomes would change the economics of food production in the United States in ways that the current system's beneficiaries have understandable reasons to resist. The restructuring would mean, concretely, that federal agricultural spending would be redirected, partially or substantially, from commodity price supports and crop insurance premium subsidies toward payments for ecosystem services: soil carbon accumulation, biodiversity outcomes, water quality improvements, and the maintenance of ecological function on working landscapes. The concept is not novel. The Conservation Stewardship Program already makes payments for conservation practices on working land, and the 2018 farm bill expanded some of these provisions. What does not yet exist is a subsidy architecture in which the ecological outcomes the book's framework measures, including survivability and the restoration of species-landscape relationships, are weighted as heavily in the allocation of federal agricultural spending as commodity production volumes currently are.

Julie Witcover and colleagues, in a 2019 analysis for the UC Davis Policy Institute for Energy, Environment, and the Economy, examined the potential effects of redirecting a fraction of federal commodity subsidies toward ecosystem-service payments and found that even modest redirections could produce measurable shifts in land-use patterns, particularly on marginal cropland where the return to commodity production is lowest and the ecological return to alternative management is highest.5 The finding is intuitive: if the subsidy system pays you to grow corn on land that is marginal for corn, you grow corn; if the subsidy system pays you to manage that land for ecological outcomes, you manage for ecological outcomes. The incentive structure determines the land use. Changing the incentive structure changes the land use. The policy question is not whether this mechanism works. It is whether the political conditions exist to redirect the incentive.

I am not confident they do. Farm bill politics in the United States are among the most entrenched in the federal legislative process. The commodity support programs have institutional constituencies in the agricultural committees of Congress, in the commodity organizations, in the agribusiness firms that supply inputs to commodity producers, and in the rural communities whose economies have been organized around commodity production for generations. Those constituencies have successfully defended the basic architecture of the subsidy system through more than a dozen farm bill reauthorizations since 1973. Structural reform of the subsidy system is not impossible. It has happened before: the 1996 farm bill's elimination of deficiency payments and the subsequent reinstatement of support mechanisms under different names in the 2002 and subsequent bills illustrate both that the system can be restructured and that restructured systems tend to reconverge on the patterns they replaced.6 I do not think the political difficulty is a reason to stop talking about the structural need. I do think it is a reason to be honest about the distance between the need and the enactment.

Conservation Baselines

Chapter 2 of this book argued that the Columbian Curtain, the conservation paradigm that treats 1492 as the ecological baseline, shapes what counts as native, what counts as damage, and what counts as restoration. The argument was analytical. The policy implications are concrete.

Conservation policy in the United States operates, in the main, under a post-Columbian baseline. The Endangered Species Act protects species that are threatened or endangered relative to their post-Columbian range and abundance. The National Park Service manages park ecosystems toward reference conditions derived from the early historical record. The Fish and Wildlife Service's management of wildlife refuges is oriented toward the maintenance of habitat for species present in the post-Columbian fauna. The regulatory architecture, in short, is built on the baseline the book has argued should be reexamined.

Shifting the conservation baseline to include near-time frames would not require abandoning the work that has been done under the post-Columbian paradigm. The bald eagle recovery, the alligator recovery, the wolf reintroduction in the Northern Rockies: all of these are genuine conservation achievements accomplished under existing baselines, and none of them would be undermined by an expanded baseline. What the shift would do is create regulatory space for a category of restoration that the current baseline forecloses: the reintroduction of species, or proxies for species, that were present in the near-time fauna but absent from the post-Columbian baseline. Under the current regulatory architecture, a proposal to introduce camels to the American Southwest as proxies for Camelops is not merely unusual. It is categorically outside the frame of what conservation policy is designed to evaluate. The camel is not listed under the Endangered Species Act, because it is not a native species under the Act's baseline. It is not a candidate for reintroduction under any existing wildlife management program, because no existing program operates under a baseline that recognizes its lineage's presence on the continent. The regulatory system does not say "no" to the proposal because the proposal has been evaluated and found wanting. The regulatory system does not have a category in which the proposal can be evaluated at all.

Creating that category, a regulatory pathway for the evaluation of near-time proxy reintroductions, would be a structural change of considerable significance. It would not require the approval of any specific reintroduction. It would require only that the regulatory system acknowledge the existence of the question and create a process for answering it: a framework for evaluating proposals, assessing ecological risks, setting monitoring requirements, and establishing the conditions under which a proof-of-concept experiment could proceed. The framework would need to be rigorous. The ecological risk assessments would need to meet or exceed the standards applied to endangered-species reintroductions. The monitoring requirements would need to be sustained and independently evaluated. The bar should be high, because the stakes of getting it wrong (introducing an animal that causes unforeseen ecological damage) are real, as Caro and Rubenstein have argued and as this book has acknowledged.

The institutional model closest to what would be needed is the experimental-population designation under Section 10(j) of the Endangered Species Act, which allows the Fish and Wildlife Service to designate reintroduced populations of listed species as "experimental" and manage them under more flexible regulatory provisions than fully protected populations receive.7 The gray wolf reintroductions in Yellowstone and central Idaho were conducted under 10(j) designations, which allowed the managing agencies to address the legitimate concerns of livestock operators and adjacent landowners through management flexibility that full ESA protection would not have permitted. The mechanism is not directly applicable to proxy-species introductions, since a camel is not a listed species, but the conceptual architecture is relevant: a regulatory pathway that acknowledges the experimental nature of the introduction, provides for rigorous monitoring, establishes conditions for expansion or termination based on empirical results, and creates a legal framework within which the experiment can proceed without being foreclosed by categorical exclusion.

Jens-Christian Svenning and colleagues, in their 2016 synthesis on trophic rewilding in PNAS, argued that the scientific case for expanding conservation baselines to include Pleistocene-era ecological assemblages has strengthened substantially over the last decade, and that the institutional frameworks governing conservation practice have not kept pace with the science.8 The gap between the scientific literature and the regulatory architecture is not unique to this issue. Regulatory frameworks routinely lag behind the science they are meant to implement. But the gap is particularly consequential here, because it forecloses an entire category of restoration work that the science has identified as worth pursuing.

Further Structural Changes

Three additional areas of structural change bear noting, each briefer than the subsidy and baseline questions but each a real barrier to the vision the book has described.

The first is land-use regulation. Current agricultural zoning and conservation-easement structures assume a binary: commodity production or conservation set-aside. Ecologically managed food production — the Knepp model, the bison operations, the proxy-browsing experiments — is a third thing, and the regulatory apparatus does not have a well-developed framework for it. The Conservation Reserve Program pays landowners to take land out of production and maintain vegetative cover, but CRP contracts generally restrict the commercial use of enrolled acreage.9 The result is a policy binary that forecloses the option of producing food from an ecologically managed landscape. What would need to change is the creation of a third land-use classification with its own ecological-outcome standards, its own monitoring requirements, and its own eligibility for public support. The classification would need to be rigorous enough to prevent greenwashing and flexible enough to accommodate the diversity of landscapes the book has described. State-level habitat exchange programs for greater sage-grouse offer institutional models that could be adapted.10

The second is regulatory space for proxy-species experiments. The proof-of-concept work the book has called for faces a permitting landscape that was not built with it in mind. A dromedary camel on Arizona rangeland under a university research protocol might need permits from the state department of agriculture, the state game and fish department, and the county — each operating under a different statutory framework, none designed for ecologically motivated species introductions. The friction is not deliberate. It is a side effect of regulatory architecture built for different purposes. What is needed is a streamlined pathway that integrates federal, state, and local authority into a single review process with clear criteria and defined timelines, modeled on the ESA's Section 10(j) experimental-population designation, which enabled the Yellowstone wolf reintroductions and the California condor recovery.7 The institutional capacity to manage large-bodied animal introductions on working landscapes exists. It needs to be adapted to a new category of question.

The third is fisheries management. Here the institutional infrastructure is, in some respects, further along than the terrestrial equivalent. The Magnuson-Stevens Act provides the statutory framework, the regional fishery management councils provide governance, and Hilborn's stock-assessment methodology provides the scientific tools. The Alaska salmon fisheries are a working example of sustainable wild-harvest food production at commercial scale.11 What would need to change is the extension of this model to marine restoration — kelp forests, oyster reefs, coastal wetlands — under regulatory frameworks that treat restored marine ecosystems as productive systems rather than merely as conservation amenities, and that treat the food-production value of restoration as part of the justification for public investment.12

What Would Have to Be True

I have described five areas of structural change: subsidy reform, conservation-baseline expansion, land-use regulatory modernization, regulatory pathways for proxy-species experiments, and the extension and strengthening of science-based fisheries management. Each is individually difficult. Together they constitute a reorientation of the policy framework governing food production and land management in the United States, a reorientation that is, by any honest assessment, politically formidable.

I want to name what would have to be true for these changes to become possible, because naming the preconditions honestly is more useful than pretending the changes are around the corner.

There would have to be a political constituency for ecological food-system reform that is large enough and organized enough to influence farm bill deliberations. Farm bill politics are currently dominated by the commodity agriculture constituency, the nutrition-assistance constituency (SNAP and related programs account for the majority of farm bill spending), and the conservation constituency, which has achieved meaningful but incremental gains over successive reauthorizations. A constituency for the kind of structural reform this chapter describes — one that includes producers operating outside the commodity system, conservation organizations working on near-time baselines, Indigenous nations managing land under traditional and restored practices, and consumers who have adopted some version of the framework this book proposes — does not currently exist as a coordinated political force. It exists in fragments. The fragments have not yet found each other.

There would have to be a sustained research program generating the empirical evidence that policy change requires. The proof-of-concept experiments the book has called for are not just interesting ecological questions. They are the evidentiary foundation without which no policy advocate can credibly argue for regulatory change. A policymaker being asked to create a permitting pathway for proxy-species experiments will ask what the evidence shows. If the evidence does not yet exist because the experiments have not been conducted, the argument stalls. The research comes first. The policy follows the research.

There would have to be an honest reckoning with the distributional consequences of subsidy reform. Redirecting federal agricultural spending from commodity support to ecosystem-service payments would produce winners and losers, and the losers would include producers and communities whose livelihoods are organized around the current commodity system. A reform that does not account for the transition costs — that does not provide pathways for commodity producers to shift toward ecological management, that does not invest in the rural infrastructure and retraining that the transition would require — is a reform that imposes its costs on the people least able to absorb them. The people most affected by subsidy reform are not the agribusiness corporations that capture the largest share of the payments. They are the family-scale commodity producers and the rural communities whose economies have been organized around commodity production for generations. A just transition would need to address their interests, and addressing their interests would need to be part of the reform's design rather than an afterthought.

There would have to be, finally, a cultural shift in how Americans think about the land they live on: a shift from treating the post-Columbian landscape as the default ecological condition to recognizing it as one state in a longer ecological history. This is the shift the Columbian Curtain argument was designed to support. It is not a shift that a book or a policy proposal can produce on its own. It is a shift that happens, if it happens, through the accumulation of evidence, education, and experience: through people visiting rewilded landscapes and seeing what they look like, through students learning near-time ecology alongside contemporary ecology, through the slow accretion of a different ecological imagination. I do not know how long this takes. I know it has not happened yet.

Closing

This book has made its argument. It has proposed a framework, applied it, followed it into territory the food-systems conversation has mostly not visited, and arrived at a set of conclusions I believe the evidence supports. The framework identifies structural vulnerabilities in the dominant food system. The fossil record identifies a direction for restoration that most of the sustainability literature has not considered. The existing proof-of-concept work — the bison operations, the feral hog harvests, the intact fisheries, the Knepp restoration — demonstrates that rewilded food production is not an abstraction. The serious critics have been engaged, and the responses to their critiques are, I think, defensible.

What the book cannot do is enact what it proposes. The distance between an argument and its realization is not a distance the argument itself can cross. The structural changes this chapter has described are preconditions for the book's vision to scale beyond the readers who adopt the framework individually. Those changes are politically difficult, institutionally complex, and dependent on conditions — a political constituency, an evidentiary base, a cultural shift — that do not yet fully exist.

I am not ending with certainty about outcomes, because certainty about political outcomes is not something the evidence supports, and the book's commitment to stating what it can defend requires applying that standard here as well. I am ending with a description of the door. The door is the set of structural conditions under which the experiments could be tried, the baselines could be expanded, the subsidy system could be restructured, and the food system could begin to move in the direction the framework points. The door exists. It is not open. Whether it opens depends on work that this book can contribute to but cannot complete.

The epilogue returns to the orchard. What remains after the argument, after the framework, after the policy, is the question the prologue asked: is the experiment worth trying? The book has laid out what it would take. The answer to whether it is worth trying is not the book's to give. It is the reader's.

EPILOGUE

One Hundred Camels

◆ ◆ ◆

The orchard does not exist yet. I want to return to it anyway.

I opened this book with a camel: Camelops hesternus, yesterday's camel, native to North America for most of the history of its genus, gone for thirteen thousand years, remembered mostly in bones and the occasional museum mount. I opened it with an osage orange tree, producing its softball-sized fruit for a mouth that is no longer there. I opened it with a wish: to see one hundred camels in an osage orange orchard, just to see if it could work. The wish was not a program. It was the thing the program would be for.

The book between the prologue and this page has been the program, or at least the argument for one. Thirteen chapters of framework, evidence, application, and honest reckoning with what the framework cannot do. The reader who has come this far knows what the four-axis assessment measures and what it reveals about the dominant food system. The reader knows what the Columbian Curtain hides and what the near-time window makes visible. The reader has walked through the Polyface math, the Savory food-truck conversation, the fossil record of the Grand Canyon, the critics' strongest arguments, the marine restorations, the Indigenous management traditions that operate under a parallel frame. The reader has sat with Chapter 13's description of the structural changes that would need to happen for any of this to move from argument to practice, and the reader has noticed — I hope — that the chapter was honest about the distance between the argument and the enactment.

I do not want to summarize any of that. The reader has just read it. What I want to do, here at the end, is return to the image that started it, because the image has changed, not in its content but in what it carries.

When I described the orchard in the prologue, it carried one thing: a longing. A specific want, plainly stated, for a specific arrangement of animals and trees on a specific kind of ground. The longing was personal. It was mine. I did not try to dress it as policy or as science, because it was not those things. It was the reason I had spent years thinking about the questions the book asks, and it seemed honest to say so at the beginning, before the analytical apparatus had been set up and before the reader had any reason to take the analysis seriously.

The longing has not gone away. I still want to see those camels. I still want to know what the fruit looks like when it has a mouth. But the image is no longer sitting in the space it sat in at the prologue's close — a private wish, carried by a person the reader had just met, before the argument had been made. It is sitting, now, on the other side of the argument. The orchard has accumulated meaning it did not have sixty thousand words ago.

The camels in the orchard are not just camels anymore. They are the proxy-selection principle from Chapter 7 — the fossil record consulted, the co-evolutionary relationship identified, the right animal placed in the right landscape for reasons the paleontology supports. They are the survivability argument from Chapter 4 — animals that feed themselves on what the landscape produces, with a dependency profile thinner than anything the industrial system can offer. They are the proof-of-concept experiment from Chapter 9 — bounded, monitored, designed to generate data rather than to prove a point. They are the structural change from Chapter 13 — the permitting pathway that does not yet exist, the regulatory category that has not been created, the political constituency that has not yet found itself. The hundred camels carry all of that now. They carried none of it before.

The osage orange carries something different than it did at the beginning, too. In the prologue, it was an anachronism: a tree stranded in a present that had forgotten what it was for, producing fruit for a disperser that had been gone for a hundred and twenty centuries. That much has not changed. What has changed is the frame around it. The tree is no longer an isolated curiosity, a bit of natural-history trivia about the deep past. It is an instance of a pattern the book has traced across the entire continent: the grasslands still calibrated to grazers that are gone, the thorny shrubs still armed against browsers that are absent, the pronghorn still running from nothing, the kelp forests and oyster reefs stripped to fractions of their historical extent, the Indigenous fire regimes suppressed, the soil communities waiting for trampling patterns that have not been produced in twelve thousand years. The osage orange, in the prologue, was one tree with one absent partner. In the epilogue, it is a representative of a continent whose ecological vocabulary has been spoken at half-volume for longer than there has been agriculture, and whose restoration (if it is attempted, if it is permitted, if the experiments are funded and the baselines are expanded and the political will materializes) would be the most ambitious act of ecological repair in the history of the species attempting it.

That is a large thing to rest on one tree and a hundred hypothetical camels. I am aware of the disproportion. The book has been aware of it from the beginning. The analytical argument is not proportional to the longing. The longing is outsized, personal, and not fully defensible on the evidence the book has assembled. The evidence supports a framework. The framework supports a set of experiments. The experiments might support a direction. The direction might, over decades, produce a food system organized around ecological function rather than industrial inputs. None of that is the same as a hundred camels in an orchard. The orchard is where the argument started, and it is further from realization than any of the intermediate steps the book has described.

I think this disproportion is honest rather than embarrassing. The book began with a wish because the wish is what generated the work. The work is what the book contains. The work does not fulfill the wish, and it would be dishonest to pretend otherwise. What the work does is take the wish seriously enough to ask what it would actually require (what framework, what evidence, what engagement with critics, what policy changes, what institutional capacity, what cultural shift) and to lay that out as clearly as I can, knowing that I do not control most of the conditions the answer depends on.

The book has not solved the problem it describes. It has not demonstrated that Pleistocene rewilding works at scale, because the experiments have not been conducted. It has not demonstrated that the four-axis framework produces better policy outcomes than the three-axis model, because the framework has not been adopted by any policymaking body. It has not demonstrated that Americans will eat camel, or that the permitting pathway will be created, or that the farm bill will be restructured, or that the cultural imagination will expand to include a continent whose memory is older than its paperwork. The book has made an argument. The argument is partial, contested, and dependent on conditions I do not control. I have tried to make it carefully and to be honest about where the evidence is strong, where it is provisional, and where I am asking for something the evidence does not yet support.

What I am asking for, in the end, is not certainty. It is not even, really, that the experiment be mine. I read Stephen Hawking when I was young, the way you do, with a flashlight, understanding half of it, and ever since I have wanted to see a black hole and throw popcorn at its event horizon. The popcorn was mine. The want was mine. The physics were going to go on doing what they do without my participation, and the participation was never the point. The point was to watch the popcorn go in and know, finally, what happens at the edge. I am going to die without throwing the popcorn, and the wanting has not gone away.

Field Note
The Popcorn

The popcorn thing is real. I was twelve, maybe thirteen, and I had read Stephen Hawking the way you read Stephen Hawking when you are that age, grasping the shape of the thing without grasping the math.

I told someone (I no longer remember who) that I wanted to throw popcorn at a black hole. Not a metaphor. Actual popcorn, packed into a tube, aimed at an event horizon, to see what happens on the way in. I was laughed at, kindly. I kept the want. I still have it, thirty-five years later, and I am aware it is not going to happen in any life I can plausibly arrange.

That is fine. The point of the popcorn, I understood only much later, was not the popcorn. It was that some wants are worth carrying even when they exceed what the carrier can do about them. The orchard is one of those. I am not embarrassed to end a book of analytical argument with a wish the analytical argument cannot fulfill. Some wishes are the reason for the analysis.

What I am describing at the end of this book is the same kind of wanting. I want to know whether a parcel of desertified ground in the basin and range country of California or Nevada — real ground, not hypothetical, ground that is sick and losing what little it has left to lose — can be flipped. Not flipped the way a house gets flipped, exactly, though that is the register I mean. Bought distressed. Returned to function by putting the right animals back on it and the right trees in it, running as a closed-loop system for a decade with honest monitoring. Sold, or kept, or left to the commons, whichever way the experiment wants to go once the experiment has been run. What I want to know is whether the ground can be healed. I see desertification as a disease. I do not see it as a settled condition. I do not see it as something the ground has chosen, or earned, or is stuck with. I see it as something nobody has yet been permitted to treat.

I have said as much at Allan Savory conferences and been eye-rolled by people in that audience whom I love and respect and whose work I admire. I do not care. I am not trying to win the argument at the conference. I am trying to find out whether the answer exists. Those are not the same project, and the second one is the one I am interested in. The experiment does not need to be mine. It needs to be somebody's. The ground is there. The camels are there: feral in parts of the American West, stabled on hobby ranches, eating mesquite because that is what camels eat when you put them on ground that grows it. The osage orange is there, falling and rotting and asking for a mouth nobody has yet put in front of it. The regulatory pathway does not exist, but that is what Chapter 13 was for. The patron, the university, the protocol, the acreage: none of these exist yet in the configuration the experiment requires, and I do not control whether they come together. What I can do is name what I am asking for, and hope the person who can do it reads this far.

I do not know what the data would show. I think the hypothesis is worth testing, or I would not have written the book. I think the ecological logic is sound: that a continent missing its keystone herbivores for twelve thousand years is functioning below its capacity, that the fossil record provides a guide to restoration that the current conservation baseline does not see, and that food systems organized around ecological function are more survivable than food systems organized around industrial inputs. I think these things because the evidence the book has assembled supports them. I do not think them with the certainty I once brought to simpler versions of this argument, because the revisions the book has documented have taught me something about the relationship between confidence and evidence that the earlier version of me had not yet learned.

The orchard is still hypothetical. The camels are not standing in it. The osage oranges are still falling, still rotting in place, still producing for no one. Somewhere in the arid basin country of the Southwest, a dromedary on a hobby ranch is eating mesquite, because mesquite is what camels eat when you put them on ground that grows it, and neither the camel nor the mesquite requires an argument to know what they are for.

I would still like to see those camels. I would still like to know what happens when the fruit has a mouth. I would like, more than either of those things, to know whether the ground can be flipped — whether a parcel that has been desertifying for a century can, under the right animals and the right trees and enough time, come back. I will probably not be the person who finds out. I want to be alive when somebody does.

Notes

Martin synthesizes the Late Pleistocene loss of more than forty North American large-mammal species, including Camelops hesternus and Hemiauchenia, dated to approximately 13,000 years before present in the North American fossil record.

CHAPTER ONE  ·  The Meat Counter
