The standard defined grass-fed as meaning the animal's diet was derived solely from forage after weaning; grain and grain byproducts were excluded from the permitted diet.
  2. The voluntary nature of the USDA grass-fed marketing claim standard meant that producers who did not apply for certification could still use the term "grass-fed" on their packaging, subject only to the general USDA-FSIS labeling approval process for meat products. See USDA Food Safety and Inspection Service, "Labeling Policies" guidance documents, particularly the label approval process for special claims under 9 CFR 317.4.
  3. United States Department of Agriculture, Agricultural Marketing Service. (2016). "Withdrawal of United States Standards for Livestock and Meat Marketing Claims, Grass (Forage) Fed Claim for Ruminant Livestock and the Meat Products Derived from Such Livestock." Federal Register, 81(12), 3073–3074. Effective January 12, 2016.
  4. United States Department of Agriculture, Food Safety and Inspection Service. "Meat and Poultry Labeling Terms." The FSIS defines "free-range" or "free-roaming" for poultry as meaning "producers must demonstrate to the Agency that the poultry has been allowed access to the outside." No specifications for duration, frequency, or size of outdoor area are included in the operative definition. See FSIS Directive 7120.1, "Safe and Suitable Ingredients Used in the Production of Meat, Poultry, and Egg Products," Appendix A, and the FSIS labeling guidance for animal-raising claims.
  5. Organic Foods Production Act of 1990, 7 U.S.C. §§ 6501–6523; implementing regulations at 7 CFR Part 205. The National Organic Program standards specify allowed and prohibited substances, require annual inspection by accredited certifying agents, and define organic production as "a production system that is managed in accordance with the Act and regulations in this part to respond to site-specific conditions by integrating cultural, biological, and mechanical practices that foster cycling of resources, promote ecological balance, and conserve biodiversity."
  6. The term "natural" as applied to meat and poultry is defined by USDA-FSIS as meaning "a product containing no artificial ingredient or added color and is only minimally processed." The definition addresses processing, not production practices; it says nothing about how the animal was raised, what it was fed, or what inputs were used in its production. See 9 CFR 317.16 and FSIS Policy Memo 055B. For non-meat food products regulated by the FDA, "natural" has no formal regulatory definition and has been the subject of repeated citizen petitions and comment periods without resolution. See FDA, "Use of the Term 'Natural' on Food Labeling," Docket FDA-2014-N-1207.

CHAPTER TWO  ·  The Columbian Curtain

  1. MacFadden, B. J. (1992). Fossil Horses: Systematics, Paleobiology, and Evolution of the Family Equidae. Cambridge University Press, pp. 1–14 and chapter 5. MacFadden's synthesis of Equidae evolution is the standard reference for the North American origin of the genus.
  2. Scott, E., & Cox, S. M. (2008). "Late Pleistocene distribution of Equus (Mammalia: Equidae) in western North America." In Geology and Vertebrate Paleontology of Western and Southern North America, Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County Science Series 41, pp. 189–208. For the specific North American late-Pleistocene equid taxa and their chronology.
  3. Orlando, L., et al. (2013). "Recalibrating Equus evolution using the genome sequence of an early Middle Pleistocene horse." Nature, 499(7456), 74–78. The Thistle Creek genome.
  4. Weinstock, J., et al. (2005). "Evolution, systematics, and phylogeography of Pleistocene horses in the New World: a molecular perspective." PLoS Biology, 3(8), e241.
  5. Martin, P. S. (2005). Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America. University of California Press, chapter 3 and chapter 9. Martin's synthesis of the North American near-time losses and the date range of the terminal Pleistocene pulse.
  6. Bureau of Land Management. "Wild Horse and Burro Program: Program Data." Current public population estimates are published annually by the BLM's National Wild Horse and Burro Program office; the AML (appropriate management level) framework and the discrepancy between AML and current population numbers are detailed in the Program Data documentation. See also the 2020 National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine review of BLM wild horse and burro management, which describes the same discrepancy.
  7. MacPhee, R. D. E., ed. (1999). Extinctions in Near Time: Causes, Contexts, and Consequences. Kluwer Academic / Plenum. The introductory chapter and the volume's synthesis chapters are the primary references for the near-time frame as used here.
  8. Koch, P. L., & Barnosky, A. D. (2006). "Late Quaternary extinctions: state of the debate." Annual Review of Ecology, Evolution, and Systematics, 37, 215–250. Koch and Barnosky's review assembles the continent-by-continent loss figures used here.
  9. Smith, F. A., et al. (2016). "Body size downgrading of mammals over the late Quaternary." Science, 351(6280), 310–313. Smith and colleagues quantify the loss of large-mammal body mass across the late Quaternary, with regional breakdowns that include the North American figures referenced here.
  10. Graham, R. W., et al. (2016). "Timing and causes of mid-Holocene mammoth extinction on St. Paul Island, Alaska." PNAS, 113(33), 9310–9314. For the latest securely dated North American mammoth populations, on a Bering Sea refugium, dating to roughly 5,600 years before present. Mainland North American mammoths are generally gone by roughly 10,500–11,000 BP; the refugial populations extend the range somewhat.
  11. Martin, P. S., Sabels, B. E., & Shutler, D., Jr. (1961). "Rampart Cave coprolite and ecology of the Shasta ground sloth." American Journal of Science, 259, 102–127. For the classic description of preserved Shasta ground sloth dung and the reconstruction of late-Pleistocene browse based on it.
  12. See the Executive Order 13112 definition of "invasive species" as operationalized by the US National Invasive Species Council: a non-native species whose introduction does or is likely to cause economic or environmental harm, or harm to human health. The "non-native" qualifier in the operative federal and most state-level definitions is assessed against the pre-Columbian biota. See also Richardson, D. M., et al. (2000). "Naturalization and invasion of alien plants: concepts and definitions." Diversity and Distributions, 6(2), 93–107, for the parallel academic formulation in invasion biology.
  13. Pauly, D. (1995). "Anecdotes and the shifting baseline syndrome of fisheries." Trends in Ecology & Evolution, 10(10), 430. A short note that has since anchored a substantial literature; see also Jackson, J. B. C., et al. (2001). "Historical overfishing and the recent collapse of coastal ecosystems." Science, 293(5530), 629–638, for a major application of the shifting-baseline concept to marine historical ecology.
  14. Hansen, R. M. (1978). "Shasta ground sloth food habits, Rampart Cave, Arizona." Paleobiology, 4(3), 302–319. For the systematic reconstruction of Nothrotheriops shastensis diet from preserved coprolites, which remains one of the most detailed diet reconstructions for any extinct megaherbivore.
  15. Stock, C., & Harris, J. M. (1992). Rancho La Brea: A Record of Pleistocene Life in California (7th ed.). Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County Science Series 37. For the systematic description of the La Brea faunal record, including Panthera atrox, Smilodon fatalis, and the dire wolf Aenocyon dirus, with chronological framework.

CHAPTER THREE  ·  Near Time

  1. Libby, W. F. (1952). Radiocarbon Dating. University of Chicago Press. Libby's foundational monograph on the method for which he received the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 1960.
  2. Martin, P. S. (2005). Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America. University of California Press, chapter 1 and chapter 9, and table 1 (pp. 10–11). Martin's canonical summary of the North American generic losses concentrated in the terminal Pleistocene pulse around 13,000 calendar years before present.
  3. Martin (2005), chapter 1, table 1 and the accompanying discussion of species-level losses. The forty-five-kilogram threshold is the standard used in the near-time literature for "large" mammals and is consistent with MacPhee, R. D. E., ed. (1999), Extinctions in Near Time: Causes, Contexts, and Consequences, Kluwer Academic / Plenum.
  4. Scott, E., & Cox, S. M. (2008). "Late Pleistocene distribution of Equus (Mammalia: Equidae) in western North America." In Geology and Vertebrate Paleontology of Western and Southern North America, Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County Science Series 41, pp. 189–208. For the terminal Pleistocene chronology of the North American equids.
  5. Matheus, P. E. (1995). "Diet and co-ecology of Pleistocene short-faced bears and brown bears in eastern Beringia." Quaternary Research, 44(3), 447–453. For isotopic reconstruction of Arctodus diet and ecological role; see also Figueirido, B., Pérez-Claros, J. A., Torregrosa, V., Martín-Serra, A., & Palmqvist, P. (2010). "Demythologizing Arctodus simus, the 'short-faced' long-legged and predaceous bear that never was." Journal of Vertebrate Paleontology, 30(1), 262–275, for a contrary view of the animal's ecology; the loss of Arctodus is uncontested, whatever its exact trophic role.
  6. Martin (2005), chapter 1 and chapter 2, on the residual post-extinction fauna; and Koch, P. L., & Barnosky, A. D. (2006). "Late Quaternary extinctions: state of the debate." Annual Review of Ecology, Evolution, and Systematics, 37, 215–250, for the continent-by-continent loss figures.
  7. Barnosky, A. D., Koch, P. L., Feranec, R. S., Wing, S. L., & Shabel, A. B. (2004). "Assessing the causes of Late Pleistocene extinctions on the continents." Science, 306(5693), 70–75, for the intercontinental pattern of selective loss of large terrestrial mammals and its implications.
  8. Mosimann, J. E., & Martin, P. S. (1975). "Simulating overkill by Paleoindians." American Scientist, 63(3), 304–313, for the simulation work demonstrating that a small human population advancing through naive megafaunal prey could plausibly produce the observed extinction pattern in a few centuries. Also Martin, P. S., & Klein, R. G., eds. (1984). Quaternary Extinctions: A Prehistoric Revolution. University of Arizona Press, for the field's mid-1980s synthesis; Martin, P. S., & Wright, H. E., eds. (1967). Pleistocene Extinctions: The Search for a Cause. Yale University Press, for the earlier collected volume that established the debate.
  9. Martin (2005), chapter 9, "Models in Collision: Climatic Change versus Overkill," p. 168 (quotation regarding Australia/Americas/New Zealand non-concordance).
  10. Alley, R. B. (2000). "The Younger Dryas cold interval as viewed from central Greenland." Quaternary Science Reviews, 19(1–5), 213–226, for the Greenland ice-core record and the place of the Younger Dryas within the broader pattern of late-Quaternary climate variability; also Dansgaard, W., et al. (1993). "Evidence for general instability of past climate from a 250-kyr ice-core record." Nature, 364(6434), 218–220, for the earlier comparable oscillations.
  11. Martin (2005), chapter 9, p. 165, quoting E. O. Wilson's 1992 characterization as epigraph and offering the subsequent "I will lay aside impartiality" declaration.
  12. For a representative version of this refined climate-centric account, see Grayson, D. K., & Meltzer, D. J. (2003). "A requiem for North American overkill." Journal of Archaeological Science, 30(5), 585–593; and Grayson, D. K. (2007). "Deciphering North American Pleistocene extinctions." Journal of Anthropological Research, 63(2), 185–213. Grayson and Meltzer are the most persistent recent critics of the strong overkill formulation; their counter-model is best read as an example of what a refined climate-centric account looks like.
  13. Broecker, W. S. (2006). "Was the Younger Dryas triggered by a flood?" Science, 312(5777), 1146–1148, for the canonical statement of the Lake Agassiz meltwater mechanism; and Carlson, A. E., Clark, P. U., Haley, B. A., Klinkhammer, G. P., Simmons, K., Brook, E. J., & Meissner, K. J. (2007). "Geochemical proxies of North American freshwater routing during the Younger Dryas cold event." PNAS, 104(16), 6556–6561, for geochemical evidence constraining the routing. Tarasov, L., & Peltier, W. R. (2005). "Arctic freshwater forcing of the Younger Dryas cold reversal." Nature, 435(7042), 662–665, offers an alternative Arctic-routing reconstruction. The mechanism remains actively argued; the onset of the cooling is not.
  14. Vartanyan, S. L., Garutt, V. E., & Sher, A. V. (1993). "Holocene dwarf mammoths from Wrangel Island in the Siberian Arctic." Nature, 362(6418), 337–340, for the Wrangel Island chronology; and Graham, R. W., et al. (2016). "Timing and causes of mid-Holocene mammoth extinction on St. Paul Island, Alaska." PNAS, 113(33), 9310–9314, for the St. Paul Island chronology and the case for non-anthropogenic mechanisms in the refugial populations' terminal phase.
  15. Firestone, R. B., et al. (2007). "Evidence for an extraterrestrial impact 12,900 years ago that contributed to the megafaunal extinctions and the Younger Dryas cooling." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 104(41), 16016–16021.
  16. Pinter, N., Scott, A. C., Daulton, T. L., Podoll, A., Koeberl, C., Anderson, R. S., & Ishman, S. E. (2011). "The Younger Dryas impact hypothesis: a requiem." Earth-Science Reviews, 106(3–4), 247–264; and Holliday, V. T., Surovell, T., Meltzer, D. J., Grayson, D. K., & Boslough, M. (2014). "The Younger Dryas impact hypothesis: a cosmic catastrophe." Journal of Quaternary Science, 29(6), 515–530. Both papers marshal the principal lines of critique; the first is particularly forceful on the markers, the second on the chronological and archaeological record.
  17. Wittke, J. H., et al. (2013). "Evidence for deposition of 10 million tonnes of impact spherules across four continents 12,800 y ago." PNAS, 110(23), E2088–E2097.
  18. Kennett, J. P., et al. (2015). "Bayesian chronological analyses consistent with synchronous age of 12,835–12,735 Cal B.P. for Younger Dryas boundary on four continents." PNAS, 112(32), E4344–E4353.
  19. Sandom, C., Faurby, S., Sandel, B., & Svenning, J.-C. (2014). "Global late Quaternary megafauna extinctions linked to humans, not climate change." Proceedings of the Royal Society B, 281(1787), 20133254. The paper's Table 1 and its climate-versus-human-arrival regression analysis are the key results.
  20. Stuart, A. J. (2015). "Late Quaternary megafaunal extinctions on the continents: a short review." Geological Journal, 50(3), 338–363. Stuart's synthesis is particularly useful for readers who want the continent-by-continent interaction of climate and human pressure laid out compactly.

CHAPTER FOUR  ·  The Fourth Axis

  1. United States Department of Agriculture, Economic Research Service. (2023). Feed Outlook. USDA-ERS, multiple issues, 2012 and 2022. Corn futures exceeded $8.00/bushel during the 2012 drought, the highest nominal prices in the history of the US corn market at that time. The 2022 spike, driven by the combination of drought stress, post-pandemic supply-chain disruption, and the loss of Ukrainian grain exports, pushed feed costs to levels not seen since the 2012 event in inflation-adjusted terms. For pastured-poultry-sector impacts specifically, see trade-press accounts in Acres U.S.A., Growing for Market, and Stockman Grass Farmer covering the 2012 and 2022 seasons.
  2. The three-pillar model of sustainability — economic, social, environmental — evolved through several institutional formulations in the late twentieth century. For the institutional genealogy, see Purvis, B., Mao, Y., & Robinson, D. (2019). "Three pillars of sustainability: in search of conceptual origins." Sustainability Science, 14, 681–695, which traces the model's emergence through the Brundtland Report, the 1992 Rio Declaration, and the subsequent corporate and institutional sustainability literature.
  3. World Commission on Environment and Development. (1987). Our Common Future. Oxford University Press. The Brundtland definition of sustainable development appears in Chapter 2 of the report.
  4. For the ISO 14000 family of environmental management standards as applied to food-system assessment, see International Organization for Standardization. (2015). ISO 14001:2015 — Environmental management systems — Requirements with guidance for use. For life-cycle assessment methodology applied to food systems specifically, see Notarnicola, B., Sala, S., Anton, A., McLaren, S. J., Saouter, E., & Sonesson, U. (2017). "The role of life cycle assessment in supporting sustainable agri-food systems: A review of the challenges." Journal of Cleaner Production, 140, 399–409.
  5. Global Reporting Initiative. (2021). GRI Standards: Consolidated Set of GRI Sustainability Reporting Standards. Global Sustainability Standards Board. The GRI Standards are the most widely adopted sustainability reporting framework globally, organizing disclosure around economic, social, and environmental performance categories, and have been applied to food and agriculture cooperatives through the GRI 13 sector standard for Agriculture, Aquaculture and Fishing Sectors (2022).
  6. McBride, W. D., & Greene, C. (2009). Characteristics, Costs, and Issues for Organic Dairy Farming. Economic Research Report No. 82, United States Department of Agriculture, Economic Research Service. See also Su, Y., Cook, M. L., & Park, T. (2019). "The impact of the farmgate milk price on the survival of U.S. organic dairy farms." Renewable Agriculture and Food Systems, 34(6), 558–568, documenting the role of the organic price premium in organic dairy farm survival, and the sector's repeated contraction episodes when premium compression has reduced the gap between organic and conventional milk prices.
  7. Waltenburg, M. A., Victoroff, T., Rose, C. E., et al. (2020). "Update: COVID-19 among workers in meat and poultry processing facilities — United States, April–May 2020." Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report, 69(27), 887–892. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The report documents infection clusters at 115 meat and poultry processing facilities across 19 states in the first two months of the pandemic, resulting in facility closures and throughput reductions that produced simultaneous live-animal surpluses on farms and retail meat shortages.
  8. Henson, C. B., Luedke, A., Allured, D. J., & Hoerling, M. P. (2022). "The February 2021 cold event in Texas and the south-central US: Historical context and attribution." Journal of Climate, 35(18), 5927–5943. For the agricultural impacts specifically, see Texas A&M AgriLife Extension Service. (2021). Preliminary Texas Agricultural Losses from Winter Storm Uri. The estimated crop and livestock losses across the Texas agricultural sector exceeded $600 million, concentrated in Rio Grande Valley citrus, livestock mortality in operations without backup power, and cold-chain disruption for produce exports.
  9. Glauber, J., & Laborde, D. (2022). "How will Russia's invasion of Ukraine affect global food security?" International Food Policy Research Institute Blog, February 24, 2022. See also Ben Hassen, T., & El Bilali, H. (2022). "Impacts of the Russia-Ukraine war on global food security: towards more sustainable and resilient food systems?" Foods, 11(15), 2301, for a systematic assessment of the trade disruption's effects on global grain markets and food security in import-dependent regions.
  10. Boyer, J. S., Byrne, P., Cassman, K. G., Cooper, M., Delmer, D., et al. (2013). "The U.S. drought of 2012 in perspective: a call to action." Global Food Security, 2(3), 139–143. The 2012 drought affected approximately eighty percent of US agricultural land and reduced the national corn yield by roughly twenty-seven percent relative to trend, driving commodity corn prices above $8/bushel.
  11. Holling, C. S. (1973). "Resilience and stability of ecological systems." Annual Review of Ecology and Systematics, 4, 1–23. Holling's distinction between stability (the speed of return to equilibrium after disturbance) and resilience (the magnitude of disturbance a system can absorb before shifting to a qualitatively different state) remains foundational in ecological theory and has been widely imported into sustainability science.
  12. Tendall, D. M., Joerin, J., Kopainsky, B., Edwards, P., Shreck, A., Le, Q. B., Kruetli, P., Grant, M., & Six, J. (2015). "Food system resilience: Defining the concept." Global Food Security, 6, 17–23. The paper reviews the disparate uses of "resilience" in the food-systems literature and proposes a consolidated definition, noting that the concept remains underspecified in most applied assessment frameworks.
  13. Taleb, N. N. (2012). Antifragile: Things That Gain from Disorder. Random House. Taleb's three-part classification — fragile (harmed by volatility), robust (unaffected by volatility), antifragile (benefiting from volatility) — applies to food systems with particular force. The argument that optimization for efficiency under stable conditions produces fragility under unstable conditions is developed throughout the book and is directly relevant to the industrial food-system case.

CHAPTER FIVE  ·  Scoring the Industrial System

  1. USDA National Agricultural Statistics Service. (2019). 2017 Census of Agriculture. United States Department of Agriculture. The census documents that approximately 99 percent of US meat animals are finished in confined operations, reflecting the consolidation of animal finishing into the CAFO model over the last half century.
  2. USDA Economic Research Service. (2023). Meat Price Spreads. ERS tracks retail, wholesale, and farm-level prices for beef, pork, and chicken in nominal and inflation-adjusted terms. The long-term trend in inflation-adjusted poultry prices is a decline of roughly forty percent between 1960 and 2020, driven by the efficiency gains of industrial confined production.
  3. Pew Commission on Industrial Farm Animal Production. (2008). Putting Meat on the Table: Industrial Farm Animal Production in America. Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health / Pew Charitable Trusts. The commission's assessment documented the externalized costs of industrial animal production across environmental, public health, and rural economic dimensions, concluding that the retail price of industrial meat does not reflect its full cost of production.
  4. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. (2019). Antibiotic Resistance Threats in the United States, 2019. US Department of Health and Human Services. The report documented more than 2.8 million antibiotic-resistant infections and more than 35,000 deaths annually in the United States, with associated economic costs estimated in the tens of billions of dollars.
  5. US Food and Drug Administration. (2022). 2021 Summary Report on Antimicrobials Sold or Distributed for Use in Food-Producing Animals. FDA Center for Veterinary Medicine. The report documented that approximately 66 percent of medically important antimicrobials sold in the United States in 2021 were intended for use in food-producing animals.
  6. World Health Organization. (2001). WHO Global Strategy for Containment of Antimicrobial Resistance. WHO. The strategy identified sub-therapeutic antibiotic use in livestock as a significant driver of antimicrobial resistance affecting human health. For the mechanism of resistance selection and transfer, see also van den Bogaard, A. E., & Stobberingh, E. E. (2000). "Epidemiology of resistance to antibiotics: Links between animals and humans." International Journal of Antimicrobial Agents, 14(4), 327–335.
  7. US Environmental Protection Agency. (2003). National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System Permit Regulation and Effluent Limitation Guidelines and Standards for Concentrated Animal Feeding Operations: Final Rule. 68 Fed. Reg. 7176. The EPA's regulatory preamble documented water quality impairment attributable to animal feeding operations.
  8. Palmquist, R. B., Roka, F. M., & Vukina, T. (1997). "Hog operations, environmental effects, and residential property values." Land Economics, 73(1), 114–124. See also Herriges, J. A., Secchi, S., & Babcock, B. A. (2005). "Living with hogs in Iowa: The impact of livestock facilities on rural residential property values." Land Economics, 81(4), 530–545. Both studies document measurable declines in residential property values near large-scale confined animal operations.
  9. Starmer, E., Witteman, A., & Wise, T. A. (2006). Feeding the Factory Farm: Implicit Subsidies to the Broiler Chicken Industry. Global Development and Environment Institute, Tufts University. The analysis calculated the savings to the industrial livestock sector from purchasing feed grain at prices below the full cost of production, attributable to federal commodity support payments.
  10. US Government Accountability Office. (2005). Workplace Safety and Health: Safety in the Meat and Poultry Industry, While Improving, Could Be Further Strengthened. GAO-05-96. The report documented injury and illness rates in meat and poultry processing substantially above national manufacturing averages.
  11. Waltenburg, M. A., Victoroff, T., Rose, C. E., et al. (2020). "Update: COVID-19 among workers in meat and poultry processing facilities — United States, April–May 2020." Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report, 69(27), 887–892.
  12. Wing, S., & Wolf, S. (2000). "Intensive livestock operations, health, and quality of life among eastern North Carolina residents." Environmental Health Perspectives, 108(3), 233–238. See also Donham, K. J., Wing, S., Osterberg, D., Flora, J. L., Hodne, C., Thu, K. M., & Thorne, P. S. (2007). "Community health and socioeconomic issues surrounding concentrated animal feeding operations." Environmental Health Perspectives, 115(2), 317–320.
  13. Wilson, S. M., Howell, F., Wing, S., & Sobsey, M. (2002). "Environmental injustice and the Mississippi hog industry." Environmental Health Perspectives, 110(Suppl 2), 195–201. The study documented the disproportionate siting of industrial hog operations near low-income and minority communities in Mississippi.
  14. MacDonald, J. M., & McBride, W. D. (2009). The Transformation of U.S. Livestock Agriculture: Scale, Efficiency, and Risks. USDA Economic Research Service, Economic Information Bulletin No. 43. The report traces the consolidation of US livestock production from the 1980s through the 2000s.
  15. USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service. (2009). Animal Manure Management. NRCS Conservation Practice Standard. The 500-million-ton annual estimate is widely cited across USDA and EPA regulatory literature.
  16. National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. (2024). Gulf of Mexico Dead Zone. NOAA National Centers for Coastal Ocean Science. The annual survey measures the extent of the hypoxic zone, which has averaged roughly 5,400 square miles over the last five years.
  17. Gerber, P. J., Steinfeld, H., Henderson, B., Mottet, A., Opio, C., Dijkman, J., Falcucci, A., & Tempio, G. (2013). Tackling Climate Change Through Livestock: A Global Assessment of Emissions and Mitigation Opportunities. Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. The FAO assessment estimated global livestock supply chain emissions at 14.5 percent of anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions.
  18. Pimentel, D., & Pimentel, M. (2008). Food, Energy, and Society (3rd ed.). CRC Press. Pimentel's analysis of energy and grain inputs in feedlot beef production provides the feed conversion ratios cited here.
  19. USDA National Agricultural Statistics Service. (2023). Acreage Report. NASS. Corn, soybeans, and wheat together account for the majority of planted acreage in the United States, with corn alone covering approximately 90 million acres in a typical year.
  20. Orden, D., Paarlberg, R., & Roe, T. (1999). Policy Reform in American Agriculture: Analysis and Prognosis. University of Chicago Press. For the structural history of commodity support programs and their effects on production incentives, see also Sumner, D. A. (2014). "American farms keep growing: Size, productivity, and policy." Journal of Economic Perspectives, 28(1), 147–166.
  21. USDA Economic Research Service. (2023). Farm Structure and Contracting. ERS. The long-term trend in farm numbers and average farm size is tracked in the Census of Agriculture and in ERS structural analyses.
  22. For diet-related chronic disease costs, see Ward, Z. J., Bleich, S. N., Long, M. W., & Gortmaker, S. L. (2021). "Association of body mass index with health care expenditures in the United States by age and sex." PLoS ONE, 16(3), e0247307. The broader economic burden of diet-related disease, including cardiovascular disease, type 2 diabetes, and certain cancers, has been estimated at several hundred billion dollars annually across the US healthcare system.
  23. Pimentel, D. (2006). "Soil erosion: A food and environmental threat." Environment, Development and Sustainability, 8, 119–137. Pimentel estimated average US cropland erosion at roughly ten times the natural soil-formation rate, with total annual soil loss of approximately 1.8 billion tons.
  24. USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service. (2015). Summary Report: 2012 National Resources Inventory. NRCS. The NRI uses the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation to estimate erosion rates across US cropland.
  25. Tilman, D., Cassman, K. G., Matson, P. A., Naylor, R., & Polasky, S. (2002). "Agricultural sustainability and intensive production practices." Nature, 418(6898), 671–677. The paper documented that agricultural intensification was the largest driver of terrestrial biodiversity loss globally.
  26. Burow, K. R., Nolan, B. T., Rupert, M. G., & Dubrovsky, N. M. (2010). "Nitrate in groundwater of the United States, 1991–2003." Environmental Science & Technology, 44(13), 4988–4997. The USGS assessment documented the geographic extent and agricultural correlation of nitrate contamination in US groundwater.
  27. Rillig, M. C. (2004). "Arbuscular mycorrhizae and terrestrial ecosystem processes." Ecology Letters, 7(8), 740–754. For the specific effects of fungicide application on mycorrhizal networks, see also Zaller, J. G., Heigl, F., Ruess, L., & Grabmaier, A. (2014). "Glyphosate herbicide affects belowground interactions between earthworms and symbiotic mycorrhizal fungi in a model ecosystem." Scientific Reports, 4, 5634.
  28. Foley, J. A., Ramankutty, N., Brauman, K. A., Cassidy, E. S., Gerber, J. S., Johnston, M., Mueller, N. D., O'Connell, C., Ray, D. K., West, P. C., Balzer, C., Bennett, E. M., Carpenter, S. R., Hill, J., Monfreda, C., Polasky, S., Rockström, J., Sheehan, J., Siebert, S., Tilman, D., & Zaks, D. P. M. (2011). "Solutions for a cultivated planet." Nature, 478(7369), 337–342.
  29. Pimentel, D., & Pimentel, M. (2008). Food, Energy, and Society (3rd ed.). CRC Press. The energy-input analysis for corn production documents that nitrogen fertilizer production and application accounts for roughly forty percent of total energy inputs.
  30. Scanlon, B. R., Faunt, C. C., Longuevergne, L., Reedy, R. C., Alley, W. M., McGuire, V. L., & McMahon, P. B. (2012). "Groundwater depletion and sustainability of irrigation in the US High Plains and Central Valley." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(24), 9320–9325.
  31. Boyer, J. S., Byrne, P., Cassman, K. G., Cooper, M., Delmer, D., et al. (2013). "The U.S. drought of 2012 in perspective: a call to action." Global Food Security, 2(3), 139–143.
  32. Schlenker, W., & Roberts, M. J. (2009). "Nonlinear temperature effects indicate severe damages to U.S. crop yields under climate change." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 106(37), 15594–15598. The study projected that under continued warming, the frequency of growing-season temperatures exceeding crop damage thresholds in the US Corn Belt would increase substantially.
  33. USDA National Agricultural Statistics Service. (2024). 2022 Census of Agriculture: Cover Crop Data. The census documented an increase in US cover crop acreage from approximately 10.3 million acres in 2012 to approximately 18.3 million acres in 2022.
  34. Brown, Gabe. (2018). Dirt to Soil: One Family's Journey into Regenerative Agriculture. White River Junction, VT: Chelsea Green Publishing. Brown's book is the primary published accounting for the specific soil organic matter increases, elimination of synthetic fertilizer and fungicide purchases, reduction of herbicide use, and water infiltration improvements on the 5,000-acre operation near Bismarck, North Dakota. The NRCS and SARE have independently profiled the ranch's soil conservation outcomes in technical site documentation, which corroborates the order of magnitude of the organic matter and infiltration changes Brown reports.
  35. LaCanne, C. E., & Lundgren, J. G. (2018). "Regenerative agriculture: merging farming and natural resource conservation profitably." PeerJ, 6, e4428. The peer-reviewed study compared matched pairs of regenerative and conventional corn operations in the Northern Plains and found that the regenerative operations were more profitable than their conventional neighbors despite lower per-acre yields, because reduced input costs more than compensated for yield differences. This is the same structural mechanism Brown's operation exemplifies — profitability driven by cost reduction rather than yield maximization — and it provides independent, peer-reviewed verification that the pattern is not unique to Brown's case.

CHAPTER SIX  ·  Pasture, Honestly

  1. Salatin, J. (1998). You Can Farm: The Entrepreneur's Guide to Start and Succeed in a Farming Enterprise. Polyface Inc., chapters on soil and pasture management. Salatin's published accounting of the soil organic matter trajectory on Polyface from the early 1960s baseline to the late-1990s figure. The continental cropland average is drawn from USDA NRCS summaries, which place typical cultivated mineral soil organic matter in the one-to-three-percent range depending on region and management history. For the NRCS synthesis see Natural Resources Conservation Service (2012). Soil Quality Indicator Sheets: Soil Organic Matter. USDA-NRCS.
  2. Natural Resources Conservation Service. (2018). Summary Report: 2017 National Resources Inventory. USDA-NRCS, Washington, D.C. The NRI is the authoritative US federal source for cultivated-cropland erosion rates under the RUSLE2 methodology; current average sheet-and-rill plus wind erosion rates sit in the four-to-five-ton-per-acre-per-year range on conventionally managed cropland. See also Thaler, E. A., Larsen, I. J., & Yu, Q. (2021). "The extent of soil loss across the US Corn Belt." PNAS, 118(8), e1922375118, for the continental-scale reconstruction of cumulative topsoil loss across the major US cropping regions since the onset of European-style agriculture, which supports the ninety-centimeter-plus figure in the body text.
  3. Machmuller, M. B., Kramer, M. G., Cyle, T. K., Hill, N., Hancock, D., & Thompson, A. (2015). "Emerging land use practices rapidly increase soil organic matter." Nature Communications, 6, 6995, for the direct long-term measurement of soil organic carbon accumulation under managed-intensive pasture rotation in the US Southeast. See also Teague, W. R., et al. (2011). "Grazing management impacts on vegetation, soil biota and soil chemical, physical and hydrological properties in tall grass prairie." Agriculture, Ecosystems & Environment, 141(3–4), 310–322, for the comparison of adaptive multi-paddock, heavy continuous, and light continuous grazing on tall-grass prairie.
  4. The National Chicken Council's Animal Welfare Guidelines and Audit Checklist for Broilers (2017 revision, subsequent updates) is the industry's own reference standard for stocking density and housing conditions in US commercial broiler production. US federal statute does not impose a specific on-farm stocking density for broiler chickens; the Poultry Products Inspection Act regulates processing rather than husbandry. For the regulatory gap see Matheny, G., & Leahy, C. (2007). "Farm-animal welfare, legislation, and trade." Law and Contemporary Problems, 70(1), 325–358.
  5. Leonard, C. (2014). The Meat Racket: The Secret Takeover of America's Food Business. Simon & Schuster, for the contemporary account of the vertical-integration structure of US broiler production and the contract-grower financial model. See also MacDonald, J. M. (2008). "The economic organization of U.S. broiler production." USDA Economic Research Service Economic Information Bulletin No. 38, for the same material from the USDA side.
  6. Carpenter, S. R., Mooney, H. A., Agard, J., et al. (2009). "Science for managing ecosystem services: Beyond the Millennium Ecosystem Assessment." PNAS, 106(5), 1305–1312, for the broader framework. For the specific rural-community-indicator work tied to livestock consolidation, see Lobao, L., & Stofferahn, C. W. (2008). "The community effects of industrialized farming: social science research and challenges to corporate farming laws." Agriculture and Human Values, 25, 219–240, which synthesizes fifty years of sociological studies on the relationship between agricultural structure and rural community outcomes.
  7. Teague, W. R., Apfelbaum, S., Lal, R., Kreuter, U. P., Rowntree, J., Davies, C. A., Conser, R., Rasmussen, M., Hatfield, J., Wang, T., Wang, F., & Byck, P. (2016). "The role of ruminants in reducing agriculture's carbon footprint in North America." Journal of Soil and Water Conservation, 71(2), 156–164.
  8. McLauchlan, K. K. (2006). "Effects of soil disturbance on ecosystem properties: a global meta-analysis." Ecological Applications, 16(6), 2245–2252, for the cultivation-versus-perennial-cover comparison on soil biota. For avian biodiversity differences between row-crop and pasture landscapes, see Rodenhouse, N. L., Best, L. B., O'Connor, R. J., & Bollinger, E. K. (1995). "Effects of agricultural practices and farmland structures." In Ecology and Management of Neotropical Migratory Birds (T. E. Martin & D. M. Finch, eds.), Oxford University Press, pp. 269–293.
  9. Bharati, L., Lee, K.-H., Isenhart, T. M., & Schultz, R. C. (2002). "Soil-water infiltration under crops, pasture, and established riparian buffer in Midwestern USA." Agroforestry Systems, 56(3), 249–257, for the direct infiltration-rate comparison among cropping, pasture, and riparian-buffer treatments in a Midwest setting.
  10. Pollan, M. (2006). The Omnivore's Dilemma: A Natural History of Four Meals. Penguin Press, Part III (the Polyface section), for the description of the farm's scale and the approximate annual broiler count.
  11. Salatin, J. (1999). Pastured Poultry Profit$: Net $25,000 in 6 Months on 20 Acres. Polyface Inc., for the feed-conversion, finished-weight, and ration specifications used in the calculation in the body text.
  12. Salatin (1999), ration specifications; the corn and soy proportions have been independently confirmed in subsequent profiles of the farm, including Ostrander, M. (2012). "How to feed the world: A conversation with Joel Salatin." Yes! Magazine, Spring 2012 issue. The exact proportions have varied slightly across editions of Polyface's documentation and across seasons of operation; the working figures used here are 49.7% corn and 30.87% soy, which yield the 71,568-pound and 44,452-pound annual totals.
  13. Salatin, J. (2007). Everything I Want to Do Is Illegal: War Stories from the Local Food Front. Chelsea Green; and Salatin, J. (2011). Folks, This Ain't Normal: A Farmer's Advice for Happier Hens, Healthier People, and a Better World. Center Street. For discussion of feed-sourcing philosophy and the preference for local non-GMO sources over anonymous commodity channels.
  14. McWilliams, J. E. (2012, April 12). "The myth of sustainable meat." The New York Times, opinion section.
  15. Salatin, J. (2012). "The sheer ignorance of James McWilliams and the New York Times." Polyface Farm Facebook post and blog, April 2012, with expanded versions appearing in subsequent essays collected in Salatin, J. (2016). The Marvelous Pigness of Pigs: Respecting and Caring for God's Creation. FaithWords, and in interviews in the Stockman Grass Farmer and Acres U.S.A. in the months following the Times op-ed.
  16. Pollan, M. (2006), chapter 2, for the history of the 1973 Farm Bill's transition from supply-management to direct-payment commodity support and its consequences for corn and soy pricing. See also Winders, B. (2009). The Politics of Food Supply: U.S. Agricultural Policy in the World Economy. Yale University Press, for the structural account of how commodity-support architecture has shaped US grain markets since the 1970s.
  17. Garnett, T., Godde, C., Muller, A., Röös, E., Smith, P., de Boer, I. J. M., zu Ermgassen, E., Herrero, M., van Middelaar, C., Schader, C., & van Zanten, H. (2017). Grazed and Confused? Ruminating on Cattle, Grazing Systems, Methane, Nitrous Oxide, the Soil Carbon Sequestration Question — And What It All Means for Greenhouse Gas Emissions. Food Climate Research Network, University of Oxford.
  18. Teague, W. R., & Kreuter, U. P. (2020). "Managing grazing to restore soil health, ecosystem function, and ecosystem services." Frontiers in Sustainable Food Systems, 4, 534187, for the Teague-side response to the Grazed and Confused? aggregation approach and the case for separate analysis of adaptive multi-paddock grazing as a management class.
  19. Crews, T. E., Carton, W., & Olsson, L. (2018). "Is the future of agriculture perennial? Imperatives and opportunities to reinvent agriculture by shifting from annual monocultures to perennial polycultures." Global Sustainability, 1, e11; and DeHaan, L. R., & Van Tassel, D. L. (2014). "Useful insights from evolutionary biology for developing perennial grain crops." American Journal of Botany, 101(10), 1801–1819, for the Land Institute's and allied perennial-grain breeding programs and their state of play. The Kernza intermediate wheatgrass program is the most commercially advanced current example.

CHAPTER SEVEN  ·  The Wrong Proxy in the Right Place

  1. Martin, P. S. (2005). Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America. University of California Press, chapter 5 ("Grand Canyon Suite"). The Rampart Cave deposits were first systematically excavated in the 1930s and have yielded one of the most complete records of Shasta ground sloth (Nothrotheriops shastensis) ecology in the American Southwest. The stratified dung deposits span approximately 40,000 to 11,000 radiocarbon years before present.
  2. Hansen, R. M. (1978). "Shasta ground sloth food habits, Rampart Cave, Arizona." Paleobiology, 4(3), 302–319. Hansen's composition analysis of ground sloth dung from Rampart Cave is the primary dietary reconstruction for the species; the roughly fifty-percent globe mallow (Sphaeralcea laxa) figure, eighteen-percent Mormon tea (Ephedra) pollen, and the near-total absence of grasses are drawn from this study.
  3. Martin (2005), chapter 10 ("Restoration"), pp. 185–186. Martin's argument that the thorny, armed vegetation of the arid Southwest evolved under heavy browsing pressure from now-extinct large herbivores, and that the anti-herbivore defenses of these plants are calibrated to animals that have been absent for approximately 13,000 years.
  4. Martin (2005), p. 185. The full passage reads: "From fossils we know that the grasses, forbs, shrubs, and trees of the Americas coevolved with a much greater variety of large herbivores than exists today."
  5. Savory, A., & Butterfield, J. (1999). Holistic Management: A New Framework for Decision Making. Island Press. The foundational text of holistic management theory, laying out the relationship between large-herbivore impact, grassland health, and desertification reversal.
  6. Martin (2005), chapter 5. The Stanton's Cave faunal assemblage, discussed alongside the Rampart Cave deposits, includes predominantly Harrington's mountain goat (Oreamnos harringtoni) bones, with smaller numbers of bighorn sheep, two small species of Equus, and some bison. Martin notes that Harrington's goat bones and horn sheaths accounted for the majority of the approximately 120 ungulate bones identified from the cave.
  7. Donlan, J., Greene, H. W., Berger, J., Bock, C. E., Bock, J. H., Burney, D. A., Estes, J. A., Foreman, D., Martin, P. S., Roemer, G. W., Smith, F. A., & Soulé, M. E. (2005). "Re-wilding North America." Nature, 436(7053), 913–914. The initial public statement of the Pleistocene rewilding proposal, arguing that ecological history justifies the managed introduction of large vertebrates — including Old World proxies for extinct New World species — to North American landscapes.
  8. Donlan, C. J., Berger, J., Bock, C. E., Bock, J. H., Burney, D. A., Estes, J. A., Foreman, D., Martin, P. S., Roemer, G. W., Smith, F. A., Soulé, M. E., & Greene, H. W. (2006). "Pleistocene rewilding: an optimistic agenda for twenty-first century conservation." The American Naturalist, 168(5), 660–681. The extended treatment of the rewilding proposal, with detailed discussion of proxy-species selection, ecological risk assessment, and phased implementation strategies for multiple North American biomes.
  9. Janzen, D. H., & Martin, P. S. (1982). "Neotropical anachronisms: the fruits the gomphotheres ate." Science, 215(4528), 19–27. The foundational paper establishing the concept of megafaunal fruit syndrome and identifying specific New World fruit species — including osage orange, honey locust, and pawpaw — as ecological anachronisms whose dispersal biology is calibrated to large-bodied herbivores that went extinct in the late Pleistocene.
  10. Martin (2005), chapters 9 and 10. Martin discusses the late-Quaternary disappearance of tapirs (Tapirus) from Florida, California, Kansas, and Arizona, including from elevations above 6,000 feet on the Colorado Plateau, and argues that modeling their range contraction as a retreat to tropical refugia under predation pressure is more parsimonious than a climatic explanation, given that packrat midden records from the relevant regions do not show the kind of tropical-to-temperate climate shift that would be required to force a thermally driven range collapse.
  11. Zimov, S. A. (2005). "Pleistocene Park: return of the mammoth's ecosystem." Science, 308(5723), 796–798. Sergey Zimov's initial description of the Pleistocene Park experiment in northeastern Siberia, which reintroduced horses, bison, muskoxen, and other large herbivores to test the hypothesis that megafaunal grazing can restore the mammoth-steppe grassland ecosystem and reduce permafrost thaw.
  12. Rubenstein, D. R., Rubenstein, D. I., Sherman, P. W., & Gavin, T. A. (2006). "Pleistocene Park: does re-wilding North America represent sound conservation for the 21st century?" Biological Conservation, 132(2), 232–238. The most careful of the early critiques of the Donlan et al. rewilding proposal, raising concerns about ecological risk, invasive-species dynamics, and the assumption that proxy species will fill the ecological roles of their extinct counterparts.

CHAPTER EIGHT  ·  Rewilding the Plate

  1. Mayer, J. J., & Brisbin, I. L., Jr. (2009). Wild Pigs: Biology, Damage, Control Techniques and Management. Savannah River National Laboratory, SRNL-RP-2009-00869. The Texas population estimate of approximately 2.6 million feral hogs is drawn from Texas Parks and Wildlife Department surveys; see also Timmons, J. B., Mellish, J. M., Higginbotham, B., Griffin, J. L., Lopez, R. R., & Sumners, J. A. (2012). "Feral hog population growth, density, and harvest in Texas." Texas A&M AgriLife Extension Service, SP-472.
  2. Anderson, A., Slootmaker, C., Harper, E., Holderieath, J., & Shwiff, S. A. (2016). "Economic estimates of feral swine damage and control in 11 US states." Crop Protection, 89, 89–94. The $52 million annual direct agricultural loss figure for Texas is conservative and accounts only for crop damage; total economic losses including infrastructure, ecological, and disease-vector costs are substantially higher.
  3. Texas Parks and Wildlife Department. (2023). "Feral Hogs — Texas Distribution and Biology." TPWD Wildlife Division fact sheet. The annual harvest estimate and the reproductive rate data — two litters per year, five to six piglets per litter, population doubling time of four months under favorable conditions — are drawn from TPWD's published management summaries.
  4. Hedrick, P. W. (2009). "Conservation genetics and North American bison (Bison bison)." Journal of Heredity, 100(4), 411–420. For the current continental population structure and the distinction between commercial and conservation herds. The roughly 500,000 total and 20,000 conservation-herd figures are consistent with current estimates from the IUCN Bison Specialist Group and the Wildlife Conservation Society's bison program.
  5. American Prairie. (2024). "About American Prairie." americanprairie.org. The 450,000-acre current footprint and the long-term acquisition target are drawn from the organization's public documentation. See also Freese, C. H., Aune, K. E., Boyd, D. P., Derr, J. N., Forrest, S. C., Gates, C. C., Gogan, P. J. P., Grassel, S. M., Halbert, N. D., Kunkel, K., & Redford, K. H. (2007). "Second chance for the plains bison." Biological Conservation, 136(2), 175–184, for the ecological rationale underlying large-scale bison restoration in the northern Great Plains.
  6. InterTribal Buffalo Council. (2024). "About ITBC." itbcbuffalonation.org. The council's membership of seventy-six-plus tribal nations and the collective herd numbering in the tens of thousands are drawn from ITBC's published program data. See also Zontek, K. (2007). Buffalo Nation: American Indian Efforts to Restore the Bison. University of Nebraska Press, for the cultural and historical context of tribal bison restoration.
  7. Hilborn, R., & Walters, C. J. (2013). Quantitative Fisheries Stock Assessment: Choice, Dynamics and Uncertainty. Springer. The foundational reference for the stock-assessment methodology that underlies Alaska's fisheries management regime. Alaska's constitutional mandate for sustained-yield fisheries management is codified in Article VIII, Section 4 of the Alaska State Constitution.
  8. Hilborn, R., Amoroso, R. O., Anderson, C. M., Baum, J. K., Branch, T. A., Costello, C., de Moor, C. L., Faraj, A., Hively, D., Jensen, O. P., Kurota, H., Little, L. R., Mace, P., McClanahan, T., Melnychuk, M. C., Minto, C., Osio, G. C., Parma, A. M., Pons, M., ... Ye, Y. (2020). "Effective fisheries management instrumental in improving fish stock status." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 117(4), 2218–2224.
  9. Beck, M. W., Brumbaugh, R. D., Airoldi, L., Carranza, A., Coen, L. D., Crawford, C., Defeo, O., Edgar, G. J., Hancock, B., Kay, M. C., Lenihan, H. S., Luckenbach, M. W., Toropova, C. L., Zhang, G., & Guo, X. (2011). "Oyster reefs at risk and recommendations for conservation, restoration, and management." BioScience, 61(2), 107–116.
  10. US Fish and Wildlife Service. (2022). 2022 National Survey of Fishing, Hunting, and Wildlife-Associated Recreation. US Department of the Interior. The six-million annual white-tailed deer harvest figure and the estimated venison poundage are derived from the Survey's hunting statistics and standard dressed-weight calculations for white-tailed deer.
  11. Tree, I. (2018). Wilding: The Return of Nature to a British Farm. Picador. Tree's account of the Knepp rewilding project is the primary reference for the ecological and economic outcomes described in this chapter.
  12. Greenaway, F., & Hill, D. (2004–present). Ecological monitoring reports for the Knepp Wildland Project, conducted by Imperial College London and associated research institutions. Species recolonization data — turtle doves, nightingales, peregrine falcons, purple emperor butterflies, all five British owl species — are documented in successive monitoring reports and summarized in Tree (2018), chapters 14–18.
  13. USDA Economic Research Service. (2024). Livestock and Meat Domestic Data. The approximately 27-billion-pound annual US beef consumption figure is drawn from USDA-ERS per-capita disappearance data extrapolated to the current US population.
  14. National Marine Fisheries Service. (2023). Fisheries of the United States, 2022. NOAA. Alaska salmon harvest tonnage and US total seafood consumption figures are drawn from NMFS annual fisheries statistics.

CHAPTER NINE  ·  Proxies and Critics

  1. Donlan, J., Greene, H. W., Berger, J., Bock, C. E., Bock, J. H., Burney, D. A., Estes, J. A., Foreman, D., Martin, P. S., Roemer, G. W., Smith, F. A., & Soulé, M. E. (2005). "Re-wilding North America." Nature, 436(7053), 913–914. The initial public statement of the Pleistocene rewilding proposal.
  2. Caro, T. (2007). "The Pleistocene re-wilding gambit." Trends in Ecology & Evolution, 22(6), 281–283. Caro's critique focuses on the proxy-equivalence assumption and the risks of introducing large-bodied species into ecosystems that have reorganized in their absence.
  3. Donlan, C. J., Berger, J., Bock, C. E., Bock, J. H., Burney, D. A., Estes, J. A., Foreman, D., Martin, P. S., Roemer, G. W., Smith, F. A., Soulé, M. E., & Greene, H. W. (2006). "Pleistocene rewilding: an optimistic agenda for twenty-first century conservation." The American Naturalist, 168(5), 660–681. The fuller treatment of the rewilding proposal, with discussion of phased implementation and ecological risk assessment.
  4. Rubenstein, D. R., Rubenstein, D. I., Sherman, P. W., & Gavin, T. A. (2006). "Pleistocene Park: does re-wilding North America represent sound conservation for the 21st century?" Biological Conservation, 132(2), 232–238. The most careful of the early critiques, focusing on political, cultural, and practical feasibility rather than on ecological principles alone.
  5. Rubenstein, D. R., & Rubenstein, D. I. (2016). "From Pleistocene to trophic rewilding: a wolf in sheep's clothing?" Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 113(1), E1. A response to Svenning et al.'s trophic rewilding synthesis, arguing that the shift from Pleistocene-proxy to trophic-process framing does not resolve the underlying feasibility and risk concerns.
  6. Svenning, J.-C., Pedersen, P. B. M., Donlan, C. J., Ejrnæs, R., Faurby, S., Galetti, M., Hansen, D. M., Sandel, B., Sandom, C. J., Terborgh, J. W., & Vera, F. W. M. (2016). "Science for a wilder Anthropocene: synthesis and future directions for trophic rewilding research." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 113(4), 898–906. The synthesis that reframed the rewilding conversation around ecological process rather than Pleistocene species proxies.
  7. Ripple, W. J., & Beschta, R. L. (2012). "Trophic cascades in Yellowstone: the first 15 years after wolf reintroduction." Biological Conservation, 145(1), 205–213. The most comprehensive early synthesis of the ecological effects of wolf reintroduction in Yellowstone, documenting cascading effects from predator reintroduction through elk behavior and distribution to riparian vegetation recovery.
  8. Painter, L. E., Beschta, R. L., Larsen, E. J., & Ripple, W. J. (2015). "Recovering aspen follow changing elk dynamics in Yellowstone: evidence of a trophic cascade?" Ecology, 96(1), 252–263. Documents the decline in elk browsing pressure on young aspen in northern Yellowstone — from ninety percent browsed in 1998 to thirty-seven to sixty-three percent by 2012 — with corresponding increases in aspen recruitment in areas of high wolf activity.
  9. Creel, S., & Christianson, D. (2009). "Wolf presence and increased willow consumption by Yellowstone elk: implications for trophic cascades." Ecology, 90(9), 2454–2466. A study that complicates the simple "landscape of fear" narrative by showing that individual elk willow consumption increased in the presence of wolves, suggesting that the primary mechanism of vegetation recovery was elk population reduction rather than behavioral avoidance alone.

CHAPTER TEN  ·  Water and Indigenous Systems

  1. Kimmerer, R. W. (2013). Braiding Sweetgrass: Indigenous Wisdom, Scientific Knowledge, and the Teachings of Plants. Milkweed Editions. Kimmerer is a member of the Citizen Potawatomi Nation and a plant ecologist whose work bridges Traditional Ecological Knowledge and Western botanical science.
  2. Anderson, M. K. (2005). Tending the Wild: Native American Knowledge and the Management of California's Natural Resources. University of California Press. Anderson's study draws on decades of ethnobotanical fieldwork and collaboration with Indigenous elders across California.
  3. Anderson (2005), chapters 6–10. Anderson documents fire management practices across grassland, oak woodland, chaparral, and mixed conifer ecosystems, with specific fire-return intervals and management objectives for each vegetation type, reconstructed from both ethnographic and fire-scar evidence. See also Lightfoot, K. G., & Parrish, O. (2009). California Indians and Their Environment: An Introduction. University of California Press, for additional synthesis of pre-contact landscape management.
  4. Johnston, L. J. (2022). "Kincentric ecology and the restoration of a 4000-year-old food forest." Paper presented at the Ecological Society of America Annual Meeting, Montréal, Canada. Johnston's research on Indigenous food forests across North America draws on both archaeological evidence and oral tradition to reconstruct the scale and sophistication of pre-contact landscape management. See also Kimmerer, R. W. (2002). "Weaving traditional ecological knowledge into biological education: A call to action." BioScience, 52(5), 432–438, for a peer-reviewed articulation of the relationship between Indigenous ecological knowledge and Western scientific frameworks.
  5. Krumhansl, K. A., et al. (2016). "Global patterns of kelp forest change over the past half-century." PNAS, 113(48), 13785–13790. The study compiled 862 kelp time series from 34 ecoregions, covering a temporal span of several decades, and found net declines in 38% of the ecoregions studied.
  6. Estes, J. A., & Palmisano, J. F. (1974). "Sea otters: their role in structuring nearshore communities." Science, 185(4156), 1058–1060. The foundational paper documenting the trophic cascade between sea otters, sea urchins, and kelp forests. See also Estes, J. A., et al. (2011). "Trophic downgrading of planet Earth." Science, 333(6040), 301–306, for the broader synthesis of top-predator removal effects across marine and terrestrial ecosystems.
  7. Beck, M. W., et al. (2011). "Oyster reefs at risk and recommendations for conservation, restoration, and management." BioScience, 61(2), 107–116. The synthesis documented that 85% of oyster reef habitat had been lost globally, making oyster reefs among the most threatened marine habitats on Earth.
  8. Newell, R. I. E. (2004). "Ecosystem influences of natural and cultivated populations of suspension-feeding bivalve molluscs: a review." Journal of Shellfish Research, 23(1), 51–61. Newell's review provides the filtration-rate estimates for individual oysters and models the water-quality consequences of historical versus current oyster populations in the Chesapeake Bay.
  9. Zu Ermgassen, P. S. E., et al. (2012). "Historical ecology with real numbers: past and present extent and biomass of an imperiled estuarine habitat." Proceedings of the Royal Society B, 279(1742), 3393–3400. For the Chesapeake Bay restoration monitoring data specifically, see the NOAA Chesapeake Bay Office restoration program reports and Schulte, D. M., Burke, R. P., & Lipcius, R. N. (2009). "Unprecedented restoration of a native oyster metapopulation." Science, 325(5944), 1124–1128.
  10. Naylor, R. L., et al. (2000). "Effect of aquaculture on world fish supplies." Nature, 405(6790), 1017–1024. Naylor and colleagues documented that farmed salmon production requires approximately 2.5 to 5 kg of wild-caught fish (processed into fish meal and oil) per kilogram of salmon produced, creating a net reduction in the total fish available for human consumption. For antibiotic and antiparasitic use in salmon aquaculture, see Burridge, L., Weis, J. S., Cabello, F., Pizarro, J., & Bostick, K. (2010). "Chemical use in salmon aquaculture: a review of current practices and possible environmental effects." Aquaculture, 306(1–4), 7–23.
  11. Jackson, J. B. C., et al. (2001). "Historical overfishing and the recent collapse of coastal ecosystems." Science, 293(5530), 629–638. Jackson and colleagues assembled historical, archaeological, and paleoecological data from multiple coastal ecosystems to reconstruct pre-exploitation baselines and document the sequential removal of megafauna, large predators, and small herbivores that produced the modern depleted state.
  12. Newell, R. I. E. (1988). "Ecological changes in Chesapeake Bay: Are they the result of overharvesting the American oyster, Crassostrea virginica?" In Understanding the Estuary: Advances in Chesapeake Bay Research, Chesapeake Research Consortium Publication 129, pp. 536–546. Newell's estimate that pre-exploitation oyster populations could filter the bay's volume in approximately one week, compared with approximately one year for the current remnant population, has been widely cited and was subsequently refined in Newell (2004).
  13. Lepofsky, D., Smith, N. F., Cardinal, N., Harper, J., Morris, M., Gitla (Elroy White), Bouchard, R., Kennedy, D. I. D., Salomon, A. K., Puckett, M., & Rowell, K. (2015). "Ancient shellfish mariculture on the Northwest Coast of North America." American Antiquity, 80(2), 236–259. See also Groesbeck, A. S., Rowell, K., Shellfish, D., Giber, A. K., & Salomon, A. K. (2014). "Ancient clam gardens increased shellfish production: adaptive strategies from the past can inform food security today." PLoS ONE, 9(3), e91235, for the quantitative comparison of clam productivity between garden and non-garden sites.

CHAPTER ELEVEN  ·  Bioregional and Urban

  1. Martin, P. S. (2005). Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America. University of California Press, chapter 10. Martin's discussion of the co-evolutionary relationship between arid-Southwest browse vegetation and the Pleistocene browsing fauna — camelids, ground sloths, and equids — whose foraging behavior shaped the plant community's defensive architecture.
  2. Archer, S. (1995). "Tree-grass dynamics in a Prosopis-thornscrub savanna parkland: reconstructing the past and predicting the future." Ecoscience, 2(1), 83–99. Archer's foundational analysis of mesquite encroachment dynamics in the Sonoran and Chihuahuan regions, documenting the shift from open grassland to dense shrubland in the absence of large-bodied browsers. See also Van Auken, O. W. (2009). "Causes and consequences of woody plant encroachment into western North American grasslands." Journal of Environmental Management, 90(10), 2931–2942, for the broader pattern across western rangelands.
  3. Bureau of Land Management. "Wild Horse and Burro Program: Program Data." The BLM estimates the current free-roaming horse and burro population across ten western states at roughly 83,000 animals on public land, with additional populations on tribal and private land. See also the 2013 National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine review, Using Science to Improve the BLM Wild Horse and Burro Program: A Way Forward, for the management challenges and the gap between current population levels and the agency's appropriate-management-level estimates.
  4. Farah, Z., Mollet, M., Younan, M., & Dahir, R. (2007). "Camel dairy in Somalia: Limiting factors and development potential." Livestock Science, 110(1–2), 187–191. For the nutritional profile of camel milk — higher fat and protein content, lower lactose, higher vitamin C than bovine milk — see also Al Haj, O. A., & Al Kanhal, H. A. (2010). "Compositional, technological and nutritional aspects of dromedary camel milk." International Dairy Journal, 20(12), 811–821.
  5. Hedrick, P. W. (2009). "Conservation genetics and North American bison (Bison bison)." Journal of Heredity, 100(4), 411–420. For current population structure and the distinction between commercial and conservation herds.
  6. Martin (2005), chapters 9 and 10. Martin discusses the late-Quaternary presence of tapirs, capybaras, and ground sloths across the Gulf Coast and subtropical Southeast, with range extensions into Florida and the coastal plain. See also Webb, S. D. (2006). "The Great American Biotic Interchange: patterns and processes." Annals of the Missouri Botanical Garden, 93(2), 245–257, for the Neotropical character of the Gulf Coast's Pleistocene fauna.
  7. Beck, M. W., et al. (2011). "Oyster reefs at risk and recommendations for conservation, restoration, and management." BioScience, 61(2), 107–116. The roughly eighty-five-percent loss of global oyster reef habitat documented in this synthesis includes severe losses in the Gulf of Mexico.
  8. For the environmental and dependency profile of farmed shrimp production in Southeast Asia, see Naylor, R. L., Goldburg, R. J., Primavera, J. H., Kautsky, N., Beveridge, M. C. M., Clay, J., Folke, C., Lubchenco, J., Mooney, H., & Troell, M. (2000). "Effect of aquaculture on world fish supplies." Nature, 405(6790), 1017–1024. The contrast between wild-caught Gulf shrimp and imported farmed shrimp is a direct application of the survivability axis to a consumer-facing comparison.
  9. Foster, D. R. (1992). "Land-use history (1730–1990) and vegetation dynamics in central New England, USA." Journal of Ecology, 80(4), 753–771. Foster's reconstruction of the clearing, farming, abandonment, and reforestation cycle in New England is the standard reference for the region's ecological history within the period of European settlement.
  10. Fisher, D. C. (2009). "Paleobiology and extinction of proboscideans in the Great Lakes region of North America." In American Megafaunal Extinctions at the End of the Pleistocene (G. Haynes, ed.), Springer, pp. 55–75. For the mastodon's range, diet, and ecological role in the eastern deciduous forests.
  11. Janzen, D. H., & Martin, P. S. (1982). "Neotropical anachronisms: the fruits the gomphotheres ate." Science, 215(4528), 19–27. The ecological-anachronism concept, as applied to eastern North American fruit species whose dispersal biology is calibrated to large-bodied herbivores absent since the late Pleistocene.
  12. Helfield, J. M., & Naiman, R. J. (2001). "Effects of salmon-derived nitrogen on riparian forest growth and implications for stream productivity." Ecology, 82(9), 2403–2409. The study documented measurable marine-derived nitrogen in the foliage and growth rings of riparian trees near salmon spawning streams, establishing the quantitative link between salmon migration and terrestrial nutrient cycling.
  13. Boyd, R., ed. (1999). Indians, Fire, and the Land in the Pacific Northwest. Oregon State University Press. The collected essays document the extent and sophistication of Indigenous fire management in the Pacific Northwest, including the maintenance of Willamette Valley prairies and Puget Sound oak savannas through deliberate burning.

CHAPTER THIRTEEN  ·  What Must Change

  1. Welch, D. (2012). "What can we do about sustainable consumption? Reframing consumer policy for shifted priorities." Journal of Consumer Culture, 12(3), 379–399. Welch's analysis examines the structural limitations of consumer-choice-based approaches to food system sustainability, arguing that the options available to consumers are shaped by policy, infrastructure, and industry structure that consumer purchasing decisions cannot, by themselves, alter.
  2. Environmental Working Group. (2022). Farm Subsidy Database. EWG. The database compiles USDA subsidy disbursement records from 1995 through 2021; the cumulative figure of approximately $478 billion includes commodity payments, crop insurance premium subsidies, conservation payments, and disaster payments across all farm bill programs.
  3. Environmental Working Group. (2022). Farm Subsidy Database: Concentration Analysis. EWG. The EWG analyses have consistently shown that the top ten percent of subsidy recipients receive approximately seventy-five percent of total payments, and that the payments are concentrated in commodity crop production — corn, soybeans, wheat, cotton, and rice — rather than in diversified, specialty, or ecologically managed production systems.
  4. Starmer, E., Witteman, A., & Wise, T. A. (2006). Feeding the Factory Farm: Implicit Subsidies to the Broiler Chicken Industry. Global Development and Environment Institute, Tufts University. The analysis calculated $35 billion in below-cost feed savings to the industrial livestock sector between 1997 and 2005, attributable to federal commodity support payments that held corn and soybean prices below the cost of production.
  5. Witcover, J., Murphy, S., & Kaffka, S. (2019). Policy Options for Alternative Uses of Agricultural Land. UC Davis Policy Institute for Energy, Environment, and the Economy. The analysis modeled land-use responses to alternative subsidy structures on marginal cropland in the US Corn Belt and found measurable shifts toward non-commodity land uses under ecosystem-service payment scenarios.
  6. Orden, D., Paarlberg, R., & Roe, T. (1999). Policy Reform in American Agriculture: Analysis and Prognosis. University of Chicago Press, for the history of farm bill restructuring from the 1996 Federal Agriculture Improvement and Reform Act through its subsequent modifications. See also Winders, B. (2009). The Politics of Food Supply: U.S. Agricultural Policy in the World Economy. Yale University Press, for the structural account of how commodity-support architecture reconverges after attempted reforms.
  7. Endangered Species Act of 1973, Section 10(j), 16 U.S.C. § 1539(j). The experimental-population provision allows the Fish and Wildlife Service to designate reintroduced populations as "experimental" and manage them under regulations that may be less restrictive than those applying to naturally occurring populations. The gray wolf reintroductions in the Greater Yellowstone Ecosystem and central Idaho (1995–1996) were conducted under 10(j) designations. See also Bangs, E. E., & Fritts, S. H. (1996). "Reintroducing the gray wolf to central Idaho and Yellowstone National Park." Wildlife Society Bulletin, 24(3), 402–413.
  8. Svenning, J.-C., Pedersen, P. B. M., Donlan, C. J., Ejrnæs, R., Faurby, S., Galetti, M., Hansen, D. M., Sandel, B., Sandom, C. J., Terborgh, J. W., & Vera, F. W. M. (2016). "Science for a wilder Anthropocene: Synthesis and future directions for trophic rewilding research." PNAS, 113(4), 898–906. The synthesis argues that the scientific literature on trophic rewilding has advanced substantially and that institutional frameworks for conservation have not kept pace with the evidence base.
  9. USDA Farm Service Agency. (2023). Conservation Reserve Program: Program Fact Sheet. FSA. CRP contracts restrict commercial use of enrolled acreage; limited haying and grazing are permitted under emergency provisions and managed-harvest provisions, but the program's primary orientation is toward maintaining vegetative cover rather than toward active ecological management that includes food production.
  10. For habitat exchange programs as institutional models for integrating ecological outcomes with working-land management, see Wilcove, D. S., & Lee, J. (2004). "Using economic and regulatory incentives to restore endangered species: Lessons learned from three new programs." Conservation Biology, 18(3), 639–645. The sage-grouse habitat exchange programs operating in Wyoming, Montana, Nevada, and other western states provide a state-level model for crediting ecological outcomes on working landscapes.
  11. Hilborn, R., Amoroso, R. O., Anderson, C. M., Baum, J. K., Branch, T. A., Costello, C., de Moor, C. L., Faraj, A., Hively, D., Jensen, O. P., Kurota, H., Little, L. R., Mace, P., McClanahan, T., Melnychuk, M. C., Minto, C., Osio, G. C., Parma, A. M., Pons, M., Segurado, S., Szuwalski, C. S., Wilson, J. R., & Ye, Y. (2020). "Effective fisheries management instrumental in improving fish stock status." PNAS, 117(4), 2218–2224. The analysis demonstrated that fisheries under intensive science-based management have been improving in stock status, while unmanaged fisheries continue to decline.
  12. Hilborn et al. (2020). The analysis documents significant disparities in management quality across the world's fisheries, with well-managed jurisdictions (US, EU, Australia, New Zealand) showing improving stock status and poorly managed jurisdictions showing continued decline.
